# What the simulator actually does

An honest capability matrix for the PacketForge **engine**: what is modelled,
what is a documented approximation, and what is simply not there.

This page is written from `engine/*.js`. It is deliberately not written from
[README.md](../README.md). Where the two disagree, believe this page — [§11](#11-where-the-other-docs-overstate-the-code)
lists the disagreements so they can be fixed in the marketing rather than
discovered in a lab.

Read it before you design a lab around a feature, and before you claim a feature
in a commit message.

---

## How to read this page

| Tag | Meaning |
|-----|---------|
| **Modelled** | The behaviour is produced by a real code path. If you configure it, packets behave accordingly. |
| **Approximate** | Something is modelled, but a constant, a rule or a tie-break is a simplification. The code says which. |
| **Absent** | There is no code path. Configuring it usually succeeds and then does nothing. |
| **Unknown** | The code does not answer the question. Said out loud instead of guessed. |

Every claim carries a `file:line` citation. Citations also name the symbol,
because line numbers drift as the engine grows — if a number is stale, the
symbol will still find it.

**Line numbers here are pinned to a moment, not to a truth.** They were accurate
at `5218e7e`; `engine/cli.js` alone has since grown by several hundred lines across
the file, so numbers into `cli.js`, `simulator.js` and `device.js` are hints, not
addresses. Where a row gives a symbol, `grep -n '<symbol>' engine/<file>.js` is the
citation that still resolves. `docs/BGP-POLICY.md` has been converted to symbols for
exactly this reason; the same conversion is outstanding for the rows below.

Two conventions used throughout:

- **A "run"** is one call to `Simulator.ping()`, `openFlow()` or `pingScion()`.
  State that lives on a `Device` survives between runs; state that lives on the
  `Simulator` does not. `Simulator.reset()` (`engine/simulator.js:141`) rewinds
  a run, not the network.
- **A "step"** is one frame handed to one port. It is a hop count, not a
  duration — see [§8](#8-time-what-rtt-means-and-what-it-does-not).

---

## 1. The engine loop

Everything else on this page depends on how a run is sequenced, so it comes
first.

**Modelled**

- A frame is a plain object (`{dstMac, srcMac, etherType, ip, icmp, arp, l4, flow}`),
  built by `ethFrame()` at `engine/simulator.js:31`.
- Transmissions go on a FIFO array and `_run()` drains it synchronously, one
  `step++` per delivery (`engine/simulator.js:1718`). There is no timer, no
  coroutine and no scheduling: a run is a single-threaded loop that ends when the
  queue is empty.
- Guard rails are loop detectors, not physics: `maxSteps = 8000`
  (`engine/simulator.js:156`) and a 100 000-iteration runaway guard
  (`engine/simulator.js:3095`). Exceeding either emits a `limit` event.
- Before the first packet, every run recomputes the world **once**, in this
  order (`engine/simulator.js:438-452`): spanning tree, PoE, SVI line state,
  LLDP advertisements, router advertisements, VRRP, DHCP, OSPF, BGP. The three
  the older version of this bullet omitted are not decoration — an SVI's line
  protocol depends on whether any port in its VLAN can still carry traffic
  *after* the spanning tree has blocked ports, a VRRP group's virtual MAC has to
  exist before anything advertises it, and the DHCP exchange is
  announce-then-hear. The protocols are therefore converged before packet one,
  and do not reconverge during the run.
- Layer decisions are gated by `hasCap()`, and the only capabilities the
  forwarding path actually consults are `l2-forward`, `l3-forward` and `host`
  (`engine/simulator.js:1856`, `engine/simulator.js:1923`).

**Approximate**

- Device state (MAC tables, ARP cache, ND cache, PoE power state) lives on the
  `Device`, inside the `Topology`, so it persists across runs — which is what a
  real box does. But nothing ages it: no MAC ageing timer, no ARP timeout, no ND
  reachable-time. DHCP leases *do* age (see [§5.1](#51-dhcp--in-the-data-path-since-1051)), but only when a question is asked at an
instant — nothing runs a clock between questions. A table learned in run 1 is still there
  in run 1 000 with the same entries (`engine/device.js:331`).
- The capability list at `engine/device.js:85-98` is partly decorative, and **where each part is
  read differs**. The forwarding path consults only `l2-forward`, `l3-forward` and `host`. `poe-pse`
  and `poe-pd` are read when port hardware is derived (`isPoePse()` / `isPoePd()` in
  `engine/media.js`). `cli`, `dhcp-server` and `http-server` are read as the flow listener table
  (`SERVICE_PORTS` in `engine/flow.js` — 22/23, 67, 80/443). `vlan`, `lacp`, `stp`, `firewall`,
  `wireless` and `dhcp-client` are read nowhere at all: VLANs and STP apply to any `l2-forward`
  device whether or not it claims them; see [§2](#2-ethernet-and-l2) and
  [§5.1](#51-dhcp--in-the-data-path-since-1051).

**Absent**

- Concurrency. Two flows, two pings and an OSPF reconvergence never interleave;
  each run owns the simulator until it finishes.
- Any notion of a device CPU. Control-plane work is instantaneous and free, so
  an OSPF flood cannot cause a CPU spike and a BGP flap cannot drop packets.

---

## 2. Ethernet and L2

### 2.1 MAC learning and flooding

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Source MAC is learned on ingress, into a table **scoped to the frame's VLAN**. | `engine/simulator.js:1734`, `engine/device.js:331` |
| **Modelled** | Known unicast → one egress port. Unknown unicast, broadcast and multicast → flood within the VLAN only. | `engine/simulator.js:1895-2020` |
| **Modelled** | A flood never returns out of the ingress port, and never out of the LAG it arrived on. | `engine/simulator.js:1955-1970` |
| **Modelled** | A learned entry pointing back at the ingress port is dropped with `loop`, rather than forwarded. | `engine/simulator.js:1957` |
| **Approximate** | Learning happens before the ACL and the STP egress checks, so a frame that is then dropped still taught the switch a MAC. Real hardware does the same; worth knowing when reading a table after a denied flow. | `engine/simulator.js:1734` vs `1792` |
| **Approximate** | Multicast is treated as broadcast: no IGMP snooping, no per-group filtering. This is deliberate — IPv6 neighbour discovery would never arrive otherwise. | `engine/simulator.js:1842` |
| **Absent** | MAC ageing, MAC move detection, per-port MAC limits, port security, storm control, error counters, CRC/FCS errors, collisions. | — |

### 2.2 VLANs (802.1Q)

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Per-port config: `mode: access\|trunk`, `accessVlan`, `nativeVlan`, `allowedVlans`. | `engine/vlan.js:6-14` |
| **Modelled** | **Access**: egress untagged on `accessVlan`; ingress accepts untagged, drops tagged. | `engine/vlan.js:303` |
| **Modelled** | **Trunk**: egress untagged for `nativeVlan`, tagged for every other allowed VLAN; ingress maps untagged → native VLAN, tagged → VID if allowed, else drops. | `engine/vlan.js:285`, `engine/vlan.js:303` |
| **Modelled** | Frames are really tagged on the wire: `_emitFrame` sets `wireTagged`/`wireVid` from `classifyEgress`, so the event log distinguishes a tagged trunk frame from an untagged access frame. | `engine/simulator.js:1666-1680` |
| **Modelled** | MAC tables are per VLAN; VLAN 1 keeps the flat legacy table. Flooding is scoped to the VLAN. | `engine/device.js:331-337`, `engine/simulator.js:1895` |
| **Modelled** | VLANs 1–4094; 1002–1005 are rejected as reserved. | `engine/vlan.js:24-26` |
| **Modelled** | **SVI**: `interface vlan <id>` creates the switch's own address on that VLAN (`Vlan<id>`), which terminates ARP and echo traffic from any access port in the VLAN and answers with one MAC regardless of which port asked. It is a *host* function, not a routing one: a switch with two SVIs still forwards nothing between the VLANs (`canTerminate` gates termination; `isL3()` gates forwarding). Never a bridge egress (`bridgedInterfaces()`), never an STP port, never a module bay. | `engine/device.js` (`addSvi`, `hasSvi`, `bridgedInterfaces`), `engine/cli.js` (`_enterInterface`), `engine/simulator.js` (`canTerminate`, `_forwardL2`) |
| **Approximate** | A switch's answer for its own SVI is **bridged**, not unicast: it goes through `_sendFromOwnIp()`'s L2 path, which floods the frame into the VLAN. That is what a management stack on the switch chip does, and it costs one extra copy on a one-host VLAN. | `engine/simulator.js` `_sendFromOwnIp()` |
| **Modelled** | **Completion and abbreviation**: `?` anywhere at the end of a line asks a question, attached to the word (`sh st?`) or spaced (`show ip ?`); both are answered from the same parsed help tree that abbreviation uses, so a completion can only offer a command that exists, and a command becomes completable exactly when it is documented. Abbreviation expands a keyword prefix that identifies one command, refuses an ambiguous one by name with candidates, and leaves a line it does not recognise untouched. A trailing `?` is always a question, so a literal one is escaped (`\?`); a `?` inside the line is text. | `engine/abbrev.js` (`aliasSpellings`, `parseForms`, `parseFormsWithDesc`, `expandSynopsis`), `engine/cli.js` (`_complete`, `_expandAbbrev`, `_bareInstantToken`), `test/abbrev.test.js`, `test/completion.test.js` |
| **Modelled** | **Saved configuration**: `write memory` / `copy running-config startup-config` stores the *rendered* running configuration (minus its `!` label) on the device, and `show startup-config` prints it followed by a line-level diff against the running configuration, rendered as `!` comments so the whole answer stays pasteable. The copy belongs to the device, survives runs and console sessions, and outlives the configuration it captured. Both configuration views take an optional interface and answer with that interface's block (the `interface` line plus its indented lines); the saved view then reports **that block's own** delta, so "is my change to this port saved?" is one question with one answer. Not modelled: there is no `reload`, so a saved copy never boots a device, and there is no boot variable or image. | `engine/cli.js` (`_saveConfig`, `_showStartupConfig`, `_runningConfigLines`, `diffLines`), `test/startup-config.test.js` |
| **Modelled** | **Per-port counters**: `rx`/`tx` packets and bytes plus `drops` with a per-reason tally, counted where the frame actually moved (`tx` on the port it left, `rx` on the port it arrived at, a drop against the port named in the drop event) so they reconcile with the event log port by port. Cumulative from the lab's start, not per run — a console command begins with a fresh run, so clearing there would make every reading zero — and `clear counters [<port>]` is the reset. Bytes are the frame as the model builds it: 100-byte ICMP payload + 20 IP + 14 Ethernet = 134, ARP and LLDP at the 64-byte minimum. Not modelled: preamble/FCS/minimum-frame padding, and error counters (CRC, runts, giants) because no frame is ever malformed. | `engine/simulator.js` (`_portStat`, `portStats`, `frameBytes`), `engine/cli.js` (`show interfaces`, `show interfaces counters`, `clear counters`) |
| **Modelled** | **SVI line protocol**: `Vlan<id>` is up only while at least one *bridged* port can carry that VLAN — cabled, not shut, link up, and a VLAN member (access membership or a trunk that allows it). `computeSviState()` writes `lineUp` / `lineStateReason` once, and `ownsIpOn()`, `routeTo()`, `routesTable()`, `show ip interface brief` and `show interfaces` all read it, so the data path and the table cannot tell different stories. An un-cabled switch and a shut-port switch are told apart by the reason string, because "down" alone is not a diagnosis. | `engine/simulator.js` `computeSviState()`, `engine/device.js` (`ownsIpOn`, `routeTo`, `routesTable`) |
| **Approximate** | An SVI has **no IPv6-specific line state**: an address configured on it is listed by `show ipv6 interface` and the link-local is derived like any other port's. A blocked spanning-tree port still counts as an active port for the line protocol, which matches a real switch. | — |
| **Approximate** | Router-on-a-stick via subinterfaces works (`Gi0/0.10` creates a tagged logical interface), and `portPvid()` falls back to the parent's VLAN when no tag is present. | `engine/simulator.js:72-78`, `engine/cli.js:187-199` |
| **Absent** | Voice VLAN in the data path: `voiceVlan` is accepted and validated in the config shape (`engine/vlan.js:13`, `engine/vlan.js:423`) but nothing in the forwarder reads it. QinQ / 802.1ad, private VLANs, VTP, VLAN pruning negotiation. | — |

### 2.3 Link aggregation (LAG)

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | A LAG is one logical port: frames are hashed onto exactly one member, the event log records which. | `engine/simulator.js:2024-2045`, `engine/lag.js:157` |
| **Modelled** | Hash policies `l2`, `l3`, `l3l4`, deterministic FNV-1a — the same frame always lands on the same member. | `engine/lag.js:128` |
| **Modelled** | Spanning tree sees one edge per bundle, so it does not block members against each other. | `engine/simulator.js:315-334` |
| **Modelled** | A frame that arrived on a member never leaves by the same group (no hairpin). | `engine/simulator.js:1970` |
| **Approximate** | `mode` (`lacp-active`, `lacp-passive`, `static`) is validated and stored, and then has **no effect on behaviour** — only the hash policy is consulted at forwarding time. | `engine/lag.js:217-225` vs `engine/simulator.js:2039` |
| **Approximate** | `minLinks` is normalised and validated but never read at runtime: a bundle keeps forwarding at one member, it never shuts down. | `engine/lag.js:55-59` |
| **Absent** | LACPDUs, negotiation, per-member failure detection beyond `iface.enabled`, load-based member selection, L3/L4 hash entropy from anything but the fields present. | — |

### 2.4 Spanning tree (802.1D)

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Root election (lowest bridge ID), root path cost, root/designated/alternate roles, forwarding/blocking states, blocked-port list. | `engine/stp.js:119`, `engine/simulator.js:306` |
| **Modelled** | Blocked ports drop **both** ingress and egress traffic (`stp-block` events). This is what turns a physical loop into a working topology instead of a storm. | `engine/simulator.js:1758`, `engine/simulator.js:1959-1963` |
| **Modelled** | Path cost derived from the negotiated link rate, and divided by member count for an aggregate. | `engine/simulator.js:81-89`, `engine/simulator.js:326-327` |
| **Approximate** | Only the **converged result** is computed: no BPDUs, no hello/max-age/forward-delay timers, no listening or learning states, no topology-change notifications. The tree is correct, the convergence is instantaneous and free. | `engine/stp.js:1-17` |
| **Approximate** | It runs for **every** `l2-forward` device regardless of whether the spec claims `stp`, and there is no way to disable it — `show spanning-tree` exists, `no spanning-tree` does not. | `engine/simulator.js:309` |
| **Absent** | RSTP/MSTP/PVST/PVRST, PortFast, BPDU guard/filter, root guard, loop guard, per-VLAN trees. | — |

### 2.5 Passive devices: taps and splitters

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | A **passive tap** (`category: tap` with no capabilities, or `passive: true`) passes frames between its network ports unchanged and duplicates them to monitor ports. A frame arriving on a monitor port is refused (`tap-inject-refused`) — a tap is not a hub. | `engine/simulator.js:1798-1821`, `engine/device.js:323` |
| **Modelled** | A **passive splitter** (`category: splitter`) replicates to every other leg, unchanged, in both directions. No learning, no lookup, no VLAN rewrite. | `engine/simulator.js:1824-1834`, `engine/device.js:317` |
| **Modelled** | Neither can learn: the MAC-learning step explicitly skips them, so a tap cannot be credited with a table it could not hold. | `engine/simulator.js:1734` |
| **Approximate** | SFP-based taps (the active/aggregating kind) are treated as passive if the spec says so; there is no aggregation port, no timestamping, no oversubscription. | — |

### 2.6 Bridge groups

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Ports in the same `bridgeGroup` (e.g. `lan`) are treated as one L3 surface: an address configured on any member answers ARP/ICMP arriving on any other member, and the reply leaves by the port that asked. | `engine/device.js:261-298`, `engine/simulator.js:2036`, `engine/simulator.js:2064` |
| **Approximate** | A router's LAN sockets also enforce role separation: a LAN address is not reachable from the WAN port. | `engine/device.js:274` |
| **Absent** | **Bridging between bridge-group members.** A frame not addressed to the device is dropped (`not addressed to this device`) — it is not switched to another port in the group. Two PCs on a router's `lan2` and `lan3` cannot talk to each other unless the router routes between subnets, or the device also has `l2-forward`. This surprises people; it is the single most common misreading of the feature. | `engine/simulator.js:1916-1920` |

## 3. IPv4

### 3.1 ARP

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | ARP request floods the VLAN; only the owner replies, out of the port that asked (or the subinterface that owns the address). | `engine/simulator.js:2003-2029` |
| **Modelled** | Replies populate the cache, and pending packets for that next hop are flushed in order. | `engine/simulator.js:2027` |
| **Modelled** | A request for an address nobody owns is simply unanswered, so the ping times out — the correct symptom, arrived at honestly. | — |
| **Absent** | ARP cache ageing, gratuitous ARP, proxy ARP, ARP inspection (DAI), static ARP entries, duplicate-address detection. The cache is address → MAC only, so `show arp` derives its Interface column from the link that leads to the owner, and prints `-` when there is none. | — |

### 3.2 ICMP

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Echo request → echo reply, with sequence numbers, per-reply success and a computed round trip. | `engine/simulator.js:2031-2090` |
| **Modelled** | TTL starts at 64, decrements per router, and expiring TTL drops the packet with an event. Measured: a frame handed to `_forwardL3()` with `ttl: 1` came out at `0` with one `drop: TTL expired` in the log. No ICMP time-exceeded is generated, so the *sender* still sees only silence. | `engine/simulator.js` `_forwardL3()` |
| **Limited** | **An echo is answered only for an address owned by the interface it arrived on.** `_handleLocalIp()` asks `dev.ownsIpOn(ingress, dst)` and drops anything else with the event reason `echo for unknown local IP`. Measured on a two-port router (`10.0.12.2/24` on `Gi0/0`, `10.9.9.1/24` on `Gi0/1`, both cabled): a host on the far subnet pings `10.9.9.1` → 4/4; a router across the link pings the same address → 0/4, **even though `show ip route` carries the route and `show ip interface brief` says `up up`**; a *host behind* that router is reached 4/4, because routed traffic never enters this path. The same rule explains why a device cannot ping its own non-ingress address — the echo leaves the wire and nothing answers. Bridge-group members are the exception: an address on one `lan` port answers an echo arriving on another. | `engine/simulator.js` `_handleLocalIp()` |
| **Absent** | **Every other ICMP type.** There is no destination-unreachable, no time-exceeded, no redirect, no parameter-problem. A black-holed packet and a TTL-expired packet both look like silence to `ping`; only the event log tells you which. The `traceroute` command does not change this: it walks the routing tables and prints the path, it does not send TTL-limited probes. | `engine/net.js:81-82` |
| **Modelled** | `traceroute <dst> [at <ms>]` (and `tracert`) walks `routeTo()` hop by hop and prints each hop's address, device and cumulative one-way delay. TTL expiry is not involved: it reports the path the routing tables select, not a sequence of ICMP time-exceeded replies. `at <ms>` rebuilds the control planes at that instant first, so a trace before convergence stops at the hop with no route yet and names the process and its ready time. | `engine/simulator.js` `tracePath()`, `engine/cli.js` `_traceroute()` |
| **Modelled — one refresh, three doors** | `ping`, `traceroute` and `openFlow` (`flow open`) all rebuild the same control planes before answering: STP, PoE, LLDP/RAs, **VRRP**, **DHCP**, OSPF, BGP. `openFlow()` was missing VRRP and DHCP, which made a flow to a virtual IP fail with "no response" on a fresh simulator while a ping to the same address succeeded — the election had never run, so nobody owned the VIP. VRRP state is sticky, so the bug only showed when the flow came first. `test/flow-vip.test.js` pins the agreement, and every case in it starts from a fresh `Simulator` for exactly that reason. | `engine/simulator.js` `openFlow()`, `tracePath()`, `ping()` |

### 3.3 Routing and administrative distance

**Modelled** — `Device.routeTo()` (`engine/device.js:541`), in this order:

1. Connected subnets (AD 0).
2. Static routes from `ip route` (AD 1).
3. BGP-learned routes (eBGP 20, iBGP 200) — `engine/device.js:401-418`.
4. OSPF-learned routes (AD 110) — `engine/device.js:420-435`.
5. `ip default-gateway` (AD 254) — only when nothing else matched.

Longest prefix always wins; equal-length prefixes are broken by lower
administrative distance. That ordering is the documented Cisco one, and it is
enforced by a single comparator (`better(prefix, ad)` at `engine/device.js:369`)
rather than by the order the loops run in — so a `/24` static really does lose to
a `/25` connected route regardless of list order.

**Approximate**

- The default gateway is consulted only when **no** route matched, instead of
  competing as a `0.0.0.0/0` route at AD 254. The practical difference shows up
  only if you configure both a default gateway and a `0.0.0.0/0` static route
  with a higher AD than 254.
- No route recursion: a next hop must resolve on a directly connected subnet
  (`Device.egressFor`, `engine/device.js:726`), so a static route pointing at a
  next hop that is itself only reachable via another route will drop.
- No ECMP. One best route, one egress port.

**Absent**

- Route redistribution in any direction (connected ↔ OSPF ↔ BGP). Each protocol
  originates only its own source of truth: OSPF from configured OSPF
  interfaces, BGP from connected prefixes (`engine/simulator.js:577-588`).
- Route-maps, distribute-lists, offset-lists, policy routing, VRF, tunnels.
- Fragmentation and reassembly, PMTUD, IP options, jumbo frames.

---

## 4. IPv6

### 4.1 Neighbour discovery

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Every interface has an EUI-64 link-local address derived from its MAC. | `engine/device.js:468`, `engine/ipv6.js:384-405` |
| **Modelled** | NS is sent to the solicited-node multicast MAC (`33:33:ff:xx:xx:xx`), not to broadcast. | `engine/simulator.js:677-700` |
| **Modelled** | NA is returned only by the owner of the target address; receiving an NA fills the cache and flushes the pending queue. | `engine/simulator.js:747-790` |
| **Modelled** | A packet to an unresolved next hop is queued while the NS goes out, exactly like the ARP path. | `engine/simulator.js:698-742` |
| **Absent** | Duplicate address detection (DAD) — a host will happily take an address that another host already holds. Unsolicited NA, router/neighbour unreachability detection, ND table ageing. | — |

### 4.2 SLAAC

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Host sends RS to `ff02::2`; router answers with an RA to `ff02::1`; host splices an EUI-64 identifier onto the advertised prefix. | `Simulator.announceRa()` at `engine/simulator.js:2136`, `deriveAddress()` at `engine/ra.js:353` |
| **Modelled** | The messages are encoded and decoded as real ICMPv6 octets (RFC 4861 §4.2/§4.6), so a layout mistake cannot hide behind a shared object. | `engine/ra.js:160-260` |
| **Modelled** | The M (managed) flag makes the host **refuse** to autoconfigure and say why (addresses come from DHCPv6). A manually configured address is never overwritten. | `engine/simulator.js:1018-1044` |
| **Modelled** | A router advertises a prefix only on a link where it holds an address inside that prefix. | `engine/simulator.js:957-964` |
| **Approximate** | One global address per interface: the first autonomous prefix wins, the rest are ignored. Real hosts keep one per prefix. | `engine/simulator.js:1049-1052` |
| **Approximate** | Valid/preferred lifetimes are carried and displayed, never aged. An address never becomes deprecated. | `engine/cli.js:2259-2289`, displayed at `engine/cli.js:9921` |
| **Absent** | DHCPv6, DHCPv6-PD, privacy/temporary addresses, stable-privacy IIDs, RDNSS/DNSSL, RA guard. | — |

### 4.3 Hop limit and forwarding

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Hop limit starts at 64, decrements per router, expiry drops the packet. | `engine/simulator.js:761`, `engine/simulator.js:1045-1049` |
| **Modelled** | Static IPv6 routes and an IPv6 default gateway, longest prefix wins, ties broken by administrative distance through the **same** `Device.betterRoute()` the IPv4 path uses. A connected prefix (0) beats a static route (1) for the same prefix; a default gateway is distance 254, so it only answers what nothing else covers. | `engine/device.js` (`betterRoute`, `route6To`), `engine/cli.js` (`_showIpv6Route`) |
| **Approximate** | The IPv6 table has only `C` and `S` rows, because only connected prefixes and static routes exist for IPv6 here — there is no OSPFv3 and no MP-BGP for v6 to rank. The IPv4 distances for those two protocols are unused on this side, and `show ipv6 route` says so by never printing an `O` or `B` legend entry. | `engine/device.js` (`routes6Table`) |
| **Absent** | ICMPv6 errors (time exceeded, destination unreachable, packet too big), fragmentation, OSPFv3, MP-BGP for IPv6, IPv6 ACLs (see [§5.3](#53-acls)), IPv6 NAT. | — |

## 5. Control plane

### 5.1 DHCP — **in the data path** (since #1051)

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | A complete DHCPv4 model: pool normalisation, validation, DISCOVER→OFFER, REQUEST→ACK/NAK, renewals, reservations, exclusions, lease expiry — all pure and deterministic. | `engine/dhcp.js` |
| **Wired** | `Simulator.computeDhcp()` runs before the first packet of a run: every interface with `ip address dhcp` sends a DISCOVER into its broadcast domain, a server whose own interface sits in a pool's subnet answers, and the lease is installed on the interface (address, mask, gateway, DNS, lease time). | `engine/simulator.js` `computeDhcp()` |
| **Broadcast domain** | Walked rather than assumed: bridges pass the DISCOVER on, routers stop it, a spanning-tree blocked port passes nothing, and a VLAN boundary is a boundary. | `engine/segment.js` |
| **In the timeline** | The exchange is an event, so a lab can see the ACK and read the pool it came from. | `{ type: 'dhcp', phase: 'ack', ip, pool, serverIp, leaseSec }` |
| **In the CLI** | `ip dhcp pool <name>` with `network`, `default-router`, `dns-server`, `range`, `excluded-address`, `lease`, `host` (reservation). The pool is validated once, on leaving the sub-mode — a half-typed pool is not an error. | `engine/cli.js` `_dhcpPoolMode()` |
| **Visible** | `show ip dhcp binding` prints the server's lease table (via `dhcpLib.leaseTable()`, the state the exchange wrote) or, on a client, the lease it holds. `show ip dhcp pool` prints each pool with its allocatable and in-use counts. | `engine/cli.js` `_showDhcp()`, `engine/simulator.js` `dhcpBindings()` |

The failures are the ones a real server produces, and each is distinguishable:

| Reason | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `no-server` | nothing in this broadcast domain holds a pool |
| `no-pool` | a server is present, but its own address is in none of its pools |
| `pool-exhausted` | every address in range is leased, reserved or excluded |
| `nak` | the server refused to acknowledge the offered address |

These reasons are carried by the exchange event, not by the console: a failed DISCOVER lands in the run's
timeline as `{ type: 'dhcp', phase: 'failed', reason: 'pool-exhausted', clientName: 'PC2', serverName:
'R1', pool: 'ONE', detail: 'pool ONE has no address left to offer', step: 0 }`, and a client that was
already holding a lease logs `phase: 'held'` instead. Nothing prints the reason at a prompt — the client
shows `unassigned` with `Method DHCP` and its ping times out (measured; see
[CLI.md's DHCP section](CLI.md#dhcp-server)) — so the timeline is the only place a `pool-exhausted` is
stated in words.

A run asked at an instant before the exchange completes (1000 ms by default) leaves the host
unaddressed and the ping reports `converging` — see [CONVERGENCE.md](CONVERGENCE.md).

**DHCP relay is modelled** (`ip helper-address <addr>`), because its absence made a whole class of
lab impossible to build: a DISCOVER is a broadcast, so it died at the first router and a central
server could never serve a remote subnet — the client just reported `no-server`. The relay is one
logical transaction rather than a replayed broadcast: the router on the client's wire unicasts the
request to the helper, stamps its own port address into **giaddr**, and the server picks its pool by
*that* address instead of by its own interface. Which is the only reason the field exists, and the
reason a server with pools for two subnets hands out an address on the far side. Tested both ways:
giaddr is what selects the pool, and the client can then ping the server that leased to it.

Not modelled within the relay: the giaddr → pool selection is a subnet match (no `ip dhcp excluded`
per-relay, no per-helper pool overrides), the request is not re-broadcast on the server's wire, and
Option 82 (`giaddr` sub-options, circuit-id/remote-id) does not exist.

Also absent: Option 82, vendor classes, DHCP snooping, a T2/rebind timer distinct from expiry, and
any DHCPv6 (which SLAAC's M-flag tells the host to go and use — and which does not exist).

**Leases now age.** `engine/dhcp.js` exports `expireLeases(state, step)`, and for a while the only
callers were `test/dhcp.test.js` and `test/protocol-regression.test.js` — the simulator never ran it,
so a lease in a live lab stayed up however far the timeline was pushed. `computeDhcp()` now calls it
on every refresh with the instant being asked about, so a lease that is past its deadline is released,
its address goes back to the pool, and a client that cannot renew is left honestly unaddressed.

Three things had to be true before that worked, and each was a defect on its own:

* **The clock had to exist.** Leases are counted in ticks (one tick = one second), and the exchange
  stamped them with `this.step` — the *frame-hop* counter, which only moves when packets fly. So a
  60-second lease still said `Expires in 60s` two minutes later, and a fresh lab reported 62 s
  because two commands had run. `Simulator._nowStep()` is now the one conversion: the asked instant in
  seconds, or 0 when the lab is at rest.
* **A client had to keep its address.** Every refresh used to re-run a full DISCOVER for a client that
  already held a lease, so asking the same question twice could hand one host two different addresses
  (measured: `10.0.1.100`, then `10.0.1.101`) and re-stamp the timer. A client holding a live lease now
  sends REQUEST for that address — a renewal — and only once past T1, half the lease. Before T1 the
  lease is simply still valid and nothing about it changes.
* **The expiry had to survive being re-rendered.** The released count is recorded with the step it
  happened at, because a second refresh at the same instant finds nothing left to expire; the first
  version reset to zero and the empty table then claimed "no client has completed an exchange", which
  is the opposite of what had just happened.

A surviving approximation: renewal is evaluated when a question is asked, not on a clock, so between
two asked instants nothing happens — a client renews at the first question after T1 rather than at T1
exactly.

### 5.2 NAT / PAT

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Static one-to-one, dynamic pool, and PAT/overload, in both directions, with ageing in simulated steps. | `translateOutbound()` at `engine/nat.js:683`, `translateInbound()` at `:717`, with the three inside forms at `translatePat()` `:576` and `translateDynamic()` `:640` |
| **Modelled** | Wired into the forwarding path: inbound rewrite **before** the local-address test, outbound rewrite **before** the routing decision — the order a real box works in. | `engine/simulator.js:1882`, `engine/simulator.js:1978` |
| **Modelled** | Port forwarding on a static rule. | `engine/nat.js:26-27` |
| **Approximate** | Translation keys on the 5-tuple and a caller-supplied step; there are no real timers, so translations age only if the caller advances `step`. | `engine/nat.js:54` |
| **Absent — important** | **Only frames carrying an L4 header are translated.** `_natOutbound`/`_natInbound` return early when `frame.l4` is absent, and an ICMP echo has no `l4` — it is recognised by `frame.icmp` instead. Measured consequence: with a PAT rule configured and a ping from the inside host to an outside host, **the ping succeeds 4/4, `show ip nat translations` stays empty, and no `nat-*` event is emitted at all** — not even a `nat-drop`. The NAT box behaves as a plain router for ICMP, and the ping is answered at the real addresses on both sides (it still answered in a run where the outside host had no default gateway, because the reply is modelled on the request's path). So ping cannot demonstrate NAT in either direction: a NAT lab has to open a flow (`openFlow()`) and then read the table. | `engine/simulator.js` `_natOutbound()` / `_natInbound()` |
| **Absent in the CLI** | No `ip nat inside/outside`, no `ip nat inside source`: rules come from `config.nat.rules` / `config.nat.portForwards` in the topology JSON. The *result* is readable — `show ip nat translations` prints the table the packet path wrote and `show ip nat statistics` counts the rules and hits. | `engine/cli.js` (`_showNat`) |
| **Absent** | ALGs (FTP/SIP/H.323), NAT64/NPTv6, hairpinning, twice-NAT, ICMP error translation. | — |

### 5.3 ACLs

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Standard (1–99, 1300–1999) and extended (100–199, 2000–2699) lists, wildcard masks, `host`/`any`, `eq`/`gt`/`lt`, service names. | `engine/acl.js:11-26` |
| **Modelled** | First match wins; falling off the end is the **implicit deny**, marked `implicit: true` so a tool can say why. | `engine/acl.js:342-350` |
| **Modelled** | Bound per interface and direction (`ip access-group`), evaluated inbound after learning and outbound on the **physical** port after subinterface parent resolution. | `engine/cli.js:339`, `engine/simulator.js:1792`, `engine/simulator.js:1688-1697` |
| **Approximate** | Extended rules can match protocol and ports, but only the fields the engine carries: src/dst IP, protocol, src/dst port. No flags, no ICMP type/code, no TOS, no fragments. | `engine/simulator.js:1487` |
| **Approximate** | ACLs are **IPv4-only**: anything that is not `0x0800` with an `ip` header passes untouched, including all ARP and all IPv6. | `engine/simulator.js:1477` |
| **Absent** | Any state. No `established`, no reflexive ACLs, no CBAC/zone-based policy, no connection table consultation — each packet is judged alone. No logging, no counters, no time-ranges, no IPv6 ACLs, no VTY/SNMP ACLs. | — |
| **Absent** | The `firewall` capability does nothing. Sixty models are catalogued under `category: "firewall"` and 575 specs declare the `firewall` capability, yet the engine never reads the string — count both with `loadCatalog()` rather than trusting these figures, which move with the catalogue. A firewall is a router with ACLs you configure by hand. | `engine/device.js:90` |

### 5.3b Link state — **in the data path** (since #1057)

`Link` has carried `state: 'up' | 'down' | 'blocked'` since the physical layer was built, and until
now nothing in the engine read it. A cable marked down still carried every frame, still cost what it
cost in the spanning tree, and was still walked by the DHCP segment search: the field was
decoration, and a lab could show a cut link passing traffic.

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Enforced** | A frame crossing a down link is dropped with `reason: 'link down'` and the link id. | `Simulator._run()` dequeue |
| **In the tree** | A port whose cable is down is not a bridge port, so a redundant topology heals around the failure. | `Simulator.computeStp()` |
| **In the segment walk** | DHCP will not offer a lease across a cut cable. | `engine/segment.js` |
| **Flushed** | A topology change empties every bridge MAC table — a stale entry is how a real network black-holes. | `Simulator.setLinkState()` |
| **Re-based** | The convergence schedule restarts from the instant of the change. | `engine/convergence.js` `fromMs` |
| **In the drawing** | A down cable is dashed and red, and carries `data-link-state` for anything that wants to know. | `web/canvas.js` |

Not modelled: degraded links (no CRC errors, no flapping), autonegotiation on recovery, interface
self-test failures, and RSTP fast convergence. See [LINK-STATE.md](LINK-STATE.md).

### 5.4 VRRP — **in the data path** (since #1052)

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Group maths, master/backup election by priority then highest interface address, address-owner priority 255, virtual MAC formation, advertisement/skew/master-down arithmetic. | `engine/vrrp.js` |
| **Wired** | `Simulator.computeVrrp()` elects once per (broadcast domain, vrid) before the first packet. The winner claims the group's addresses and `ownsIpOn()` serves them, so ARP and ICMP for the VIP are answered locally instead of being forwarded and dying at TTL 1. | `engine/simulator.js` |
| **Virtual MAC on the wire** | The master answers ARP for the VIP with `00:00:5E:00:01:<vrid>`, not its own, and accepts frames addressed to that MAC although no port owns it. Without both halves a host resolves the gateway and still cannot reach it. | `Device.macForIp()`, `Simulator._handleFrame()` |
| **Gratuitous ARP on takeover** | The new master announces every virtual address in a broadcast ARP, which re-points the switches' MAC tables. Not decoration: without it the old master's port keeps receiving the traffic and black-holes it. | `Simulator._sendGratuitousArp()` |
| **Failover** | A master whose interface goes down relinquishes (it is no longer a candidate), and the backup takes the VIP with the same virtual MAC — the host never relearns. | `test/vrrp-in-path.test.js` |
| **In the CLI** | `vrrp <vrid> ip <address>`, `priority`, `preempt` / `no preempt`, `advertise`, plus `show vrrp` reporting Master/Backup, the virtual IP and the virtual MAC. A group belongs to an interface, so the command is refused outside one. | `engine/cli.js` |

Absent: VRRPv3, IPv6 virtual addresses, authentication beyond storing the text, and any detection of
lost advertisements — a master is displaced only by losing its interface, never by going quiet.
HSRP and GLBP do not exist.

### 5.5 OSPF

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Adjacency graph from live links, Dijkstra SPF, code `O` routes installed at AD 110, next hop resolved to a directly connected neighbour. | `engine/simulator.js:397-493`, `engine/ospf.js:467` |
| **Modelled** | Areas: one area per link, an interface area overrides the router area (the ABR pattern), and a link is only usable when both ends agree on the area. | `engine/ospf.js:10-21`, `engine/ospf.js:363` |
| **Modelled** | Cost is per interface: `ip ospf cost <n>` overrides, otherwise it is derived from the port's speed against a **10 Gbps** reference — 100M → 100, 1G → 10, 2.5G → 4, 5G → 2, 10G and faster → 1. A link's cost is the **sum of both ends**, so downgrading one end is enough to move traffic off it. | `_ospfCost()` in `engine/simulator.js`; the comment above it says "100 Mbps reference" and its own table is 10 Gbps-referenced |
| **Approximate** | No protocol state at all: no hellos, no dead timer, no LSAs, no DR/BDR election, no adjacency FSM. The SPT is recomputed from scratch before each run, so "convergence" is instantaneous and cannot be observed, only its result. | `engine/ospf.js:1-9` |
| **Approximate** | The `cost: 10` on each entry of the simulator's link list is a **last-resort fallback**, used only when an end contributes no interface cost — not the metric in the normal case. | `buildGraph()` in `engine/ospf.js`; the `links.push({ … cost: 10 })` call in `computeOspf()` |

The cost ladder is worth seeing move, because the reference bandwidth in the comment (100 Mbps) and the
one its numbers use (10 Gbps) disagree by two orders of magnitude. Three routers, a direct `R1 -- R2`
link and a two-hop detour `R1 -- R3 -- R2`, all 1G, OSPF on all three:

```
R1#show ip route ospf
  *  O 10.0.23.0/24  110  10.0.12.2  Gi0/0        (direct: 10 + 10 = 20, detour 20 + 20 = 40)

R1(config-if)#speed 100                            (one end of the direct link)
R1#show ip route ospf
  *  O 10.0.23.0/24  110  10.0.13.3  Gi0/1        (direct: 100 + 10 = 110, detour 40)
```

One end downgraded to 100M is enough to push the prefix onto the detour, which is the sum-of-both-ends
rule doing something visible. `ip ospf cost 50` on one end only moves it the same way (60 against 40),
and on both ends it reads 100 — so configuring the knob on a single router half-works, which is the kind
of half that costs an afternoon.
| **Absent** | Area types (stub/NSSA), virtual links, ABR/ASBR route leaking, authentication, graceful restart, OSPFv3, redistribution. | — |
| **Modelled** | Adjacency comes from the **segment walk**, not from direct links: two OSPF speakers that share a segment through a switch become neighbours. A tagged walk (`segmentOf(…, { vid })`) is used for subinterfaces, and a tag crosses only switch ports whose trunk permits it. Measured: `R1 -- SW1 -- R2` answers `FULL 10.0.0.2 … Gi0/0`; `segmentOf(R1, Gi0/0)` returns `SW1:Fa0/1, R2:Gi0/0`; the same call with `{ vid: 10 }` on an untagged port returns nothing. | `segmentOf()` and `portCarriesTag()` in `engine/segment.js` |

Known defects that change what a lab will show, and are now fixed - kept visible because a lab
built on the old behaviour would look wrong today: [#42, #43, #44](#10-fixed-defects-kept-visible).

### 5.6 BGP

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | A session forms only when **both** sides list the other as a neighbour with a matching remote-as. Half a peering is not a peering. | `engine/simulator.js:534-564` |
| **Modelled** | Advertisement runs to convergence in rounds (max 12) before the first packet, originating every connected prefix. | `engine/simulator.js:577-638` |
| **Modelled** | Split horizon: a route is never advertised back to the peer it was learned from. | `engine/simulator.js:609` |
| **Modelled** | Best path: weight, local-preference, locally originated, shortest AS path, lowest origin, lowest MED, eBGP over iBGP, lowest router ID — in that order. | `engine/bgp.js:408-436` |
| **Modelled** | AS-path loop prevention including AS_SET members; 4-byte ASNs; eBGP prepend, iBGP no-prepend. | `engine/bgp.js:308`, `engine/bgp.js:503` |
| **Modelled** | Administrative distance 20 for eBGP, 200 for iBGP, installed into the routing table. | `engine/simulator.js:1956` — `ad: adj.type === 'ebgp' ? 20 : 200` |
| **Approximate** | MED is compared unconditionally. Real BGP compares MED only between paths from the same neighbour AS. Declared in the module header. | `engine/bgp.js:18-19` |
| **Approximate** | The FSM exists (`idle→connect→active→opensent→openconfirm→established`) but is never fed: hold/keepalive timers are validated and carried, never expire, and no session ever drops. | `engine/bgp.js:14-21`, `engine/bgp.js:220-266` |
| **Approximate** | Next hop is rewritten to the advertising router's address on every advertisement, which is right for eBGP and wrong for an iBGP relay — see defect #41. | `engine/simulator.js:1949` — `nextHop = adj.type === 'ebgp' ? adj.localIp : (e.nextHop \|\| adj.localIp)` |
| **Absent** | Route reflectors, confederations, peer groups/templates, AFI/SAFI negotiation, IPv6 unicast, communities, route-maps, prefix-lists, `next-hop-self`, dampening, graceful restart. | — |
| **Absent in the CLI** | `router bgp`, `neighbor … remote-as`, `router-id` only. | `engine/cli.js:228-246` |

`docs/BGP-POLICY.md` is a **design document**, not a description of the code:
its tables read "Today … This design changes it to" (`docs/BGP-POLICY.md:39-50`)
and route-maps/communities are §8 and §5 of a proposal. None of that policy
layer exists in `engine/bgp.js` today.

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |

The failure is named for what actually happened: `dns-nxdomain` (the name is not in the zone), `dns-nodata` (the name exists, the type does not — NOERROR with an empty answer), `dns-servfail` (no device in the lab hosts a zone). | `engine/simulator.js:137`, `engine/simulator.js:228-260` |

### 6.5 Ferrule polish refusals

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Six ferrule shapes with polish and angle: SC/UPC flat, SC/APC 8°, LC/UPC, LC/APC, MPO/UPC, bare LC → UPC. | `engine/media.js:250-257` |
| **Modelled** | Same shape, different polish → refused with `E_POLISH_MISMATCH` and a message that explains the ~3 dB cost and the risk of chipping the endface. | `engine/media.js:1264-1277` |
| **Modelled** | The one legal way across a polish boundary is a declared **hybrid cord**, which is charged its insertion loss and flagged `W_HYBRID_CORD`. | `engine/media.js:691-706`, `engine/validation.js:138` |

### 6.6 Cable length

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Length is a property of the lead (`Link.lengthM`), not a maximum reach; null means "not stated". | `engine/link.js:14-36` |
| **Modelled** | `W_DISTANCE` fires only when a length **and** a medium reach are both known. An unstated length produces no warning rather than an invented one. | `engine/validation.js:770-780` |
| **Readable** | `show interfaces transceiver [<port>]` prints the numbers that verdict was made from — negotiated medium, rate, reach, declared length — and the `W_DISTANCE` code itself, so a link that mates but cannot carry light is visible from the console instead of only in the verdict a cable made once. | `engine/cli.js` `_showTransceiver()`, `test/transceiver.test.js` |
| **Modelled** | `W_COPPER_LONGRUN` at 90 m for the horizontal-run limit. | `engine/validation.js:157`, `engine/validation.js:781-790` |
| **Approximate** | Length affects **latency only** ([§8](#8-time-what-rtt-means-and-what-it-does-not)). An over-length cable still passes every packet perfectly — the warning is the entire model of the failure. | — |
| **Absent** | Attenuation, link-loss budgets for copper, bend radius, cable damage, intermittent faults. | — |

### 6.7 The cable matrix

Every cage pair has an **expected verdict** in a table that is treated as the
specification, not as a snapshot of the engine — a divergence fails the test and
names the pair, and each accepted pair must also produce a real link with a
sane medium and rate (`test/cable-matrix.test.js:1-30`).

That is the strongest correctness claim in the repo, and it is worth knowing
that it covers **cages and speeds**, not "this cable will carry traffic".

---

## 7. Wireless

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | Five radio media: 2.4 GHz, 5 GHz, 6 GHz (Wi-Fi 6E), 6 GHz (Wi-Fi 7), 60 GHz (802.11ad). | `engine/media.js:145-149` |
| **Modelled** | A radio's band set follows its **generation**, which follows its rate (n/ac/ax/be), unless the spec states bands explicitly. A Wi-Fi 5 radio is 2.4+5. | `engine/media.js:532-560` |
| **Modelled** | 60 GHz is a different radio, not a faster one: it is selected from the model text (`60 GHz`, `802.11ad`, `WiGig`), never from a high rate. | `engine/media.js:867-870` |
| **Modelled** | Association = the best shared band caps the rate, so a 2.5 Gbps phone on a 574 Mbps 2.4 GHz radio associates at 574 instead of refusing. | `engine/media.js:1148-1162` |
| **Modelled** | A radio can never be cabled to a wired port (`E_WIRELESS_TO_WIRED`), and an antenna port is not a cable at all. | `engine/media.js:1234-1237` |
| **Approximate** | "Wireless" is a link type with an antenna cage. There is no shared medium: two clients on one AP do not contend, do not share airtime, and cannot hear each other. | — |
| **Absent** | SSID, BSSID, beacons, association/auth frames, WPA/PSK/802.1X, channels and width, interference, RSSI, rate adaptation, retries, roaming, mesh, band steering, hidden SSID, MU-MIMO/OFDMA. `grep -i "ssid\|channel\|interfer" engine/*.js` finds nothing in the forwarder. | — |

A wireless "cable" in PacketForge is an Ethernet link with a different cage.
That is enough to teach cabling and VLANs over a radio, and not enough to teach
anything about 802.11.

---

## 8. Time: what "rtt" means and what it does not

| Verdict | Behaviour | Where |
|---|---|---|
| **Modelled** | A round trip is **computed** from the path the exchange actually walked. Every `frame` event between request and reply names the port it left; that port's link supplies the medium, the length and the line rate. | `engine/simulator.js:180-208` |
| **Modelled** | propagation = `length / (velocity factor × c)`, with per-family velocity factors (copper 0.66, fibre 0.68, coax 0.77, DAC 0.70, wireless 1.0, DSL 0.66, powerline 0.60). | `engine/latency.js:70-79`, `engine/latency.js:157` |
| **Modelled** | serialisation = `bits / line rate`, with the frame measured from destination MAC to FCS, plus 4 bytes per VLAN tag. | `engine/latency.js:165`, `engine/latency.js:186-194` |
| **Approximate** | A per-hop store-and-forward term of **50 µs** is charged on every crossing but the last. This is a single constant for every device in the model — switch, router, firewall — and is labelled as an approximation in the source, not derived from hardware. | `engine/latency.js:86-98`, `engine/latency.js:174` |
| **Modelled** | Round trip = forward + reverse, each summed over its own legs; both directions are charged, because the frame is serialised at every port twice. | `engine/latency.js:296-318`, `engine/simulator.js:204` |
| **Modelled** | An unstated length costs 0 m and reports `lengthKnown: false`; an unstated rate costs 0 s. The model refuses to invent a distance. | `engine/latency.js:100-106`, `engine/latency.js:132-136`, `engine/simulator.js:279` |
| **Absent** | Queueing delay, jitter, host ICMP processing, NIC interrupts, kernel wakeups, ARP resolution time, optical dispersion and FEC. All four are named as deliberately excluded at `engine/latency.js:22-31`. | — |

Three consequences a lab author needs:

1. **There are no timers anywhere in the engine.** `grep -n "setTimeout\|setInterval\|Date.now" engine/*.js` finds only comments. The delay is a
   number computed at the end of a run that already happened
   (`engine/latency.js:32-34`). Nothing sleeps, nothing interleaves, and a run
   takes the same wall-clock time whether the path is 1 m or 40 km.
2. **`hops` is not a duration.** `replies[].hops` is still the queue-step count
   (`engine/simulator.js:276`); `replies[].rtt` is the computed round trip in
   milliseconds, with `rttUs` alongside.
3. **RTT is a floor, not a prediction.** With queueing excluded, the number is
   what an idle network would deliver. A lab that "proves" a latency SLA is
   proving arithmetic.

---

## 9. What this is not

Blunt list. Each line is a thing someone reasonably assumes, and what is
actually true.

| Assumption | Reality | Where |
|---|---|---|
| "It's a TCP/IP stack." | There is a tiny TCP model — three-way handshake, MSS from path MTU, cumulative ACKs, four-way close — and nothing else: no congestion window, no retransmission timer, no SACK, no TIME_WAIT, no window scaling, no ICMP port-unreachable for stray UDP. | `engine/flow.js:19-24`, `engine/simulator.js:2249` |
| "Traffic starts connections." | Only an explicit API call does. `openFlow()` is invoked by the server on `POST /flows`; no packet ever triggers a connection on its own. | `engine/simulator.js:2249` |
| "Services respond." | A capability only registers **listeners** — `http-server` means "ports 80 and 443 answer SYN" (`engine/flow.js:77-85`). There is no HTTP request, no TLS handshake, no SSH banner, no FTP, no SMTP. A closed port answers RST, which is the whole application layer. | `engine/simulator.js:2546-2560` |
| "Congestion happens." | Nothing is ever dropped because of load. There are no queues, no buffers, no contention, no tail drop, no AQM. A link at 100 % of its rate behaves exactly like an idle one. | — |
| "Loss happens." | Nothing is lost except by an explicit rule: VLAN, ACL, STP, PoE, TTL, no route, NAT failure. A cable 10× over its reach still delivers every packet. | — |
| "There are real timers." | None. No scheduling, no concurrency, no ageing, no expiry. See [§8](#8-time-what-rtt-means-and-what-it-does-not). | `engine/latency.js:32` |
| "It's a stateful firewall." | ACLs are per-packet, stateless, IPv4-only. The `firewall` capability is inert. | `engine/simulator.js:1477`, `engine/acl.js:342` |
| "Routes redistribute." | They do not. Connected → OSPF and connected → BGP only; no OSPF→BGP, no BGP→OSPF, no static→anything. | `engine/simulator.js:577-588`, `engine/simulator.js:406-419` |
| "MPLS / VXLAN / GRE / IPsec / LISP / SRv6." | None of them. `grep -ri "mpls\|vxlan" engine/` returns nothing. | — |
| "DAI, DHCP snooping, IP Source Guard, RA guard." | None. ARP and ND are trusted completely. | — |
| "IPv6 is first-class." | ND and SLAAC are good; ACLs, NAT, routing protocols and first-hop redundancy are IPv4-only or absent. | `engine/simulator.js:1477`, `engine/device.js:484` |
| "HA exists." | VRRP is in the data path (#1052), so a virtual gateway fails over. Still absent: HSRP/GLBP, BFD, LACP failure detection, graceful restart, and detection of a master that goes quiet rather than down. | `engine/simulator.js` `computeVrrp()` |
| "Multicast is routed." | Multicast floods like broadcast inside a VLAN and is never routed between subnets. No IGMP, no PIM. | `engine/simulator.js:1842` |
| "QoS exists." | No marking, no queues, no shaping, no policing, no CoS/DSCP anywhere. | — |
| "Counters show errors." | `show interfaces` counters are bytes/packets in and out. No CRC, no runts, no giants, no collisions, no drops-by-reason beyond the event log. | `engine/flow.js:354-360` |
| **Modelled — the conversation is readable and drivable** | A flow can be started and closed from the console (`flow open <dst> [port…] [bytes…] [proto…] [sport…]`, `flow close <id>`, where `dst` is an address or a name), not only through the UI/API, and it is never only a timeline entry: `show flows` lists every conversation still in the table, `show flows <id>` prints one in full (both ends by device name, state, segmentation, MSS/MTU, egress port), and `show flows counters` prints per-device bytes/packets/opened/refused. The heading separates `recorded` from `opened` because a refused flow is counted and then **deleted** — so `recorded < opened` is the trace of a refusal, not a defect. `Opened` is a logical step, not a time. | `engine/cli.js` (`_flowCommand` 8371, `_showFlows` 8679), `engine/flow.js:400` (`listFlows`) and `:407` (`describe`) |
| "A device can be overwhelmed." | Control-plane work is instantaneous: OSPF/BGP reconverge for free, LLDP/RAs cost nothing, and 8 000 steps is a loop guard rather than a capacity limit. | `engine/simulator.js:110` |
| "A hop is a time." | A step is a frame handed to a port. Latency is computed separately. | `engine/simulator.js:276` |
| "Config is validated." | Each module validates its own config when asked to; the engine will happily run an inconsistent topology and show you whatever the graph produces. | `engine/validation.js:1-24` |

And a short list of things that exist on the model but are **never read** —
i.e. configuring them is decoration:

- `Link.state` (`engine/link.js:41`) — `'up' | 'down' | 'blocked'` is serialised and never consulted; only `iface.enabled`, STP blocking and PoE gate a frame.
- `minLinks` on a LAG (`engine/lag.js:55`).
- LAG `mode` (`engine/lag.js:217`) — validated, then ignored.
- `voiceVlan` (`engine/vlan.js:13`).
- `iface.dhcp` (`engine/cli.js:307`).
- Capabilities `firewall`, `wireless`, `dhcp-server`, `dhcp-client`, and the tokens `vlan`/`lacp`/`stp` (`engine/device.js:85-98`).
- RA valid/preferred lifetimes (`engine/cli.js:2259-2289`).

---

## 10. Fixed defects, kept visible

Five control-plane defects were found by reading the code rather than by tripping
over them, and they are **fixed**. They stay listed because each one changed how a
lab behaves, and a topology saved before the fix may not route the way its author
expected:

| Issue | What used to happen | Now |
|---|---|---|
| **#41** | BGP re-advertised an iBGP-learned route to another iBGP peer, rewriting the next hop to the relayer — traffic that a real iBGP mesh without a route reflector would never carry. | A route is not sent back to the peer it came from, and learned routes are not relayed to other iBGP peers. |
| **#42** | OSPF installed a route for a router's **own** connected network at transit cost, so a code `O` entry could shadow the `C` entry. | Connected networks are excluded from OSPF installation; the connected route is the route. |
| **#43** | A passive OSPF interface was dropped from the graph entirely, so its prefix disappeared instead of being advertised as a stub. | The prefix is advertised; only the adjacency is suppressed. |
| **#44** | When a network was reachable from several routers, the **more expensive** advertisement won, by device-id order. | Cheapest cost wins; a genuine tie breaks on the lower advertising router id, so the result is deterministic. |
| **#54** | NAT's flow key omitted the inside source port, so two sockets from one host behind PAT collided on a single translation. | The key includes the inside port; each socket gets its own global port. |

Two more were found the same way and fixed in the same pass:

| Issue | What used to happen | Now |
|---|---|---|
| **#240** | An OSPF adjacency formed from the cable alone, so two routers addressed in different subnets installed routes via a next hop on no connected subnet — traffic black-holed. | Both ends must share subnet **and** mask; a mismatch forms no adjacency. |
| **#241** | Every DNS failure returned NXDOMAIN — a missing A record, a missing name, and a lab with no DNS server all looked identical. | `dns-nodata` (NOERROR, empty), `dns-nxdomain`, `dns-servfail` are reported separately. |

The mechanism this page relies on: a defect found while writing a suite is pinned
as a `DOCUMENTS BUG` assertion, so the suite stays green while the bug is live and
**fails the moment somebody fixes it** — the fix cannot land without flipping the
assertion to the correct behaviour. As of this revision no such assertion remains;
if one reappears, it names a live defect.

### One live defect, unasserted

**A rejected `access-list` line answers `% [object Object]`.** `acl.js` returns the failure as an
object (`{ ok: false, code: 'E_ACTION', message: … }`) and the CLI's error path concatenates it into
a string instead of reading `.message`. Measured on four different mistakes — `permitx` as the
action, `bogus` as the protocol, `eq 99999` as a port, and `no access-list 101 99` for a sequence
number that does not exist — every one prints the same `[object Object]`, and in each case the list
is left untouched, so nothing is corrupted and nothing is explained. The parser itself is well covered — `test/acl.test.js`
asserts `E_ACTION` three times, at three levels (the raw parser, `validateAcl()`, and the round trip) —
but **nothing asserts what the console prints**, so a stringified object passed every suite. That
gap in coverage, rather than a gap in the parser, is why it survived. It is recorded in `docs/CLI.md`
next to the syntax so a reader is not left thinking they mistyped a path into the tool.
---

## 11. Where the other docs overstate the code

Found while writing this page. Rows marked **Fixed** / **Reconciled** / **Filled in** have since
been corrected in the document named; the rest are recorded so the next reader does not have to
rediscover them.

**Seven shapes, and the rows below are mostly one of them.** If you are auditing a page and want to know
what to look for, these are the failures this codebase actually produced, each with a row that
illustrates it:

1. **A count no build reads** — models, vendors, media, cages, suites, ports. Every one of them was
   correct when written and none of them is checked by anything. Recount; do not read.
2. **A number that means something else** — an assertion count that grew (`79 → 133`), a line citation
   that now points into an unrelated function, `grep -c` counting a different thing than the prose means.
3. **A documented form the engine answers differently** — a command that refuses, an argument read as a
   timestamp instead of an id, a two-argument form that silently means "…at t=2 ms".
4. **A status word that expired** — `Absent` about a command that has since landed, `Approximate` about
   arithmetic that is now exact. The word was true on the day it was typed. Several rows already carry the
   correction inline, in the form *"at the time; per-entry removal landed later — see the row on
   `no access-list`"*, which is the honest way to keep the record: the claim stays, dated.
5. **A tool contract that drifted** — a flag attributed to the wrong script, or a flag that is silently
   ignored and performs the default action instead.
6. **Two pages disagreeing, or one page disagreeing with itself** — the corrected note under a table
   contradicting the row above it is the commonest version.
7. **True of the library, false of the product** — `expireLeases()` and the DHCP exchange were both
   implemented, tested and never called. "The code can do it" and "the simulator does it" are different
   sentences and only the second one belongs in a fidelity claim.

**About the assertion counts in these rows.** A count here is what the suite printed *when the row was
written*, not a promise about today — suites grow, and most of these rows are history. Do not quote one
forward as current. Measured today: of **83** suite-plus-count citations across `docs/`, `README.md`,
`CONTRIBUTING.md` and `docs/wiki/`, **21 disagreed with a run**, always in the same direction (the suite
had gained assertions: `absent-commands.test.js` 79 → **133**, `completion.test.js` 34 → **52**,
`ipv6-route.test.js` 45 → **53**, `show-at.test.js` 37 and 42 → **43**, and fifteen smaller drifts of one
to five). The four that were stating a *current* fact rather than a measurement — three in
[`CLI.md`](CLI.md) and one in [`REQUESTING-HARDWARE.md`](REQUESTING-HARDWARE.md) — were corrected. To
recount any of them:

```sh
node test/show-at.test.js | tail -1      # prints "43 passed, 0 failed"
grep -rno 'test/[a-z-]*\.test\.js[^|]\{0,40\}[0-9]\+ assert' docs/ | head
```

**About the `file.js` + line number citations.** These rot faster than anything else on a page, because nothing
in a build reads them. A mechanical sweep of every `file.js:NNN` citation in these docs — **321** of them,
across 40 files — found **17** in two pages pointing at code that has nothing to do with the sentence:
`E_NO_COMMON_SPEED` was cited at `media.js:709`, which is the `qsfpdd:` speed table; the radio-association
row pointed at the same table when the logic lives in `negotiateSpeed()` at `media.js:1148`; `W_POE_DRAW`
was cited at `validation.js:668`, a `Math.min` line, 150 lines above the warning; `E_POLISH_MISMATCH` at
`media.js:262`, a comment about Cat 4 modems, 1,000 lines from the check. All seventeen were re-pinned to
the line that actually contains the identifier or the `code:` string, and where a definition had moved
(`Device.routeTo()` 366 → **541**, `Simulator.reset()` 106 → **141**, `hybridCord()` 324 → **377**,
`Device.egressFor()` 524 → **726**) the citation moved with it. Prefer the symbol over the number: a line
number cannot be checked by reading, and a grep for the symbol can.

| Document | Says | Reality |
|---|---|---|
| `README.md:24` | "89 media types across nine families … 26 connector cages" | **Correct as of this revision**: `engine/media.js` holds 89 media in 9 families across 26 cages. (An earlier revision said 53 / eight / 18; that count is gone.) |
| `README.md:86` | "1,584 models across 245 vendors, in 24 categories" | **Correct as of this revision** — `node scripts/audit-devices.js` prints `1584` / `245`, and the 24 categories match the palette's `CAT_ORDER` in `web/app.js` (the figure has since moved to 1,586 — two devices landed; the point of that row is the mechanism, not the number). |
| `README.md:19-21` | "Real simulation … MAC learning, flooding, ARP, longest-prefix routing, default gateways, TTL decrement and ICMP echo/reply" | Accurate, and a statement of scope rather than an understatement — the feature table below it carries the rest of the list, transport flows included. |
| `README.md` "What this does not model yet" | "Flows are tracked; TCP state, HTTP, TLS and friends are not simulated" | **Fixed**: TCP state *is* modelled (`engine/flow.js` — handshake, MSS from the path MTU, cumulative ACKs, four-way close, pinned by `test/flow.test.js`). The bullet now names the real remaining gap: no application protocol above the transport. |
| `README.md:88,92` (was) | Protocols row listed "ICMP echo + **errors**, **traceroute**"; the CLI row listed a `traceroute` command | **Fixed, then overtaken by the feature**: ICMP errors are still absent (echo request/reply only — no destination-unreachable, no time-exceeded, no redirect), but `traceroute` / `tracert` now ships (`Simulator.tracePath()`, `CliSession._traceroute()`): it prints the path the routing *tables* select with cumulative one-way delay, and deliberately does not send TTL-limited probes. Both rows and the ICMP bullet now say that. |
| `README.md:24-28` | "Physically accurate … impossible cables are refused, not drawn" | Accurate and well tested; just remember [§6.6](#66-cable-length) — length is otherwise cosmetic. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:41` | capability table: `nat`, `firewall`, `wireless`, `http-server` "reserved for future protocol support" | **Fixed**: the table now lists all fifteen capabilities grouped by what reads them (forwarding predicates / PoE derivation / flow service ports / nothing). `nat` and `firewall` are inert as *flags* but NAT and ACLs work from configuration, and `http-server` is real — it names a listener table. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:61-69` | Routing order: connected, static, default gateway | **Fixed**: the page now states longest-prefix first with the real administrative distances (connected 0, static 1, eBGP 20, OSPF 110, iBGP 200), and that the default gateway is only a fallback. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:94-111` | Flow API and counters | Accurate. |
| `docs/CLI.md:50` | `ip address dhcp` — "(reserved)" | **Fixed**: the exchange runs in the data path (`Simulator.computeDhcp()`), and the page now says so. |
| `docs/CLI.md` (whole) | Documented `show`, `interface`, `ip address`, `ip route`, `ping`, IPv6 ND | **Filled in**: `access-list` / `ip access-group`, `switchport` (incl. subinterfaces and `encapsulation dot1q`), `channel-group`, `ospf` / `ip ospf`, `router bgp` / `neighbor`, `dns`, `scion`, per-mode `help`/`?` and the `show` gaps are now documented from the parser, not from memory. The page also records the one remaining gap (per-entry `no access-list`) and that `show running-config` now round-trips the configuration completely — IPv6 RA policy, the DHCP flag on a dual-addressed port, and a paste of the dump reproducing the dump. |
| `docs/ROADMAP.md:7-19` | Listed VLANs, STP, OSPF/RIP, NAT, ACLs, wireless, DNS as unchecked TODOs | **Reconciled**: each shipped item is now ticked with the module and suite that proves it; TCP/UDP, RIP, 802.11 association and wireless remain open, and the line says which part exists. |
| `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` "Derivation" and the error tables | "If unset, a port is a PSE when the model name contains `PoE` or the device declares `poe-pse`"; 18 cages; 11 error codes and 5 warnings | **Fixed**: the PoE budget now documents the real resolution chain (`poeBudget` → `poe.budgetW` → `poe.budgetUnknown` → a `NN W` token in the model, and the name guess is *discarded* unless a port declares `poeOut`; there is no `poe-pse` check in that path). The cage count is 26, `inferCage()`'s ten ordered rules are listed instead of four, and the error/warning tables gained `E_POLISH_MISMATCH`, `E_PATCH_CORD_UNKNOWN`, `E_PATCH_CORD_ENDS`, `E_OPTICAL_BUDGET`, `W_HYBRID_CORD` and `W_OPTICAL_HEADROOM` — every code `engine/validation.js` can emit is now in them. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` "The server" | "Topologies are kept in memory (one default topology, plus named ones on demand)" | **Fixed**: they are also written to disk — one file per board in `data/topologies/`, atomic temp+rename, immediate rather than debounced, `PF_DATA` overrides the tree. The page now says that, and `docs/ROADMAP.md` has persistent storage ticked rather than pending. |
| `docs/DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` "Capabilities" | Listed 10 capabilities and said `host`, `l2-forward`, `l3-forward` **and `cli`** "decide behaviour" | **Fixed**: there are 15. A first pass of this row claimed only three were read at all; that was too strong and is corrected here too — `poe-pse`/`poe-pd` are read by `isPoePse()`/`isPoePd()` in `engine/media.js`, and `cli`/`dhcp-server`/`http-server` are read by `SERVICE_PORTS` in `engine/flow.js` to decide which ports a flow may terminate on. Seven are labels with no reader anywhere (`nat`, `firewall`, `wireless`, `dhcp-client`, `vlan`, `lacp`, `stp`), and the page now says which configuration or port field drives each feature. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` "Capabilities, not vendors" | Table of 10 capabilities, with `nat`/`firewall`/`wireless`/`http-server` marked "reserved for future protocol support" and DHCP "live" *because of the flag* | **Fixed**: same fifteen, grouped by what actually reads them (forwarding predicates, PoE derivation, flow service ports, nothing). DHCP server is live because of `ip dhcp pool`, not the capability. The "Adding a feature" section also stopped using DHCP as an example of a protocol that does not exist yet, and now names `_handleLocalIp6` alongside `_handleLocalIp`. |
| `README.md` "Timed interleaving" | "no hello loss, no dead-interval expiry, **no re-convergence after a topology change**" | **Fixed**: re-convergence is exactly what `markChange()` and `convergence.buildSchedule(topo, {fromMs})` do — `test/link-state.test.js` asserts "converges again at 40000 ms" after a cable is pulled at 10 s, and the same README's "Link failure" row says "every timer re-runs from the instant of the change". The bullet now separates the real gap (nothing expires on its own; there is no clock) from the capability that exists. |
| `docs/CONNECTORS.md` glossary and console row | "`CAGES` (18 entries)", "`MEDIA` (53 entries)"; console = one medium at 115200 baud | **Fixed**: 26 cages, 89 media across 9 families, and five console media (9600/19200/38400/57600/115200). Added a "checking this table against the engine" section with two runnable one-liners, because no audit script covers cages/media and both catalogues are functions of the port rating. One real disagreement is written down rather than papered over: `xgspon-*` media exist but `CAGE_MEDIA` returns only `gpon-*` for the SC/LC cages. |
| `CONTRIBUTING.md` | "PRs are carried across **automatically** by `gh-bridge.js`"; "docs/CI.md lists the **five** checks"; capabilities list of 10 | **Fixed**: no workflow invokes the bridge, so a GitHub PR waits for a maintainer; the test job runs **seven** checks plus an API-smoke job and a Dockerfile job; the capability list now matches `CAPABILITIES` (15). |
| `docs/REQUESTING-HARDWARE.md` "What happens next" | "**Automatic acknowledgement.** A workflow labels the issue `device-request` and comments with what is present and what is missing"; CI "fails if" five things | **Fixed**: nothing triggers on issues (`.forgejo/workflows/` = `ci.yml` + `auto-rebase.yml`), so the label and the reply come from a maintainer running `request-queue.js`. The invariant list now says it is a sample of **22** assertions and adds the line-rate-vs-cage check, PoE plausibility, zero interfaces and the stale known-gap list. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` — the API surface | The page documented four board endpoints plus the two catalogue ones and said so — "Four board endpoints (plus two catalogue/build ones)". The dispatcher in `server/index.js` has **sixteen** namespaces under `parts[3]`, so the UI's actual client surface was undocumented | **Wrote the missing table and then tested every row against a live server** (`PORT=8123`, scratch `PF_DATA`). Found and documented the shapes that matter: `POST …/devices` returns `409` with the *existing* device named when a client-supplied id is taken (confirmed live), `POST …/ping` returns `{ events, replies, success, step, runtime }` (2 echoes, 23 events, step 8), `POST …/links` returns `{ link, state }` — not `warnings` as I first wrote — `POST …/interfaces/…` gives `400` for a role the hardware does not offer and `200` for a description edit, `POST …/layout` ignores ids that are gone, `POST …/reset` clears flow state too, and an unknown route under a real board answers `404 { error: 'unknown endpoint: <path>' }`. Two corrections came out of the probing rather than the reading: there is **no `GET …/links`** (the cable list rides in `state.links`), and `…/links/:linkId` is `DELETE` to remove or `PATCH`/`POST` to set `lengthM` with `422` on a bad length — not a re-patch. The best find was behavioural: the console session is kept per `topology:device`, so a second `/config` call resumes in `(config)#` and refuses `enable`. Anyone scripting the API would hit that on their second request. Verified in the same pass that the counts every page quotes still hold — 1,584 models, 245 vendors, 16,151 effective ports (`effectiveInterfaces`, not the raw array), 89 media in 9 families, 26 cages, 124 suites — and that all four assertion counts cited in the docs still match: 67, 13, 27, 22. `node scripts/build-wiki.js` is deterministic and the six wiki pages already match their templates. One environment note for the next pass, learned the hard way: a backgrounded `node server/index.js` reports as **exited** (`stdin is not a tty`) in the process tracker while the node child keeps the port, so `curl` still answers after the tracker says the job is dead — kill it by owning PID (PowerShell `Stop-Process`) or the stray server outlives the check. |
| Every number in the docs — and the command that produced it | The pages quote exact figures (1,584 models, 89 media, 145 codes, 134 suites) with no statement of how to re-derive one, and `CI.md` was still quoting "131 suites, 5,568 assertions" from a run several commits old | **Ran the whole set and wrote the commands down.** Measured fresh: **134 suites in 166.7 s, 5,648 assertions pass, 4 fail** — so `CI.md` was wrong on all three numbers and presented the tree as greener than it is; the two red files are unchanged in cause (`spec-vs-description` on four Ubiquiti specs claiming `poeOut` while their own description says PoE-in, and `canonical-urls` tripping on the untracked `DO-NOT-USE.md`, whose failure takes two cleanup assertions with it). `CONTRIBUTING.md` now carries a **re-measure appendix** — one verified command per published figure — and `CI.md` says to quote the runner rather than the number. **Writing the appendix caught a live trap:** my first draft's one-liners returned "0 models / 0 vendors / 0 ports", because `loadCatalog('devices')` returns **1,584 entries and all 1,584 are errors** — the helper `require()`s `path.join(dir, …)`, a relative path is read as a module specifier, nothing resolves, and the `catch` meant for one broken spec swallows the directory. `path.resolve('devices')` is the fix; the trap and the `{ vendor: 'error' }` row shape are now documented in `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` too, since the same filter is what keeps a count honest after an import. |
| Numeric audit — counts, categories, suites | Long-lived pages quote model, vendor, port, media, cage and suite numbers; this loop has already had to move several of them once | **Re-measured every number the docs quote against the current tree.** Still exact: **1,584** models, **245** vendors, **16,151** effective ports (`effectiveInterfaces`, not the raw array), **89** media in **9** families across **26** cages, **1,234** specs carrying a `sourceUrl`, **104** models with a `+`, and `node scripts/run-tests.js --list` reporting **128 suite(s)** — equal to `ls test/*.test.js \| wc -l`, so the runner glob and the tree agree. **One number was wrong in two pages and right in a third.** The catalogue holds **25** distinct `category` values, not 24: everything in `CAT_ORDER` plus a single legacy `ap` spec (`huawei/WiFi Extender AX3000`, filed by an importer that predates the `wireless` bucket). `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` already said 25 and explained the 24 buckets; `README.md` said 24 and enumerated 24 names, and an older §11 row claimed the 24 categories "match the palette's `CAT_ORDER`", which is only true after dropping the odd one. README now reads "25 `category` values rendered as 24 palette buckets". **The practical consequence is worth the words**: `buildFilterControls()` builds the type `<select>` by walking `CAT_ORDER`, so that device has no dropdown entry at all and is reachable only through palette search — documented in `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` rather than left to be rediscovered. Suite count moved 127 → 128 during this pass, so the counts are drift-prone by nature and the pages that use them quote a command alongside. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` — the live-sync section, checked with a real socket | The WebSocket table promised a protocol nobody had driven: connect → `default`, `subscribe`, broadcast, discard, ping/pong. All plausible from the code, none of it observed | **Wrote a zero-dependency RFC 6455 client (handshake, masked text frames, ping/pong) and drove the server with it.** Every claim in the table holds: connect delivers `{ type: 'topology', topologyId: 'default' }` before any subscribe; `subscribe boardA` delivers boardA; `subscribe nope` is silently ignored; **a mutation on `boardB` arrives at a client watching `boardA`** (topology ids observed: `default, boardA, boardB`), so the broadcast really is global; invalid JSON and an unknown `type` both vanish with no reply; a `0x9` ping comes back as `0xA` pong. Two things the section did not say went in: **the socket is not mounted at a path** — `upgrade` runs before routing, so `/`, `/ws`, `/deep/path` and `/api/topologies` all answer `101` — and **a handshake with no `Sec-WebSocket-Key` is destroyed with no HTTP reply**, so a client that forgets the header sees a silent close instead of a `400`. Both measured across four paths and two handshake shapes. |
