| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` and `docs/ROADMAP.md` — deleting a board is not durable | The persistence paragraph said boards are written to `data/topologies/<id>.json` and delete was "idempotent and unauthenticated"; nothing said the file survives the delete, and `PF_DATA` was described as relocating the data directory without saying the boards go in a subdirectory of it | **Deleted a board and restarted the server to see what the file tree actually remembers.** Create `ghost`, add a device, `DELETE` it: `GET /api/topologies` reads `{"ids":["default"]}`, yet `topologies/ghost.json` is still on disk. Boot the server again and the board is back — `{"ids":["default","ghost"]}` and `GET /api/topologies/ghost` answers `200`. Deleting is forget-in-memory, so anything removed in the UI returns on the next restart until the file is deleted by hand. Two more measurements went into the same paragraph: **`PF_DATA` is the parent** and boards land in `$PF_DATA/topologies/<id>.json`; and a **newly created board is not written at all until its first edit** — `POST /api/topologies` alone leaves no file, so the directory listing is boards that have been *edited*, not boards that exist. Corrected the `DELETE` bullet to say idempotent, unauthenticated **and not durable**, added the missing gap to the ticked ROADMAP row, and cleaned up the two probe boards (`lab-f`, `lab-flows`) my earlier API passes had left in `data/topologies/` — the directory is gitignored, which is why they never showed in `git status`. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` — the `flows` namespace | Last pass added the sixteen API namespaces and this one documented eleven of them; `…/flows` (four endpoints) had no row, and the link body shape was never written down | **Enumerated `parts[3]` again after the sibling's engine work and tested the missing four against a live server.** `GET …/flows` returns `{ flows, counters }` (counters is `{}`, not a zeroed table, before anything opens); `POST …/flows` answers `201 { flow, mtu, mss, segments, events, step, counters, runtime }` with `state: 'established'`, `bytesDelivered: 2000`, `openedStep: 2`; `GET …/flows/:id` is `200` or `404 { error: 'flow not found' }`; `DELETE` is `200` with `already: false` then `already: true` on the second call, while a made-up id is `404 { error: 'no such flow: …', code: 'E_NO_FLOW' }` — so "already closed" and "never existed" differ. Two asymmetries went in with it: a refused flow is reported in the `POST` body only and is **not** retrievable afterwards (fetching its id 404s), and an unknown device is a `400` with no `code` field at all, so a client switching on `code` calls it unclassified. **The link body was the trap worth catching**: `POST …/links` needs `{ a: { deviceId, iface }, b: { … } }`, and the flat form returns `409 { error: 'Unknown device: undefined', code: 'E_LINK', issues: [] }` — a status that reads like a mating refusal when nothing parsed. I hit it myself: my first flows probe used the flat body, the cable was never made, and `POST /flows` dutifully answered `E_UNREACHABLE`; the docs now warn that both the parse failure and a real refusal arrive as 409, so read the message rather than the code. |
| `docs/CLI.md` DHCP — reservations, and a pool that vanishes quietly | The syntax block listed `host <address> <mac>` and nothing ever showed it being used; more importantly nothing warned that a reservation can take the whole pool down | **A reservation outside `range` silently disables the pool.** Measured by building the same lab three ways: base pool → `ack`, client `192.168.10.100`; with a gateway → `ack`, same; with `host 192.168.10.200` (outside `range 100-120`) → `no-pool`, client stays `unassigned` and the server has no bindings. `dhcpLib.validatePool()` knows exactly why and returns `E_DHCP_RESERVATION_OUT_OF_RANGE` naming the reservation and the range, but the exchange **skips** an invalid pool instead of refusing, so nothing prints it; the only symptom is the DHCP event blaming the server's own address (`the address of 192.168.10.1 is in none of the server pools`), which sends you to the wrong config line. Moving the reservation inside the range (`host 192.168.10.110 …`) makes the client take exactly that address, so the page now shows the reservation working (`Reserved : 192.168.10.110 for 02:00:00:00:01:00` in `show ip dhcp pool`, `IP address : 192.168.10.110/24` on the client) and states the trap plainly. Also re-verified the segment claim from the previous pass properly this time: server — switch — client completes DORA on a plain `ping` and the client takes `192.168.10.100`, so "bridges pass it" holds; the relay note for routers was measured in the previous pass and is unchanged. Two of my own probe bugs on the way: `runCommands('PC1', …)` takes a device object, not a name (`Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'hostname')`), and a plain `ping` before any DHCP-relevant run had already been explained by the out-of-range pool rather than by the ping. |
| `docs/CLI.md` DNS — the type argument, and reverse lookups | The page promised `nslookup <name> [type]` with a table of types and the empty-NOERROR rule, written as though the type always applied; nothing said what a PTR does | **Both promises needed qualifying.** Measured on a real client-server lab across a router: the type argument is honoured only against the device's **own** zones — a client asking `AAAA` gets the `A` value under a bare `->` line (`www.lab.example -> 10.0.2.10`), and a client asking `MX` gets `lab.example exists but has no A record (NODATA)`, i.e. it answers a question you did not ask. The same two commands **on the server** produce the typed answers the page shows, so the section now says which path does what. **PTR has no working path at all**: `dns add 10.0.2.10 PTR www.lab.example` is accepted, `show dns` lists it, and `nslookup 10.0.2.10 PTR` still answers NXDOMAIN from the lab path. I first wrote that building an `in-addr.arpa` zone by hand would fix it, then tested it and it does not — the query name is never rewritten into the reverse form, so the same NXDOMAIN returns. The published advice is now that PTR is a record you can store and read rather than one you can query, which is the measured truth. Also hit a mid-edit syntax error in `engine/cli.js` (`Unexpected identifier 'port'` near line 2195) from the concurrent engine work; re-ran once it loaded, so no probe result in this pass came from a half-written tree. |
| `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` PoE — the PSE that refuses to be a PSE | The section documented the budget chain (spec → research → unknown → model-name guess) and the populated/denied outputs, but not the common case where a device declares `poe-pse` and carries no wattage anywhere | **Hit it by wiring a MikroTik PoE router to a real AP**: the switch answers `% SW1 neither sources nor draws PoE` while the AP prints `12.9 W requested from SW1 port eth1 - no PSE answers`. Both true, neither sufficient — the refusal is keyed on a resolved budget, not the capability — so a reader would reasonably conclude PoE is broken. Counted how common it is before writing: **60 of 102** PSE-capable devices have no budget (57 MikroTik, 3 Ubiquiti), which is why this belonged in the page rather than a footnote. Also checked the working case so the positive path is on record: an Aruba 1830 65 W switch with an AP11 on port 1 prints `Budget : 65 W  (from research)`, `Used : 12.9 W`, `Available : 52.1 W`, `1  AP1  12.9 W  on`, with the PD agreeing (`12.9 W from SW1 port 1 - powered`). And verified that `poe.budgetUnknown: true` is unused by every device in the catalogue before describing it as an escape hatch rather than a pattern. |
| `docs/CLI.md` ACLs — no refusal shown, and pings hide one | The section explained building a list, binding it and unbinding it, and never showed what a **denied** packet looks like — which is the only thing you see when your lab stops working | **Captured both, and they disagree.** A denied flow is explicit — `% flow refused (E_ACL_DENY): denied by access list 101 in Gi0/0 on d1`, naming the list, direction and port — while a ping denied by an ICMP-deny ACL prints nothing but `....` and `Request timed out (seq 0..3)`, identical to a pulled cable. Bound `in` on the ingress port or `out` on the egress one, both really do filter transit traffic (measured in both directions with `show access-lists` confirming `applied OUT on Gi0/1`). Two smaller measurements went into the same block: the refusal names the device by **id** (`on d1`) even when `hostname CORE-RTR` is configured — verified by setting the hostname and re-triggering — and the `try … ping` hint after a refusal is not a diagnosis, since a ping cannot confirm an ACL that denies only TCP. |
| `docs/CLI.md` LAG — what `(P)` means | The page showed the `show etherchannel` table and left the reader to assume `(P)` was a status, since every example has healthy members | **Tested the bundle under load and under failure.** A two-member static LAG carries traffic (`4/4`), survives losing one member (`4/4` again after `link down` on `l3`), and carries nothing when both are down (`0/4`) — and **the table is byte-identical in all three cases**: `1      static    Fa0/1(P) Fa0/2(P)`. So `(P)` marks membership, not health, and `show etherchannel` is not a health check; `links` is where a dead member reads `down` on its own row. Documented exactly that. Along the way I nearly filed a false alarm against `links`: my probe filter dropped any line containing `>` or `#`, which silently removed every cable row because the endpoints are printed as `SW1:Fa0/1 <-> SW2:Fa0/1`. With a correct filter `links` behaves perfectly — `down` on the cut cable, `up` on the other, restored by `link up`. The lesson is the filter, not the engine: strip prompts with an anchored pattern against the prompt text, never against `>` in general. |
| `docs/CLI.md` subinterfaces — no end-to-end example | The section showed the three config lines and the refusal for `encapsulation dot1q` on a physical port, which proves the parser accepts them and nothing about whether tagged frames cross | **Built the full router-on-a-stick lab and it works**: two VLANs, one access port each, a trunk to a router with `Gi0/0.10` and `Gi0/0.20`, and `PC10 -> PC20` across subnets pings `4/4` at `0.307 ms`. Captured the config and the ping as a worked example, plus the two views that prove the tagging landed — `show ip interface brief` listing the subinterfaces beside an `unassigned` parent (`Gi0/0.10   10.10.0.1/24  YES  manual  up  up`) and `show ip route` carrying both as connected on the subinterface names (`C  10.10.0.0      /24  via -  Gi0/0.10`, the format at the time — the mask is printed attached now; see the `show ip route` row). Then tested the failure I had just described: drop VLAN 20 from the trunk's allowed list and the ping goes to `0/4` while **nothing else changes** — `show vlan brief` still lists `20 SERVERS active Fa0/3`, both subinterfaces still read `up up`, and the only visible difference is that the trunk appears under VLAN 10 alone (`Fa0/1(t)`). That is the silence worth warning about, so the page warns with measured evidence rather than a guess. |
| `docs/CLI.md` DHCP — the segment boundary, and BGP vs OSPF peering | The DHCP section explained pools, leases and `In use` but never said a pool only serves its own broadcast domain — and the failure is silent from both ends. The OSPF note added a wakeup earlier needed its mirror image, because BGP behaves the opposite way | **Measured both directions.** DHCP: a client behind a router gets no lease, and the two consoles say `% PC1 is not a DHCP server (no pools) and holds no DHCP lease` and `DHCP bindings on SRV: (none - no client has completed an exchange)` — honest, and neither one explains that routers stop the DISCOVER. No relay agent exists (`ip helper-address` is not in the parser, and `SIMULATION.md` already said so in a list; the page where you configure a pool did not). Now it does, with the advice to give each segment its own server. BGP: put two eBGP routers on either side of a switch and the session reaches `Established  eBGP` — the exact topology where OSPF refuses to form an adjacency — because a BGP session is keyed on the configured neighbour address and rides the data path. Both pages now say so, so the next person to see one protocol work through a switch and the other not knows it is the model, not their config. |
| `docs/SIMULATION.md` §5.5 and `docs/CLI.md` OSPF — multi-access segments | The fidelity table said "adjacency graph from live links" and the CLI section said nothing, so the obvious campus lab — two routers in one VLAN through a switch — looked supported | **Measured what actually happens and documented the gap.** No adjacency ever forms across a switch: checked at 29 s, 30 s, 31 s and 40 s, with the switch's ports already `designated  forwarding`, and the neighbour table stayed at `(no adjacencies - no neighbour passed the passive and subnet checks)` throughout. The cause is in `computeOspf()` — it walks `topo.links` and keeps only links whose **both** ends run OSPF, so a switch between two speakers breaks the pair into two links that each have one speaker. Wrote that into §5.5 as an explicit *Approximate* row (no broadcast/multi-access segment model), and noted in `CLI.md` that the empty-table message blames passive/subnet checks — true, and not the reason in this case. Also added the BGP counterpart to `CONVERGENCE.md`, which listed six timers and silently omitted BGP: there is no ConnectRetry or hold timer, `show ip bgp` reads `Established` at **t=0**, and `converge` in a BGP-only lab says `% Nothing in this lab runs a control plane that converges over time`. A run is still needed — a fresh lab says `(no neighbours have been evaluated yet - run a simulation)` — but the run needs no time. |
| `docs/CLI.md` OSPF — the `INIT` claim | Said the neighbour `state` is `FULL` at the run instant and `INIT` before it, and offered `ping … at <ms>` **or** `converge <ms>` as the way to look at an earlier instant | **Half of that was wrong and the corrected half is a trap worth naming.** Measured: `converge 500` is a pure query — the instant does not move, and the next `show ip ospf neighbor` still reads `FULL`. `ping … at 500` *does* leave the simulator sitting at t=500, and the `INIT` then **sticks**: a plain `ping` afterwards does not clear it, a bare `converge` does not clear it, and `show ip ospf` counts it honestly as `Neighbors: 1 (0 FULL)`. Only `ping … at converged` goes back, after which the row reads `FULL` again. Wrote that cycle into the page instead of the parenthetical. Added the empty-adjacency state too, since a failed neighbour is the common case and the table explains itself: `(no adjacencies - no neighbour passed the passive and subnet checks)` — reproduced with a passive port and again with a subnet mismatch. |
| `docs/CLI.md` VRRP — the `Master` column and the backup line | Presented `show vrrp` as reporting "the state after the last election" and described the `(no group holds its address yet - run a simulation)` line as something only a pre-run lab prints | **Checked it on a pair of routers sharing group 1, and both readings were wrong.** The `Master` column prints **this device's own name** on every row — a backup row reads `Backup  R1`, which looks self-contradictory because the peer is never named. And the "no group holds its address yet" line still prints on a **healthy backup** long after the election, because it is keyed on whether *this* device won rather than on whether the group is up. The page now says to read `State`, not `Master`, and explains what the line means. Also measured the tie-break nobody documents: at equal priority the **higher interface address** wins (`10.0.12.2` beat `10.0.12.1`, virtual MAC `00:00:5e:00:01:01` appearing only on the winner), so a lab with two default-priority routers is decided by address, not by which one came up first. |
| `docs/CLI.md` — the `traceroute` transcripts | Four worked examples of the newest command, all written before `renderTable()` landed. Every one was missing the engine header row and used hand-typed column offsets | **Re-rendered all four from live runs**: `Hop  Address  Cumulative  Device` is the real header, hop delays carry a `*` (a link with no declared length or line rate falls back to a documented default) with the footnote `* 3 links had no cable length or line rate, so the documented default was used`, and the arithmetic cross-check was re-measured rather than assumed — `102.6 us` one way is the `0.205 ms` a ping on that path reports, so doubling the last figure still gives the round trip. Confirmed the two failure messages reproduce verbatim (`% no route to 8.8.8.8 on PC1`, `% next hop 10.0.1.99 is not a device in this lab`) and that `at 5000` still stops with `% no route … yet: ospf still converging (ready at 16000 ms)`. The same sweep found the defect twice more: `show dns` was missing its `Name  Type  Value` header, and `show flows counters` carried the column padding of a wider lab. Fixed both. Then checked the remaining table surface deliberately — `show ip route` (`Codes: C - connected, S - static` plus both rows) and `show ip dhcp binding` reproduce to the column, `show power inline` answers `% SW1 neither sources nor draws PoE` as documented — so all seventeen `renderTable` tables are accounted for. |
| `docs/TRIAGE.md` — the quoted close comment | Quoted the comment the sweep posts, but with an em dash the script never emits and without its closing line, so anyone diffing the doc against a real comment would find two differences and trust neither | **Quoted it verbatim** — hyphen, and the trailing `Closed as already modelled.` paragraph included. Then re-measured the numbers the page is built on rather than trusting them: the catalogue really has **104 of 1,584** models carrying a `+`, `RB5009UPr+S+IN` and `RB5009UPr+S+OUT` both exist (so the `+`→`plus` rule has live work to do), `roku/Ultra` exists (so the "Ultra" anecdote is a real near-miss), and the guards are where the page says — `hasDigit \|\| modelKey.length >= 2`, vendor agreement, longest-tail first. The live issue count is still **126**, and all 126 carry both `device` and `device-request`, so the page's opening sentence was current to the day. |
| `docs/TRIAGE.md` — where the script points | Never mentioned that `scripts/sweep-device-requests.js` has `API = 'http://10.0.10.20:3000/api/v1'` hard-coded in the file, so a contributor on any other network gets a connection failure and no clue why | **Documented it above the flag table**: the address is a LAN one, the token is scraped from the `github`/`origin` git remote, and the public host answers the same API — so the read-only checks in the page work from anywhere while closing issues needs the expected address. The page's own `curl` example already used the public host, which is what made the mismatch invisible. |
| `docs/LINK-STATE.md` — the `converge` and `ping` samples | Both transcripts were hand-written. The `converge` block showed a `Basis` of "forwarding delay after a topology change", which the engine never prints, and the ping example was a bare `....` with the claim that it fails with `reason: 'converging'` | **Replaced both with verbatim captures** from a two-switch redundant pair cut at 10 s. The real `Basis` is `listening + learning (15 s forward-delay each) (after the change at 10000 ms)  (pending)`, one row **per switch**, with a `(pending)` marker that distinguishes "waiting" from "never ran". The real ping at 11 s prints four separate `% Still converging at t=11000 ms: stp on SW1, stp on SW2 (ready at 40000 ms)` lines — one per lost echo, not a single `reason:` field — and the same question at 41 s is `!!!!` / `100 percent (4/4)`. Added the flow-window variant too: `flow open … at 11000` refuses with **`E_STP`** ("port in STP blocking state") and a `% yet:` line, not the `E_CONVERGING` a reader might expect. Everything else on the page verified: `link-state.test.js` **67** assertions and `link-state-drawing.test.js` **13** pass, `.link.link-down` is at `web/style.css:199` and really dashes and recolours, and the router-only rough edge reproduces to the line — `Network converges again at 40000 ms` followed by `Fully converged at 10000 ms`. The API wart the page warns about is real: `server/index.js` sets `state` twice in that reply object and the second wins, so a client reading `state` gets the topology. |
| `docs/FIBRE-CHANNEL.md` — the quoted refusal | Quoted the engine's `E_PROTOCOL_MISMATCH` message as `fc-sfp28 is a Fibre Channel port and SFP+ is Ethernet…` — the cage **id**, which the engine never prints | **Replaced with the verbatim text**, `SFP28 (FC) is a Fibre Channel port and SFP+ is Ethernet…`, captured from a G610 port mated against a real SFP+ port. Everything else on the page checked out against the engine, which is unusual enough to record: all eight rate-ladder rows match `speedMbps`/`fcRate` exactly (2125, 4250, 8500, 10519, 14025, 28050, 57800, 112200), all four cage ladders match `CAGE_SPEEDS` row for row, `cagesMate('sfp+', 'fc-sfp')` is `true` as claimed, `negotiateSpeed` on a 64G/32G pair returns **28050**, and every device in the table matches the catalogue — 5800V 20 ports at 2/4/8G plus 4 at 10GFC, G610 and SN3600B 24 × 32G, G720 56 × 64G, MDS 9124V 24 × 8/16/32/64G, MDS 9148S 48 × 2/4/8/16G, DS-6630B 96 × 32G + 8 × 128GFC. |
| `docs/FIBRE-CHANNEL.md` — the 1G SFP claim | "An FC optic in a **1G SFP** cage is refused on the *connector*" — a claim no test pins, so nothing would have caught it going stale | **Verified it, then wrote down why it looks wrong from the outside.** The refusal is real (`E_CONNECTOR_MISMATCH`, `Cannot mate SFP+ (FC) to SFP (incompatible connectors).`), but the raw matrix disagrees: `cagesMate('sfp', 'fc-sfp')` is **`true`**. The test file explains in a comment that it tests the SFP+ pair instead precisely so the refusal is about the protocol — which leaves the connector case unpinned and the two functions looking contradictory to anyone reading `media.js`. The page now says which one a link is judged by: `cagesMate` answers "could these sockets ever meet", `canMate()` is what decides. |
| `docs/CONNECTORS.md` Speeds column | A reader comparing the table to `CAGE_SPEEDS` finds the table saying `sfp28` carries 10000/25000 while `CAGE_SPEEDS['sfp28']()` is `[]`, with nothing to explain the difference | **Added the distinction**: the Speeds column is what media in that cage can carry, while `CAGE_SPEEDS` is only the fallback for a port declaring neither `speedCaps` nor a rate label — and several cages have no entry **on purpose**, because a port there should state what it trains at rather than inherit a number (`sfp+` supplies `[1000, 10000]`, `sfp28` supplies nothing). Measured each of the 26 cages' entries to write it. |
| `docs/CLI.md` — new "Which shows need a run first" | Nothing anywhere told a reader that half the `show` output is written by a **run**, not by configuration — so the placeholder answers looked like broken features | **Swept all eighteen `show` commands on one configured lab, then swept again after a single `ping`, and tabulated what changed.** Only four move: `show arp`, `show mac address-table`, `show lldp neighbors`, `show vrrp`; `show ip bgp` joins them once a peer is actually configured (`0 of 0 neighbour(s) established` → `1 of 1`, `Established  eBGP`), and `show spanning-tree` on a switch in a loop goes from `No bridged ports` to the port table. Everything else — running-config, interfaces, vlan, access-lists, dns, power inline, route, nat, flows — answers immediately. The section ends with the pattern behind it: a table the **data path** writes is empty until frames cross it, a table the **control plane** elects is empty until the election runs. **My first draft of the table was wrong and the sweep caught it**: I had put `show ip ospf neighbor` in the needs-a-run column, but it prints `FULL` before any ping because the command calls `computeOspf()` itself — moved to an explicit exception note, which is a more useful fact than the row it replaced. |
| `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` §7 "Check it from the console" | A bare list of three commands with no output, and the claim that blocked ports "drop both ingress and egress traffic" with nothing to compare against | **Put the measured tables in.** Built a two-switch double link and captured both ends: the non-root switch prints `Fa0/2  alternate   blocking   (traffic dropped)` while the root keeps `designated`/`forwarding` on both ports, which is what "a loop becomes a working topology" actually looks like. Added the precondition that caught me out: **STP state is computed on a run**, so a freshly opened lab with the loop already cabled answers `No bridged ports: this device is not part of any L2 loop.` — same wiring, honestly reported as not-yet-computed — and the table appears after any `ping`, flow or `converge`. Verified the loop really is broken rather than described as broken: SW2's `device.stp.states` reads `Fa0/2: blocking` with role `alternate`. |
| `docs/CLI.md` `show etherchannel` | Output sample padded `Protocol` one column wider than the engine prints | **Corrected to `Group  Protocol  Ports`** and confirmed the populated table against a real bundle: `1      static    Fa0/3(P) Fa0/4(P)`, with the `(P)` marker per member. The section's documented wart reproduced exactly, which is the point of documenting it: `channel-group 2 mode active` is accepted *and* warns `unknown mode "active" (expected one of lacp-active, lacp-passive, static)`, while `mode lacp-active` — the spelling the validator wants — is rejected with `% Invalid mode: lacp-active (use active\|passive\|on)`. So a static LAG is still the only one the CLI can build. **Since fixed:** one table (`LAG_CLI_MODES`) translates the console's words into the validator's, so `mode active` stores `lacp-active` and prints `LACP active`, `mode on` stores `static`, and the library spellings are accepted as well — an LACP bundle is now buildable from the console, and `show etherchannel` prints the protocol instead of the stored identifier. |
| `docs/CLI.md` IPv6 section | The `R1# show ipv6 nd` in the worked example was followed by a **blank line** — the command's actual output was never shown — and the host example implied a SLAAC address appears as soon as `ipv6 address autoconfig` is typed | **Filled in both transcripts from the engine**, including the router's seven-line report (`Advertise : enabled`, `Flags : M=0 O=0 router lifetime 1800s`, the `prefix  L  A  valid(s)  preferred(s)` table, `Interfaces : Gi0/0 (2001:db8:aa::/64)`) and the host's, which also carries a `Default gw : fe80::ff:fe00:0 via eth0` line the page never mentioned. Then documented the precondition that makes the example true: `Autoconfig` reads `eth0 (waiting for a router)` until a **run** happens, and **nothing on the console starts an IPv6 run** — `ping 2001:db8:aa::1` is refused (`% Invalid ping target`), `flow open 2001:db8:aa::1` is refused (`% Invalid destination`), and `show ipv6 route` / `show ipv6 interface` do not exist. The path that exercises IPv6 is `Simulator.ping6()`, from the API and the tests. Before this, a reader following the section would type `show ipv6 nd`, see `waiting for a router` forever, and have no way to know why. |
| `docs/CLI.md` SCION section | `show scion`'s Reachability block listed only reachable ASes, and the end of the report was undocumented | **Added the two lines the engine actually prints**: an AS with no usable path appears as `1-ff00:0:0        unreachable` inside the reachability list, and the report closes with `("show scion paths <isd-as>" describes the selected path)`. Also documented the refused-segment form — `no beaconed path (1 refused)` — and that a reachable AS prints `1 path  best 3 hops, 4 ms`, singular for one and plural beyond. Verified against a three-AS lab (non-core 110, core 0, non-core 120) whose path output reproduced the page's `[0] up` / `[1] down` / `hops:` breakdown exactly. |
| `docs/CLI.md` ACL syntax block | Put `no ip access-group …` at column 0 under an `interface Gi0/0` block, which reads as a global config command — and `no ip access-group` at the config prompt is refused (`% Unrecognized config command`) because the binding belongs to an interface | **Indented both `no` forms into the interface block and said the rule out loud.** Also made the `show access-lists` example self-consistent: its config lines add `permit` *and* `deny`, but the sample output printed only entry 1; the real table lists both (`1 permit tcp any any eq 80` / `2 deny ip any any` / `    applied IN on Gi0/0`). Verified the surrounding claims while there: unbinding one direction prints `Interface Gi0/0: removed access list binding (in)`, the argument-less form clears the bound direction, and `no access-list <n> <entry>` really did drop the whole list *(at the time; per-entry removal landed later — see the row on `no access-list`)*. |
| `docs/CLI.md` `show arp` row | "the Interface column is derived from the topology" — with no hint that the derivation is narrow | **Measured when it works and when it does not.** Directly cabled: `Internet  10.0.0.10  -  02:00:00:00:01:00  Gi0/0`. Same ping with a switch in between: the column reads `-`, because the lookup only matches a **direct peer** of this device, and the owner is behind the switch. The row now says so and adds the `(self)` case. Subinterfaces checked in the same pass: `interface Gi0/0.20` + `encapsulation dot1q 20` works, appears in `show ip interface brief`, and the same command on a physical port is refused with `% encapsulation dot1q is only valid on a subinterface (e.g. Gi0/0.10)`, exactly as documented. |
| `docs/CLI.md` `show running-config` round-trip | Claimed the dump is "pasteable … the device comes back identical", unaudited | **Tested it three ways rather than trusting the sentence**: dumped a switch config (two VLANs, an access port, a trunk with native VLAN and an expanded allowed list), a router config (address, static route, OSPF, BGP with a neighbour, DNS zone, ACL) and an IPv6 RA config, replayed each into a fresh device of the same model, and dumped again — **byte-identical all three times**. Administrative state turned out to survive with an IOS-style asymmetry worth writing down: a port is up by default, so `no shutdown` never appears in the dump while `shutdown` does, which means a restored device comes back up unless the dump shut it. That is now in the page next to the claim. The VLAN section was verified in the same pass: `show vlan` marks trunk ports `(t)`, an allowed list `10,20,30-32` is echoed as `10,20,30,31,32`, and `vlan 10 LAB` takes its name from the trailing text as documented. Two sloppy probe sequences of mine (a stray `exit` that dropped me to exec mode, and `name LAB` on its own line) looked like engine bugs for a minute — the `% Unrecognized` replies were the engine correctly refusing what I typed, and both were my errors, not the doc's. |
| `docs/CLI.md` Flows — refusal hint | Showed the `% the timeline says why - try show flows, or ping …` line only under the `E_REFUSED` example | **It prints on every refusal that is not a convergence wait**, measured: an `E_NO_ROUTE` for `flow open 192.0.2.99 port 80` emits it too, where it points at a timeline that has nothing to say — the answer is in the route table. Added the line to that example and a sentence saying the hint is a hint, not a diagnosis. The rest of the section reproduced exactly, including the success line the page quotes word for word (`Flow flow-1 opened: tcp 10.0.1.10:49152 -> 10.0.2.20:80, established` / `5000 bytes in 4 segments, 11 packets on the wire, MSS 1460 (MTU 1500)`), the `E_REFUSED` text for a port with nothing behind it, and `show flows`'s summary (`Flows in this lab (1 recorded, 1 opened, 0 refused):`) with its `Id Proto Source Destination State Payload Segs Pkts Opened` row. Re-checked the cable commands in the same pass: `links`, `link state l4`, `link down l4 at 10000` (`… is now down.` / `Network converges again at 40000 ms.`) and `link up l4` all behave as the page says. |
| `docs/CLI.md` `converge` | Documented as `converge [at <ms>]`, matching the `ping … at` and `traceroute … at` forms | **That syntax does not work, and it fails silently.** `_converge()` reads `tokens[0]` as a number, so the instant is a **bare** argument: `converge 5000` returns rows marked `(pending)`, while `converge at 5000` — the documented form — prints the same table with no marks and no error, which is indistinguishable from a lab that has already converged. Rewrote the section with both measured transcripts (with and without the instant), stated the bare-number rule, named the silent-ignore trap, and corrected the two other places on the page that repeated `converge at <ms>`. Also added the answer an idle lab gives: `% Nothing in this lab runs a control plane that converges over time`. Verified the neighbouring claim in that section: `ping 10.0.0.1 1 at 5000` really does print `(at t=5000 ms)` and `% Still converging at t=5000 ms: ospf on R1, stp on SW1 (ready at 16000 ms)`, so the `at` keyword is fine for ping — it is only `converge` that diverges. |
| `docs/CLI.md` traceroute | Main example showed hop delays with no `*` markers | **Checked against a four-device lab and left the page alone** — the asterisks are conditional, not missing: a link with no declared length or line rate gets a `*` and the footnote `* 3 links had no cable length or line rate, so the documented default was used`, which the page already documents two screens down. My probe lab produced `102.6 us*` where the doc shows `103.2 us` precisely because its links carry no lengths. The arithmetic claim survived the test: one-way × 2 matched the ping (`102.6 µs` → `0.205 ms`, the doc's `103.2 µs` → `0.206 ms`), and the `Path: PC1 -> R1 -> R2 -> PC2  (3 hops)` summary reproduced exactly. |
| `docs/CLI.md` OSPF `show ip ospf` block | Printed the interface rows without the column header the engine emits | **Verified the whole OSPF section against a live two-router adjacency** and found one missing line: the real output has `    Interface       Cost  Area  State` between `Interfaces:` and the first row. Everything else matched to the character — the process header, `Neighbors: 1 (1 FULL)`, the `show ip ospf neighbor` column layout and the `FULL`/`Gi0/0` row, and `O` appearing in `show ip route`'s code line once a route was learned. Added the header row, and recorded the refusal a reader will meet while typing the section's own example: `% OSPF is not enabled (use "ospf router-id <ip>" in config mode)`. |
| `docs/CLI.md` BGP and DNS sections | — | **Checked line by line and found nothing wrong, which is worth writing down.** `show ip bgp` before a run reproduces exactly (`BGP router identifier 1.1.1.1, local AS number 65001`, the empty `Neighbor  V  AS  State  Reason` header, `(no neighbours have been evaluated yet - run a simulation)`, `0 of 0 neighbour(s) established`); `dns add` before a zone is refused with the quoted message; `show dns` prints the two-record zone with `Name servers: none (queries go to any server in the lab)`; authoritative `nslookup` prints `Query: … A -> NOERROR (authoritative)` and the `3600 IN A` record from the server but `www.lab.example -> 10.0.0.10` from a client; an unknown type is refused with the list. Two sections that needed no edit, now known to be verbatim rather than assumed. |
| `docs/CLI.md` `show etherchannel` / `show power inline` | Described only their populated output | **Added the empty states both commands actually print**, measured: `% No link aggregation groups configured` for a switch with no bundles, and `% SW1 neither sources nor draws PoE` on a device that is not a PSE or a PD. A reader on a plain L2 switch would otherwise meet a refusal with no documentation for it, and the refusal is the honest answer — it does not invent a budget. |
| `docs/SIMULATION.md` §5.2 NAT "Absent — important" row | Said a ping across a NAT box "reaches the router, fails the *is this address mine* test, and is dropped as unroutable" | **The mechanism was right and the consequence was wrong, and the difference matters.** Built the PAT lab and measured it: `ping 203.0.113.9` from `192.168.1.10` succeeds **4/4**, `show ip nat translations` stays empty and the event log contains **no `nat-*` event at all** — not even a `nat-drop`. ICMP is not dropped for being unroutable; it simply never enters the NAT stage (`_natOutbound`/`_natInbound` early-return when `frame.l4` is absent, and an echo is recognised by `frame.icmp` instead), so the box behaves as a plain router and answers at the real addresses. It even answered in a run where the outside host had **no default gateway**, because the reply is modelled on the request's path — so the old wording's "dropped as unroutable" case does not reproduce. The row now states the measured result and the operational consequence: ping cannot demonstrate NAT in either direction. Citations in that row became symbols (`_natOutbound()` / `_natInbound()`) while I was in it. |
| `docs/CLI.md` NAT `protocol` row | Listed `icmp` as a supported rule protocol with no caveat, implying an ICMP rule would translate a ping | **Consequence of the measurement above**: the field is accepted and validated (`engine/nat.js` really does know `icmp`, with a 60-second default timeout), but no ICMP packet ever reaches the table during a run. The row now says so and points at a new paragraph in the same section — *"ICMP is never translated"* — carrying the measured numbers, so a reader who writes an `icmp` rule learns before spending an afternoon on why it appears to do nothing. |
| `docs/CLI.md` DHCP `lease` | The pool examples print `Lease : 600s` while the syntax block says `lease <days>`, which reads as though `lease 600` were 600 seconds | **Measured all three forms against the engine**: `lease 600` → `51840000s` (600 days), `lease 1` → `86400s`, `lease 0 0 10` → `600s`. The engine is IOS-correct and the page was the confusing part, so it now says the unit out loud, gives the three verified numbers, and names which case the examples below are. Also corrected my own first draft of that note: I had written that the table's `594s` was "six seconds of exchange", which I had not measured — a real DORA run followed by a four-ping run reads **570s**, so the note now says `Expires in` counts down and gives the instant it was read at. Verified in the same run that a client's `Lease : 600s` and the server's `Expires in` agree, which is the claim the section actually cares about. |
| `docs/CLI.md` VRRP | `show vrrp` shown only in its resolved state — `Master`, a virtual MAC, no hint that a configured group starts unresolved | **Added one paragraph from the measured pre-run output**: before any run the table reads `Backup`, the virtual MAC is `-`, and a line underneath says `(no group holds its address yet - run a simulation)`. A configured group is not a resolved one, and the page had been showing only the happy state. |
| `docs/CLI.md` "Example session" | A hand-formatted transcript — `Router> enable`, spaces after the prompt, `R1# show ip route` — with the engine's own confirmation lines edited out | **Replaced with the engine's verbatim output**, captured by running the session through `runCommands()`: `Router-d1>enable`, `IP address set on Gi0/0: 10.0.1.1/24`, `Gi0/0 up`, and `R1>show ip route` where the doc had claimed `R1#`. The differing prompt exposed a real behaviour: **`exit` from config lands in plain exec (`>`), not privileged (`#`) as IOS does**, and nothing is enforced by the difference — `show ip route` works from `>`. That is now a bullet rather than an unstated surprise, along with the id-suffixed prompt an unnamed device shows and the rule that a state-changing command prints its confirmation. Everything else in the block was checked while capturing: the route-table column padding, the four per-sequence timeouts, and "a device cannot ping its own address" (`ping 10.0.2.1` from R1 times out despite R1 owning it). The *second* example in that page (`time=0.103 ms`) was verified too and needed no change — a two-PC switch lab reproduces `4/4` and `Reply from 10.0.1.11: bytes=100 time=0.103 ms` exactly. |
| `docs/BGP-POLICY.md` code map and inline citations | Every anchor was a bare line number into files that has since been rewritten: `cli.js:143-161` was labelled "config-mode BGP dispatch" but now points at exec-mode `nslookup`/`converge`/`link` dispatch; `cli.js:385` was labelled `this._dnsZone` and now points at VRRP group deletion; `simulator.js:507-545` was the BGP advertisement loop, which the session work replaced wholesale (it now lives in `computeBgp()`) | **Converted the document to symbol citations** — `bgp.js` `comparePaths()`, `compareForBest`, `advertise()`, `pathIsLoopFree`, `Simulator.computeBgp()`, `CliSession._configMode()`, `CliSession._bgp()`, `CliSession._help()`, `this._dnsZone` — with a note above the table saying why: line numbers in `bgp.js` survive because nobody moves that file, and ones in `cli.js`/`simulator.js` do not. Twelve inline references in the proposal sections were rewritten the same way. The substantive claims were checked while converting, and they still hold: `computeBgp()` really does re-derive the AS path and next hop inline instead of calling `advertise()` (`asPath = adj.type === 'ebgp' ? [a.as].concat(e.asPath) : e.asPath.slice()`), which is precisely what the design proposes to change. **A mechanical fix was attempted first and rejected**: I built an old-to-new line mapper from the `git diff -U0` hunks and it would have rewritten 108 citations in three documents — but spot checks showed it lands on the wrong code wherever the target region was itself edited (`cli.js:143-161` mapped to `159-178`, which is `_configMode()`'s `hostname`/`vlan` branches, not the BGP ones), because insertions inside a rewritten region shift everything after them. Renumbering is not a safe transformation; naming a symbol is. |
| `docs/SIMULATION.md` citation policy | "Citations also name the symbol, because line numbers drift" — with no statement of *when* the numbers were taken, and 92 of them pointing into files the engine work has since moved | **Pinned them to a commit and said so out loud**: the numbers were accurate at `5218e7e`, `cli.js` has since grown by several hundred lines spread across 27 hunks, and a number into `cli.js`/`simulator.js`/`device.js` is a hint rather than an address — `grep -n '<symbol>'` is the citation that still resolves. Counted the outstanding job rather than hand-waving at it: **22 rows carry a symbol, 92 carry only a number**, and the conversion is named as outstanding. This is the honest version of a page whose whole value is that its citations can be trusted. |
| `docs/SIMULATION.md` intra-page links | Two `#anchor` links pointing at headings that no longer say what the link claims: `#51-dhcp--absent-from-the-data-path` (the §5.1 heading now reads "DHCP — **in the data path** (since #1051)") and `#10-known-open-defects` (the section is titled "Fixed defects, kept visible", and #42/#43/#44 in it are fixed) | **Found by counting, not reading.** Nothing in the repo checks anchors, so I checked all **32** intra-repository anchor links across the README and `docs/` the way a renderer resolves them — collect every heading, slug it, assert each `#fragment` lands on one. Two failed, both here. Repointed the first at the real §5.1 slug, and rewrote the second to say the defects are **fixed and kept visible** rather than "known", since the destination says exactly that. Re-ran the check: **32 checked, 0 broken**. The gap is now written into `docs/CI.md` with the three-line test that would close it, because a heading rename silently breaks every link to it. |
| `README.md` protocols row | "NAT (static + PAT — configured in the topology JSON, **no CLI command yet**)" | **Half wrong in a way that undersold the engine.** There is no `nat` *config* command, and `docs/CLI.md` says so correctly, but `show ip nat translations` and `show ip nat statistics` both exist and are listed in CLI.md's command table — the README sentence read as though NAT were invisible from the console. Reworded to "translated in the data path, configured in the topology JSON; there is **no `nat` config command**, but `show ip nat translations` / `statistics` read back what the data path wrote", linking the CLI.md section. Verified rather than assumed: `test/nat-show.test.js` runs **27 assertions, all passing**, covering the translation table and the statistics naming the static rule. Also regenerated the wiki (`node scripts/build-wiki.js`, 6 pages) and confirmed `docs/wiki/` now matches its templates. Along the way, a coverage check of CLI.md against all **89** `helpLine()` entries in `engine/cli.js` reported 22 "missing" commands — every one a false positive from placeholder spelling (`show lldp neighbors` vs `lldp neighbors`, `interface <parent>.<vlan>` vs a worked example). Worth recording so the next pass does not chase the same 22 ghosts: match by keyword, not by literal string. |
| `README.md` | Linked 13 of the 18 documents, with no index and no way to reach the rest: `TRIAGE.md` and `UPSTREAM-DESIGN.md` were cited **only** from `CHANGELOG.md` and `SIMULATION.md`, so a reader had no path to either | **Built the index**: a `## Documentation` section grouping all eighteen — start here / the physical layer / devices and behaviour / operating and contributing — with a one-line description each, so the two unreachable pages are now one click from the top. The method was a link graph rather than a read-through: extract every `docs/*.md` reference from README, CONTRIBUTING and the wiki pages, and diff it against the directory listing. Also corrected the directory tree's `docs/` comment, which listed 6 of 18 as if that were all. Two claims in my own first draft failed the check: `ROADMAP.md` has no "declined" content (it is organised by area with the gap named in each), and I had described `HYBRID-PATCH.md` before confirming it discusses reflection loss — it does. Verified afterwards that the README now links **18 of 18** with no dangling targets. |
| `CHANGELOG.md` | Opened with `## Unreleased` entries **above** the `# Changelog` title, then had a **second** `## Unreleased` and a third `## [Unreleased]`, and closed with two dead links to the old GitHub repository | **Restructured without touching a word of the entries**: title and intro moved to the top, the three unreleased headings collapsed into one, `## [0.1.0]` renamed to a plain heading. Content-preservation check by multiset diff before writing — the only lines lost are the duplicate headings and the two stale URLs. The links were dead in a way nothing reported, because `scripts/canonical.js` puts `CHANGELOG.md` in `EXEMPT_FILES` on purpose (a changelog records the GitHub era, so it must be able to name it). Checked the replacements rather than assuming: `compare/main...HEAD` answers **200** on git.jayw.uk, while `compare/v0.1.0...HEAD` and `releases/tag/v0.1.0` both **404** — the Forgejo remote has no tags and no releases (`…/tags` and `…/releases` return `[]`) — so the `0.1.0` tag link was removed instead of repointed at nothing. Added the features that shipped with no changelog line, each exercised this cycle: `traceroute`/`tracert`, `show power inline`, `show ip dhcp pool`/`binding`, the built-from-the-table `show ip route` legend, `nslookup <name> [type]` with empty-NOERROR semantics, and the `ipv6 nd` round-trip. Normalised the file back to LF afterwards (writing it from Python had turned the working copy CRLF, which `.gitattributes` would have silently rewritten on the next touch). |
| `README.md` "With Docker" | Told the user to `docker run --rm -p 8000:8000 ghcr.io/jaylouisw/packetforge:latest` | **That image cannot be pulled by anyone**: no workflow in this repository pushes an image (`grep` over `.forgejo/workflows/` finds no registry step), and both the anonymous GHCR token request and `docker manifest inspect ghcr.io/jaylouisw/packetforge:latest` answer **denied**. The section now says plainly that no image is published and gives the path that works — `docker build -t packetforge .` then `docker run --rm -p 8000:8000 packetforge` — **verified end to end on this machine**: build succeeded from a clean context, the container reported `GET /api/version` → `devices: 1584`, `node v20.20.2`, `linux/x64`, and Docker showed `health: starting` from the `HEALTHCHECK` on `/api/catalog`. Image removed afterwards. |
| `docs/CI.md` "Docker build" row | "A `Dockerfile` that does not build. Present as a syntax/build proxy, not a full image test" | **Corrected to what the job actually does**: `check-dockerfile.js` asserts five things a build would have caught early — pinned base image, non-root, `HEALTHCHECK`, every `COPY` source exists, a command to run — and then a **real `docker build` runs only if `docker info` succeeds** in the job. A green Docker job on a daemon-less runner means "well-formed", not "it builds", and the row now says that instead of implying the build was verified. The seven-step list of the test job was already accurate. |
| `CONTRIBUTING.md` / `docs/CI.md` — publishing the wiki | Nothing anywhere named `scripts/publish-wiki.js`, the only path from `docs/wiki/` to the Forgejo wiki | **Documented and measured**: `npm run wiki:publish` clones `packetforge.wiki.git` and pushes; `--dry-run` prints the pages that differ. Ran the dry run — it works, and it reports **all six pages out of date**, so the wiki readers see is behind the docs in this tree. Also recorded the fresh-instance trap the script's own header explains: the wiki repository does not exist until somebody saves one page through the web UI, and until then the clone answers "Repository not found", which reads like a credentials failure and is not one. Nothing was pushed. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §The UI | Claimed `app.js` has "keyboard shortcuts" and stopped there — no binding, no mouse behaviour, nowhere to look but `web/app.js` | **Wrote the manual**: a "Using the editor" table read out of the two UI files — wheel zooms towards the pointer (1.12×/0.89×, clamped 0.25–3), drag background pans, drag a device moves it, port→port cables (the medium/length panel takes over), double-click opens that device's console tab, right-click gives the context menu (*Take link down* / *Bring link up*), `c` arms link mode (ignored while typing), `Esc` cancels placement or blurs, `Delete`/`Backspace` deletes with `preventDefault`, `Enter` commits. Corrected my own first draft: I wrote that the toolbar buttons share the wheel's implementation and that there is a double-click-to-fit — neither is true. There are **two** zoom implementations (`zoomBy()`/`fitView()` and the wheel's inline arithmetic) that currently agree only because all three clamp to 0.25–3, and double-click opens the console. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §The server | One clause: "upgrades connections to WebSocket for live multi-client topology sync". Nothing about the message protocol, and nothing about the board endpoints — a client author had to read `server/index.js` | **Added "Live sync (WebSocket)" and "Board lifecycle"**, both measured against a server started from this tree (`Devices: 1584 models across 245 vendors`): the socket carries exactly one application type, `{type:'topology', topologyId, state}`, sent on connect (always the `default` board), on `{type:'subscribe', topologyId}`, and after every mutation — **to every client, not to that board's subscribers**, so a client must filter on `topologyId`; bogus JSON and unknown `subscribe` ids get no reply. Board endpoints: `POST /api/topologies` takes `{id}` or generates `topo-<base36>`, and **silently ignores `name`** (`{name:'lab2'}` → `201 id=topo-mw9lwojc`), while `DELETE` answers `{ok:true}` for an id that never existed. Two of those facts were nearly reported wrong: an earlier probe hit a **foreign server already listening on port 8123** (it reported `devices: 1717`, not this tree's 1,584), so every claim was re-run against a locally spawned server before it was written down. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §The server | Documented the flows API down to `already: true`, but **nothing about how failures are shaped** — 23 error responses exist in `server/index.js` and only four carry a machine-readable `code` | **Added an "Error responses" section** from reading every `sendJSON(res, 4xx/5xx, …)` call: status classes (400 malformed, 404 missing, 405 wrong method, 409 refused, 422 impossible), the four codes that exist (`E_BAD_REQUEST`, `E_BAD_STATE`, `E_NO_LINK`, `E_LINK` as the 409 fallback), and the two different 409 bodies — a refused cable `{error, code, issues[]}` versus a duplicate device id `{error, id, existing}` with no code at all. Corrected my own first draft twice against the handler: `validate-link` returns `200` with `{verdict, mediaOptions}`, not a flat `{ok, code, message, options}`, and the verdict's fields are `errors[]`/`warnings[]` while a refused `POST /links` says `issues[]` — same engine codes, two envelopes. |
| `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` `show power inline` | Example showed `Budget : 370 W` with `Available : 344.1 W` **and** a port `denied (budget exhausted)` — 344 W of headroom cannot deny anyone; and the page implied `poe-pse` had no role at all | **Corrected against measured runs**: the sample is now a coherent 20 W budget (`Used : 13 W`, `Available : 7.1 W`, one port `on`, one `denied`), with the two things a reader trips over spelled out — `Used` is the rounded total beside per-port figures that keep 12.95, and a `denied` port is normally **unreachable** because cabling an over-budget pair throws at `Topology.connect` (`E_POE_BUDGET`, "2 powered ports draw 40 W of a 30 W budget (Gi0/1, Gi0/2)"). That check sums advertised `poeOut`, not what the PDs request. Also fixed my own overstatement: `poe-pse` *does* matter — it supplies the 802.3af **15.4 W** default when a port declares no `poeOut` (measured; a device without the capability gets `null`), it just plays no part in the budget sum. |
| `docs/CLI.md` §DNS (`nslookup`) | Documented `nslookup` with no mention of its second argument, though `?` advertises `nslookup <name> [type]` | **Found by diffing the runtime help against the page** (66 command entries across exec/config/interface/DHCP-pool, all covered except this): the type argument accepts `A`, `AAAA`, `CNAME`, `MX`, `NS`, `PTR`, `SOA`, `TXT`, `SRV`, refuses anything else with the list (`% Unknown record type "BOGUS" (use …)`), and answers a type the name does not carry with **empty NOERROR, not NXDOMAIN**. Added with the three measured transcripts. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §The engine | Named exactly **two** of the engine's 29 modules (`device.js`, `flow.js`) — a reader asking "where does BGP live?" had nowhere to look but the file tree | **Added a module map**: all 29 files with a one-line job each, taken from each module's own header comment so every row is checkable against source. Two of my own claims were wrong first and were corrected against the code: `optical.js` *does* read `catalogue/optics/` (lazily, so it is the one non-pure module), and "the only file that knows about time" was false — `flow.js` and `nat.js` both document taking caller-supplied logical ticks instead of `Date.now()`, which is the reason a lab replays identically. The map closes with "if a protocol is not in that list it is not in the engine", pointing at the fidelity matrix. Also re-ran `npm run smoke`: all 13 HTTP checks clean, so the API surface the docs describe is the API that answers. |
| `docs/ROADMAP.md` "TCP/UDP" | Unticked: "**TCP/UDP** sockets and a tiny transport layer (ports, SYN/ACK handshake). The line is now ticked, names the suite, and carries the module's own residual list (no congestion window, retransmission timer, SACK, TIME_WAIT, window scaling, or ICMP port-unreachable) plus the still-absent sockets/HTTP/TLS. `SIMULATION.md` §"TCP/IP stack" had already been corrected; the README bullet too — the roadmap was the straggler. |

Two warts written down: `link down` quotes `reconvergeAtMs` as change + 2 × forward delay **even with no bridge in the topology**, so a router-only lab is told 40 s and then `converge` correctly says 10 s — trust `converge`; and the state-change handler emits `state` twice in one object literal, so the reply's `state` is the whole topology rather than `"down"`. |
| `docs/TRIAGE.md` | "**around 800** at the time of writing" open device requests; `--limit`/`--close` described without defaults; lookup described as a single exact-name pass | **Measured instead of guessed**: a public, token-free API call shows **126 open issues, all 126 titled `[Device]: …` and all 126 labelled `device-request`** — so the page was 6× out, and now carries the re-measure one-liner rather than a number to trust. Added the two flags' real defaults (`--limit 200`, `--close 0`, report prints at most 40 matches), the three-attempt lookup (whole title as vendor+model, then model, then a trailing token run longest-first — which is how `MikroTik Cloud Core Router CCR2004-16G-2S+` finds `CCR2004-16G-2S+`), and the facts behind the normalisation guard: **104 of 1,584 models carry a `+`**, `RB5009UPr+S+IN`/`RB5009UPr+S+OUT` are both in the catalogue, and the `++`→`plusplus` branch is defensive because no model uses one. LAG is the mirror image: `static` is the only mode the CLI parses *and* the validator accepts, so **an LACP bundle cannot be built from the CLI**; README now says which side each mode comes from. |

| Every table transcript in `docs/CLI.md`, `docs/CONVERGENCE.md` and `docs/LINK-STATE.md` | Column widths were typed per table, and the transcripts had drifted from the engine the moment a value got wider — a VRRP ready time (`3218.75 ms`) is wider than an STP one (`30000 ms`), and `LINK-STATE.md` showed `10000 ms the link state changed` with the column boundary gone entirely | **Widths are now measured, not typed.** `renderTable(header, rows, indent)` — since moved to `engine/table.js` so `nat.js` can share it — pads every cell to the widest value in its column plus two spaces, and thirteen tables go through it (`links`, `show ip interface brief`, `show vlan`, `show spanning-tree`, `show etherchannel`, `show arp`, `show mac address-table`, `show ip ospf`, `show ip ospf neighbor`, `show ip bgp`, `show vrrp`, `show lldp neighbors`, `show flows`, `show flows counters`, `show dns`, `traceroute`, `converge`). The eight transcripts above were then re-rendered and checked against a live run — `links` and `converge` byte-identical to the console. `test/table-layout.test.js` sweeps fifteen of those tables on a lab built from 29-character hostnames and asserts every row starts its cells at exactly the header's column offsets, so a new table cannot skip the check |
| `show ip nat translations` and `engine/nat.js` | NAT carried its own formatter: five 23-character columns typed into `nat.js`, with the console computing `5 + 23*4` by hand to glue the `Type` label onto the header. Any address wider than the column, or any change to the column count, silently misaligned it — and it was the one table the layout sweep could not reach | **The rows and the layout are now separate concerns, each in one place.** `engine/table.js` holds `renderTable()` (moved out of `cli.js` so `nat.js` can share it); `nat.js` exposes `translationRows()` and builds its own `formatTable()`/`TABLE_HEADER` from `TABLE_COLUMNS` through that renderer, so the columns cannot drift from the rows. `test/nat-show.test.js` asserts the console row and `formatTable()`'s row are character-for-character identical, and that every column name sits over its value. `show ip nat translations` joined the sweep in `test/table-layout.test.js` (17 tables, NAT populated by a TCP flow through a PAT rule — a ping is ICMP and is never translated). An unknown port is refused by name instead of ignored — a silent 'all ports' answer looks like a real one. `test/port-addressing.test.js` (24 assertions) pins it, including the cross-check that a spelling config accepts is a spelling show accepts |
| Destination names, argument validation | `ping <name>` was **refused outright** (`% Invalid ping target: www.lab.test`) while `docs/CLI.md` described the resolver path as "the same one `ping <name>` uses" — a documented promise with no code behind it. `ping x count abc` (also `0`, `-3`) silently sent **four** because a count that failed `parseInt` was dropped. `converge abc` meant "no instant" and `converge -1` meant "1 ms before power-on", so both printed a confident table. And the lab resolver reported `nothing.example` as **NXDOMAIN from <server>**, accusing a server of denying a domain it never held, because a non-authoritative NXDOMAIN counted as "a zone was asked" | **Resolve, then validate.** `_resolveHost()` is one path shared with `nslookup` (own zones, then lab resolvers) and is used by `ping`, `traceroute` and `flow open`; it prints the translation only when a name was given and keeps three failures distinct: answer, **NXDOMAIN** from an authoritative server, and "no server hosts a zone for it". Digits-and-dots is treated as a malformed address, not an unknown host. `sim.resolveName()` now requires `authoritative` before recording that a zone was consulted. Counts and instants are refused by name rather than coerced. `test/target-resolution.test.js` (37 assertions) pins all of it — files touched: `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/target-resolution.test.js` |

Each was a few characters in Markdown, found by hand, twice in two iterations|**`test/docs-syntax.test.js`** reads both sides with the *same* splitter — alternatives (`in \| out`), aliases (`enable / en`), optional groups (`[neighbor]` yields both readings), and a trailing ellipsis — then asserts no phantom command, no invented placeholder, and no undocumented exec verb. Config help gained the `no ip route …` row the description only hinted at, which is what turned the change from doc-editing into a help improvement. Files: `test/docs-syntax.test.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `docs/CLI.md` |
| help vs the parser, and docs transcripts | The `converge [at <ms>]` row was a promise the parser broke, and only a reader who typed it found out. Nothing verified that a help row is typable, and nothing verified that the commands shown in the doc *transcripts* exist — the tables were guarded (docs-syntax) but a prompt line like `SW1#show lldp neighbours` is prose that looks like input | **Two sweeps.** `test/help-forms.test.js` takes every help row, fills its placeholders with values the parser accepts, and requires the *fullest* reading (every optional group present) to avoid a shape complaint — which is exactly what catches `converge [at <ms>]`, verified by re-injecting that defect. `test/docs-commands.test.js` extracts all 82 prompt lines from the fenced blocks in `docs/*.md` and requires each to be recognised in some mode, while treating a refusal on state as a pass so the transcripts that *exist* to show a refusal still pass Files: `test/help-forms.test.js`, `test/docs-commands.test.js` |
| `no access-list <n> <entry>` | Documented as dropping the **whole list** because the engine had no per-entry delete, so `no access-list 101 permit tcp any host 10.0.1.5 eq 80` deleted every other entry and answered `Access list 101 removed` — a request to remove one line silently discarded the rest, and the confirmation named the list rather than the entry | **Per-entry removal implemented** in `CliSession._noAccessList()`. The typed entry is parsed and compared structurally (port and address canonicalised, so `eq 80` matches `eq www` and `10.0.1.6 0.0.0.0` matches `host 10.0.1.6`); a missing entry is refused with the list printed; removing the last entry deletes the list and says so; a bare number still clears everything and now reports the count; a partial removal leaves the interface binding in place Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/acl-remove.test.js` (26 assertions, including a ping that fails while the deny entry exists and succeeds once it is removed) |
| `show ip route` — the mask column, and the missing instant | Two problems in one command. The table printed `C  10.0.1.0       /24`, the mask detached by a hand-typed 15-wide field, so the route was not a token a reader could copy — and the padding was a second copy of the layout that no renderer measured. And the command could only answer "as last computed", so "what routes exist at t=0?" was reachable only through `traceroute … at`, one path at a time | **Prefix attached, width measured, instant accepted.** Rows print `C  10.0.1.0/24  via -  Gi0/0` with the column width taken from the widest destination, so a `/32` widens the table instead of misaligning it. `show ip route at `<ms>`\|converged` rebuilds the control planes first and labels the answer; the plain form keeps its "as last computed" meaning. The rebuild moved into `Simulator.refreshRoutes()`, now shared with `tracePath()`, so a control plane cannot be missing from one door and present in the other Files: `engine/cli.js` (`_show`), `engine/simulator.js` (`refreshRoutes`), `test/route-at.test.js` (37 assertions) |
| Control-plane shows and the mode stack | Two defects with one shape: a state that could only be reached, never asked for. (1) The instant form existed only for `show ip route`, so "what was STP doing at t=0" was a race against convergence, not a query. (2) Worse, `exit` from `(config)#` dropped the session to **user** exec instead of privileged exec: the configuration survived, the privilege did not, and the next `show` said `% Invalid exec command` - which reads as a missing command, not as "you are no longer enabled". It is also the reason a stray `exit` used to turn the following block of config into a wall of refusals. | **One `at` implementation for every control-plane show**, in `CliSession._show()`: the trailing instant is split off, `Simulator.refreshControlPlanes({atMs, lldp, ra})` rebuilds in dependency order (shared with `tracePath()` and `openFlow()`), and the answer is labelled with the instant it describes. Shows whose answer is not a control-plane product refuse the instant by name and list the ones that take it. **The mode stack now remembers where `configure terminal` was entered from**, so `exit`/`end` return there - and privilege is never granted by leaving configuration. The interface-block fall-through now prints a note when it applies a global command, so `ospf area 0` in a port block cannot pass for `ip ospf area 0` **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js` (`_show`, `_splitInstant`, `_instantCapable`, `_modeAfterConfig`, help footer), `engine/simulator.js` (`refreshControlPlanes`), `test/show-at.test.js` (42 assertions), `test/mode-exit.test.js` (15 assertions) |

Then compared each row against the first comment line of its file: every one corresponds, some expanded with detail that also checks out — `nat.js`'s header says only "NAT model" while the row says "static, dynamic and PAT translation", and `NAT_TYPES` really does carry all three; `simulator.js`'s header says "event-driven packet simulation" and the row names the forwarding path and the event queue, both of which `Simulator` exports. Nothing to repair, which is worth recording precisely because it is the exception. **The one thing this iteration did change is the habit that made the check worth doing**: `CI.md` was still printing "134 suites" — accurate when measured last pass, already 135 — so the totals are gone from that bullet entirely in favour of `node scripts/run-tests.js` and `ls test/*.test.js \| wc -l`. A number in prose that moves weekly is a footnote, not a fact. |
| Route selection vs `show ip route` | The table listed the *candidates* for a prefix and said nothing about which one forwards: a static route (distance 1) and an OSPF route (distance 110) for `10.0.99.0/24` were two identical-looking rows, so a reader could believe they had two paths, or a backup, when they had one live route and one shadow. `routeTo()` knew the answer and the command did not say it | **The mark and the reason, from one table of distances.** `Device.ADMIN_DISTANCE` now holds the numbers `routeTo()` breaks ties with (connected 0, static 1, eBGP 20, OSPF 110, iBGP 200, default 254) instead of literals scattered through the method; `routesTable()` attaches the distance to every row and marks the winner per prefix with the same strictly-lower rule, and the console renders `*`, `Prefix`, `AD`, `Gateway`, `Interface` through `renderTable()`. The note explaining `*` appears only when something is actually shadowed.
