| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` "Using the editor" — bindings checked against `web/` | The table says "every binding below is in the code, not in a manual — this table is the manual", and nothing had compared the two | **Read the four UI files and matched every row.** Wheel zoom really is `deltaY < 0 ? 1.12 : 0.89` clamped by `Math.min(3, Math.max(0.25, …))`, and it really zooms towards the pointer — the handler takes `screenToWorld()` before and after and corrects `panX/panY` by the difference. `zoomBy()` carries the identical clamp, so the toolbar and the wheel agree as claimed. `c` arms link mode only when `document.activeElement` is not an input/textarea/contenteditable and no `Ctrl`/`Cmd`/`Alt` is held; `Esc` blurs a field and otherwise clears `placing`/`connecting`; `Delete`/`Backspace` call `preventDefault()` before deleting, exactly as the row says. **Two rows were wrong and are now fixed**: the link-mode hint was quoted with single spaces around the dot where the code emits `Create link: click the first device  ·  Esc to cancel` (two each side), and `Enter` in the console was described as committing "the console prompt" when `#console-prompt` is only the label of a `<form>` — the submit handler on `#console-form` is what fires, and it clears `#console-input` first. Writing that fix also caught me inventing `#length-box` for the cable-length field: the real id is `#link-length`. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` "Error responses" — measured with a live server | The table said `400 E_BAD_REQUEST` was what a cable request without both ids returned, that `405` was the class for any wrong method, and that only four responses carried a code | **One request per row against a server on `PORT=8231` with a scratch `PF_DATA`.** Most of the classes are right; the attribution was not. `400 E_BAD_REQUEST` with `reason: 'expected {a, b} device ids'` belongs to **`POST …/link-options`**, and its body has **no `error` key at all** — `POST …/links` does no shape check, so a request with no `a` falls into the generic refusal handler and answers **`409 { error: "Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'deviceId')", code: 'E_LINK', issues: [] }`**: a JavaScript TypeError handed to the client as the user-facing string. `405` is **one path only** (`…/links/:id/state`, `{ error: 'use POST' }`) — `POST /api/version` is `404 unknown endpoint`, because the dispatcher matches on path presence rather than method. Added the two rows the table had never carried (`422 E_BAD_LENGTH` from `PATCH …/links/:id` with `lengthM: -5`, and the code-less duplicate-id `409 { error, id, existing }`, confirmed with `existing: "Router"` — it is the label, not the id) and kept the measured good news: `validate-link` answers `200` with `{ verdict, mediaOptions }` and every field the page lists, and a refused cable really does come back `409` with `code: E_MEDIA_NOT_SUPPORTED` plus a matching `issues[]` entry. |
| Route lookup — `show ip route <dest>` | The console could print the table and nothing else, so "what would this box do with 10.0.99.5?" had to be inferred from the rows, and the reason the static route won over the OSPF route was invisible. The row order said nothing and the distances were not shown | **A lookup that answers from the data path.** `_routeLookup()` accepts a bare address (longest match), `addr/len`, or `addr mask`, and answers from `routeTo()` — the same call a ping makes — naming the covering prefix, source, distance, next hop and interface, plus a `Shadowed` line for each candidate that lost. Prefix forms print that prefix's candidates with the installed one marked. No route is a refusal (`% No route to …` plus the missing default route). Validation parses rather than shape-checks, because `net.isIp()` accepts `10.0.0.999` and the arithmetic behind it threw `% Error: Invalid IPv4 address` at the console. Found while doing it: help advertises `[at <ms> converged]`, so a bare `converged` or number is a spelling too — it used to be ignored, printing the table as last computed **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js` (`_routeLookup`, `_splitInstant`, `_looksLikeBareInstant`), `test/route-lookup.test.js` (50 assertions), `test/show-at.test.js` (42 assertions) |
| `docs/CONVERGENCE.md` — which object the defaults actually live in | The page said "the defaults are the ones in `engine/convergence.js` (`C`)" — a half-correction from an earlier pass, since `C` is the name inside the file and a reader importing the module sees something else entirely | **Read the export instead of the declaration.** `engine/convergence.js` declares `const C = { … }` at line 28 and ends with `module.exports = { DEFAULTS: C, … }`, so `require('./engine').convergence.DEFAULTS` is the object to read — verified: 9 keys, `stpForwardDelaySec: 15` — while `C` is what a stack trace or a `grep` shows you. The page now states both names and which one is which, because "`C`" alone sends a reader looking for an export that does not exist. **Also spot-checked four `CHANGELOG.md` claims the changelog asserts without proof**, since that file is read as a promise: `show power inline` really does carry the four provenance labels (`spec`, `research`, `model name`, `unknown`) and `denied (budget exhausted)`; an unknown DNS type is refused with the list as claimed (`% Unknown record type "BOGUS" (use A/AAAA/CNAME/MX/NS/PTR/SOA/TXT/SRV)`); and the route legend behaves exactly as the entry describes — measured `Codes: C - connected, S - static` on a converged router with no OSPF row installed, which is the entry's point, though the code is worth knowing: `present` starts life as `{ C: true, S: true }` and only `O`/`B` are conditional, so the legend never drops `S` even when no static route exists. |
| The wiki page for hardware requests — a promise the catalogue does not keep | Step 3 read "the spec lands with the documentation URL recorded inside it", stated as what happens; `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` meanwhile calls `sourceUrl` *recommended* and quotes a count | **Counted the catalogue, then the enforcement.** 1,584 specs, **350 with no `sourceUrl`** — so 1,234 carry one, which is exactly the figure `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` already published (that page was right, the wiki was not). Nothing enforces it either: the suites that touch `sourceUrl` are about *preserving* it (`test/importer-roundtrip.test.js`: "sourceUrl is preserved") or about one researched device (`test/hybrid-cord.test.js`), and no sweep asserts the field exists across the catalogue. Rewritten as what it is: a recommended field, the real count, and the note that the rest are tracked rather than refused. Editing the **template inside `scripts/build-wiki.js`** broke the generator on the first attempt: the surrounding string is a template literal, so an unescaped backtick around `sourceUrl` ended it mid-sentence and `build-wiki.js` died with `SyntaxError: Unexpected identifier`. Escaped, rebuilt, republished over SSH, re-cloned to confirm the live page says it. |
| DHCP lease expiry — "not aged" versus "no caller" | The absence list said only "lease expiry during a run (leases are computed at run start, not aged)", which reads as unimplemented — and `engine/dhcp.js` exports `expireLeases()` | **Grepped for callers rather than reading the export list.** `expireLeases(state, step)` is real and correct — `test/dhcp.test.js` asserts a lease survives at step 699 and is released at 700 — but the only callers in the entire repository are `test/dhcp.test.js` and `test/protocol-regression.test.js`: `Simulator` never invokes it, so a lease in a live lab never expires however far the timeline is pushed. The absence was accurate and the *reason* was missing, which is the difference between a reader concluding "this is not built" and "this is built, tested, and unwired". `SIMULATION.md` §5.1 now says which it is. Also re-checked the rest of the same list while there: HSRP and GLBP really have no hits anywhere in `engine/`, and README still says "eighteen documents" against eighteen files on disk. |
| No saved configuration, in a console that has `show running-config` | `grep` for `startup-config` and `write memory` across `engine/`, `docs/` and the tests returned **nothing**: the concept did not exist. So the most-typed pair after `sh run` was missing entirely, and nothing could answer "what was on this box before I started changing it" | `write memory` (and `copy running-config startup-config`) stores the rendered configuration on the device; `show startup-config` prints it plus a line-level LCS diff against the running configuration, as `!` comments so the dump stays pasteable. One rendering feeds all three (`_runningConfigLines`), because two would drift and the diff would then compare a config to something never printed. The suite proves the dump rebuilds a bare device byte-for-byte, and recomputes the diff **independently** (multiset difference) rather than trusting the engine's count |
| A configuration the console accepted and its own dump could not re-enter | Found by that paste test, not by design: `ip dhcp pool lan` + `network` + `exit` reported `E_DHCP_BAD_RANGE` on exit **and kept the pool anyway**. The pool then appeared in `show running-config` as a block that failed its own validation when pasted back, and `show ip dhcp pool` listed a pool that could allocate nothing | Leaving the pool sub-mode is now transactional: the pool is snapshotted on entry, and on an invalid exit a newly created pool is **discarded** (with the message naming `range`) while an edited existing pool is **restored** to its previous settings. The rule is one rule - a pool exists only if it validates - so the dump, the listing and the allocator cannot disagree about whether a pool is real. Also corrected the `E_DHCP_BAD_RANGE` gloss in the refusal table, which said "only one of rangeStart/rangeEnd was given" when the common case is neither |
| A configuration show that ignored its own argument | `show running-config interface Gi0/0` printed the whole configuration, and so did `show running-config bogus` — a typo answered with a page that looks like an answer. Found by holding the *new* saved-copy command to the port-argument rule the older shows were fixed to: once `show startup-config` narrowed, the two views disagreed about what the same argument means | `_portArg()` now serves both, so a port narrows, an unknown name is refused by name with `show ip interface brief` named, two ports are refused rather than guessed, and `interface` with no port is refused as **truncated** instead of answered with everything. One block extractor (`_configBlock`) picks the `interface <name>` line and the lines indented under it — text matching would take a `description see Gi0/0` or a route that names the port, which the test pins by configuring both |
| A trailing number read as an instant when it was a port name | `show mac address-table interface 22` answered "MAC table at t=22 ms (control planes rebuilt for this answer)" and **dropped the port**: the bare-instant rule took the last token whenever it was digits. Surfaced by the help-forms sweep, which types `interface <port>` with a numeric fill | A trailing number is an instant only when it is the question. `_bareInstantToken()` owns the rule for both callers (`_looksLikeBareInstant` and `_splitInstant`, which had to agree and did not), and refuses the instant reading when the preceding token is a value-taking keyword (`interface`, `last`, `device`, …). `show mac address-table interface 22` is now `% No interface named 22 on SW1`; `show mac address-table 5000` still means t=5000 ms |
| A `?` that only worked with a space in front of it | `show ip?` answered `% Invalid show command: ip?` — the `?` was read as part of the word. On a real console `?` is a key, not a character, and `sh st?` is how it is typed; the spaced form was the exception, not the rule | A trailing `?` is a question whether or not it is attached. Attached, it asks about **the word being typed**, so the answer is the spelling that word completes to (`sh st?` → `startup-config`) rather than the list of what follows it; the stem is matched as an abbreviation, so `conf t?` still reaches `terminal`, and `Then:` reports what follows when everything that fits agrees. A spaced `?` at a word that ends the command now says so instead of printing an empty table, and names the attached form. A `?` that is part of the text is escaped (`description ready\?`), which is the job Ctrl-V does on a real device; a `?` in the middle of free text needs nothing |
| An alias help row parsed as one synopsis | `configure terminal / conf t` came out of the synopsis grammar as **two wrong forms** — `configure terminal t` and `configure conf t` — so completion offered `t` as the word following `terminal`, `conf t` existed as no form at all, and `clear counters [<port>] / clear interface counters` was mangled the same way. The lexer turned `/` into a bar, and a bar in the middle of a row is read as an alternation *within* one synopsis | A row separator is not a token. `aliasSpellings()` splits a synopsis on a **spaced** `/` before the grammar sees it, in both parse paths (`parseForms` and `parseFormsWithDesc`), and leaves an unspaced `/` alone because that is part of a value (`<addr>/<len>`, `10.0.0.1/24`). The parsed tree went from 196 rows with two bogus ones to 194 rows, none of them invented |
| A drop counter charged for something that was not a drop | The counters landed with the rule `iface && dev && /drop\|deny\|block/.test(type)`. A regex over English event names is a guess about meaning, and it guessed wrong twice in one line: **`poe-deny` matched `/deny/`, and the PSE's budget computation emitted exactly that type** - so the port that lost the budget reported `drops : 1 (1 poe-deny)` although nothing had ever crossed it. Meanwhile the drops whose reason already named a port (`egress interface down`, `no source IP on Gi0/0`) carried no `iface` at all, so the port they named was never charged | Two changes, each answering one half. (1) **The budget decision is its own event**, `poe-budget`: at that moment no frame exists, and a type name has to mean one thing. `poe-deny` is kept for a *frame* refused for want of power, which is genuinely a drop on the port that refused it - measured on a PSE with a 25 W budget: the switch port reports `drops 0`, the unpowered device reports 3 with two distinct reasons. (2) **The counter reads a named set** (`FRAME_DROP_TYPES`) instead of a regex, with the excluded types and the reason for each written beside it, and the attribution sites that named a port in prose now set `iface` too. `test/port-drop-attribution.test.js` (27 assertions) does not trust either the set or the engine: it walks the accumulated event log across runs, counts every event that carries a port and means "a frame died here", and requires the counter to match per port - total *and* reasons - while asserting that excluded types contribute no reason at all. Non-vacuous: dropping `poe-deny` from the set fails 2, dropping `drop` fails 6, renaming the budget event back fails 1. Also fixed while reading the output: a sentence-long reason ran off the right edge and took the count with it, so long reasons are now listed underneath, commonest first Files: `engine/simulator.js` (`FRAME_DROP_TYPES`, `_emit`, `poe-budget`, egress attribution), `engine/cli.js` (`dropReasons`), `web/app.js` (`describeEvent`), `test/poe.test.js`, `test/port-drop-attribution.test.js` |
| A promise with no counters behind it | Last iteration's refusal for `show interfaces counters` ended with "per-port counters and status are in `show interfaces <port>`", and that command printed a name, a state, an address and a peer - **no counters**. An operator sent to a command that cannot answer concludes the lab never carried traffic, which is worse than "not a command here" | The counters are real and the refused command exists. Counted where the frame moved, so the numbers are checkable against the event log port by port; the test asserts counter equality with the log's frames rather than with my expectations, and the byte arithmetic is stated exactly (64 LLDP + 64 ARP + 134 per echo = 530 for a three-ping run) because a byte counter nobody can reproduce is a byte counter nobody can trust. Two bugs of my own caught by running: a flattened `dev/iface` map key could not be split back apart (port names contain slashes), so `show interfaces counters` reported an empty table for every device while `show interfaces Gi0/0` happily showed its counters; and the first reset-clears choice made every reading zero, because the console's own command started the run. Also: `output: 1 packets` was fixed to `1 packet` - a counter is prose, and the small wrongness makes the number look wrong too Files: `engine/simulator.js` (`_portStat`, `portStats`, `frameBytes`, reset), `engine/cli.js` (`show interfaces`, `show interfaces counters`, `clear counters`, `plural`, `dropReasons`), `test/port-counters.test.js` (38 assertions) |
| An SVI was up because it existed | The SVI landed last pass with `lineUp` unmodelled: `Vlan10` answered on a VLAN whose every port was shut, which is the exact situation an operator reads that line to diagnose. The absence was recorded honestly in §2.2 and then fixed rather than left as a note | **`computeSviState()`**: `lineUp` is true only while at least one *bridged* port can carry the VLAN - cabled, not shut, link not down, and a member (access membership or a trunk that allows it). It is called from `refreshControlPlanes()` **and** from `_show()`, because configuration can change between two questions and a cached value would answer about a VLAN that no longer has a live port. Four readers, one field: `ownsIpOn()` (a down SVI owns nothing), `routeTo()` and `routesTable()` (no line protocol, no connected route, so the table and the data path agree), and the two show commands. Two refusals to let "down" mean one thing: the reason string separates *no active port in VLAN 10* from *no port is cabled*, and my first version got the second wrong on a merely-shut switch — it asked `bridgedInterfaces()` (which excludes shut ports) whether anything was cabled, so every shut-port case blamed the cabling. The reason is now computed from all non-SVI interfaces. Also: `show ip interface brief` gained the distinction its two columns exist for - **Status** is the administrator's state, **Protocol** is the line - and `show interfaces` prints `line protocol: down (<reason>)` for a logical port instead of a `peer` it can never have Files: `engine/simulator.js` (`computeSviState`), `engine/device.js` (`ownsIpOn`, `routeTo`, `routesTable`), `engine/cli.js` (`_show`, brief, interfaces), `test/svi-line.test.js` (33 assertions) |
| A switch could not hold a management address | `interface vlan 10` answered `% Interface not found: vlan`, so a 28-port switch's `show ip interface brief` was 28 rows of `unassigned` and `ping` from a switch could not be attempted at all. The cause was not a missing rule: `ownsIpOn()` in `engine/device.js` carried the sentence *"a switch's management address belongs to a VLAN, not to a socket, so any port in that same access VLAN can reach it"* — a rule written for an object nothing could create. Two labs I built in earlier passes worked around this without noticing what I was working around | **`interface vlan <id>`** creates `Vlan<id>` (`addSvi`), a logical port with its own MAC from the device's block, access membership of its VLAN, and normal admin state. Four things had to be true, and each was found by probing rather than reasoning: (1) the frame must *reach* the stack — `canTerminate` was `l3\|isHost()`, so a switch bridged the packet past its own address; `hasSvi()` joins it, because owning an address is a host function and forwarding is not. (2) it must never be a **bridge egress** — `bridgedInterfaces()` excludes it, or the switch would transmit out of its own management identity. (3) `interface Vlan10` must also *create* the object, or `show running-config` cannot be pasted, since the dump names the SVI by interface name. (4) the dump must not emit `switchport access vlan 10` on it — an SVI is in its VLAN by definition, not by configuration. One mistake worth recording: I first made the ARP/echo reply leave by the ingress port, which *looked* right because the ping worked; `_sendFromOwnIp()` already bridges an L2 device's own frames into the VLAN, so the special case replaced a bridge with a unicast and the injection proved it — removing it changed nothing. The MAC needed no rule either: `macForIp()` resolves an address to its owner, so a reply for `Vlan10`'s address already carried `Vlan10`'s MAC. Also fixed: a 48-bit MAC does not fit the 32 bits `& ~0xff` silently truncates to, so the block computation handed every SVI `00:00:00:00:00:81` Files: `engine/device.js` (`addSvi`, `_sviMac`, `hasSvi`, `bridgedInterfaces`), `engine/cli.js` (`_enterInterface`, running-config), `engine/simulator.js` (`canTerminate`, `_forwardL2`), `test/svi.test.js` (49 assertions) |
| Five show commands accepted a port and printed everything | `show ip interface brief 1/1/3`, `show spanning-tree 1/1/3`, `show lldp neighbors 1/1/3`, `show arp 1/1/3` and `show mac address-table 1/1/3` all took the argument and answered with the full 28-port table. `show interfaces ether1` had the same defect and was fixed in an earlier pass; these five were added afterwards and inherited it. Worse than a refusal, because the answer looks like an answer | **One helper, `_portArg(args, dev, what)`, decides for all of them**: resolve the name through `iface-name.js`, refuse an unknown one by name with the port quoted and `show ip interface brief` named, refuse two ports rather than guessing, and otherwise filter. Filtered answers carry a heading that names the port (`Mac address table on 1/1/3 at SW1:`) so a narrowed answer cannot be mistaken for a full one, and an empty result says `(none - nothing has been learned on this port)` instead of going blank. Found alongside it: `show ipv6 interface sfp-sfpplus1` **refused** a spelling the config parser and two other show commands accept, because the IPv6 listing filtered module bays *before* matching the name it was given - a listing rule applied as an answering rule. Also: `show interfaces counters` answered `% No interface named counters`, sending the operator to hunt for a port by that name; it then said the command did not exist and named where counters were — and since that pointer was to a command with no counters in it, the command now exists (see the counters row above). And `ip address 10.0.0.1/24` was refused with `% Incomplete command` while `ipv6 address 2001:db8::1/64` was accepted and every table in the console *prints* the slash form - the same one-resolver-two-opinions asymmetry, now accepted, with a bare address refused by name (`needs a mask`) instead of by silence Files: `engine/cli.js` (`_portArg`, the five show handlers, `_showIpv6Interface` bay rule, `ip address`), `test/port-args.test.js` (83 assertions) |
| `?` was an error, not a question | `?` worked only on its own; every other position treated it as a bad argument (`sh ?` → `% Unrecognized command: sh ?`, `show ip route ?` → `% Invalid destination: ?`). After abbreviation landed this was actively incoherent: `sh ip int br` worked, `sh ip ?` did not. IOS answers a trailing `?` with the completions for what you have typed, which is the whole reason it is worth typing | **`?` prints the completions for the words so far**, from the same parsed help tree abbreviation uses (`_complete`, `_helpFormRows`). Four rules earned by probing: a label several commands lead through says `more options` instead of borrowing one child's description (`ip` was publishing "address and link state per port" for a six-child subtree); an **exact keyword closes the position**, so `show ip` no longer also means `show ipv6` - which had leaked `nd` into `show ip ?` and given `<dest>` two meanings; when every completion belongs to one command the description is printed **once** with a `Next:` line rather than repeated per ending; and `do sh ip ?` works because `do` is dropped before matching the exec tree. Completion answers rather than acts - nothing is inserted and no packet moves. Two defects were in the **help text**, found because completion reads its shape: `ip ospf cost <n> \| area <id> \| passive` used a spaced bar for a shared prefix (so `area` and `passive` became commands of their own), and sixteen interface/config rows had no description at all, which `?` rendered as a blank Meaning column. The rule is now stated and enforced: a tight bar joins alternatives sharing a prefix, a spaced bar separates complete commands Files: `engine/cli.js` (`_complete`, `_completionMode`, `_helpFormRows`), `engine/abbrev.js` (`parseFormsWithDesc`), help rows, `test/completion.test.js` (34 assertions) |
| Abbreviation — `sh ip int br` was refused | The console accepted exactly the words in its parser and a pair of hand-coded aliases (`conf t`, `en`), so the single most-typed line in networking answered `% Unrecognized command: sh ip int br`. IOS has accepted unambiguous keyword prefixes for forty years; a hand-kept alias list can never be complete, and every new command would have needed one more alias | **One rule, applied before dispatch in every mode** (`engine/abbrev.js`): a prefix matching exactly one continuation expands, several are refused by name, none leaves the line untouched. The command tree is **parsed out of `help`** rather than kept in a second list, so a command is abbreviatable exactly when it is documented and the docs/help guards keep the two in step. Values are never abbreviated (a device named `links`, `description route to core`), a keyword beats a value slot (`show ip route summ` → `summary`), and an exact match beats a longer keyword (`int` stays the alias). Four defects found by running, not reading: the synopsis was cut at the help *padding column*, so `show ip route [<dest>\|…\|summary]` parsed as `show ip route os` and `sh ip route summ` answered "invalid destination"; a bracketed group was first read as one opaque value slot for the same reason; splitting the top level on `\|` lost the shared tail, turning `link up\|down <id> [at <ms>]` into a bare `link up`; and the help output carries two different meanings in the *spacing* of the bar — tight (`ipv6 nd ra enable\|disable`, shared prefix) versus spaced (`interface <name> \| int <name>`, two commands) — which the lexer now keeps. `do sh ip int br` works because `do` defers to the exec tree rather than expanding only its first word Files: `engine/abbrev.js`, `engine/cli.js` (`_expandAbbrev`, `_helpForms`, `run`), `test/abbrev.test.js` (47 assertions) |
| The event stream — thirty event types, no reader | `Simulator._emit()` records every decision: 25 separate `drop` sites, `forward`, `acl-deny`, `stp-block`, `vlan-drop`, `poe-deny`, `dhcp`, OSPF and BGP state changes, `scion-hop`, `slaac-refused`. Grepping `engine/cli.js` for `events` returned **nothing** — no command read them, so the stream was reachable only from a test. The cost was concrete: `ping` to a denied address printed `Success rate is 0 percent (0/1)` and stopped there, while the reason sat in the log (`acl-deny`, R1, ACL 101, inbound Gi0/0). An operator could see *that* something failed and never *why* | **`show logging [<type>\|summary] [last <n>] [device <name>]`**, plus **`show clock`** and **`clear logging`**. Ordered and numbered (a gap in the `#` column is visible rather than smoothed over), attributed to devices by name, with a `frame` naming *both* ends instead of a dash, and a per-type/per-device summary whose counts reconcile to the total — `test/logging.test.js` asserts both sums, because a summary that does not add up is worse than none. `show clock` states plainly that there is no wall clock and reports which instant `at` currently pins, which was previously silent and therefore easy to leave stuck. Two design points: the log is lab-wide (one event stream, like `links` and `show flows`), and a filter matching nothing names the types that exist rather than printing an empty table. Three of my own defects surfaced while building it — `frame` rows disappeared from my *probe* because the prompt filter ate anything containing `->` (the arrow's `>` looked like a prompt; the new suite drives `CliSession` and reads `.output` instead of filtering, which removes the class), the `acl`/`dir` fields were missing from the rendered detail (an `acl-deny` without the list number is not actionable), and the summary listed link-level rows as pseudo-devices named `PC1 -> R1`. The `help-coverage` guard then caught a real omission: `clear` was accepted but absent from help, and `help-forms` caught a misleading message — `% Invalid exec command: clear logging` for what was really a privilege refusal, now `% clear logging needs enable mode` Files: `engine/cli.js` (`_showLogging`, `_loggingSummary`, `_showClock`, `describeEvent`, `clear logging`), `test/logging.test.js` (53 assertions) |
| DHCP relay — a broadcast cannot cross a router | `docs/SIMULATION.md` listed DHCP relay as absent, framed as a deliberate limit ("by design rather than by accident"). It was not a design decision so much as a missing feature with a real cost: a central server could never serve a remote subnet, so the commonest enterprise DHCP layout was unbuildable, and the console answered `no-server` — a failure that reads like a misconfigured client | **`ip helper-address <addr>`** in interface mode, plus the relaying in `Simulator._dhcpRelayFor()`/`_runDhcpExchange()`. The router on the client's wire unicasts to the helper and stamps its own port address into **giaddr**; the server selects its pool by giaddr rather than by its own interface, which is the difference between handing out an address the client can use and one on the wrong side of the router. `show running-config` emits the helper so a pasted dump keeps the relay (a dump that forgets it is worse than no dump), `no ip helper-address [<addr>]` refuses an address that is not set rather than clearing the port, and the console gained a third view: a relay now answers `show ip dhcp binding` with `DHCP relay on R1: Gi0/0 -> 10.0.2.2` instead of the true-and-useless "not a DHCP server (no pools)". Both ends report provenance — the client's lease says `Relayed via Gi0/0 on R1 (giaddr 10.0.1.1)`, the server's table says `1 binding arrived through a relay` Files: `engine/cli.js` (`_helperAddress`, `_noHelperAddress`, `_showDhcp`), `engine/simulator.js` (`_dhcpRelayFor`, `_runDhcpExchange`, `dhcpRelayedBindings`), `test/dhcp-relay.test.js` (36 assertions) |
| DHCP lease expiry — the function nobody called, and the clock that did not exist | `engine/dhcp.js` exports `expireLeases()`; `test/dhcp.test.js` and `test/protocol-regression.test.js` assert it releases a lease exactly at its deadline; **`Simulator` never called it**. So a lease in a live lab never aged, `show ip dhcp binding` printed a constant "Expires in 60s", and the lease timer was decoration. Four defects were stacked on one cause — DHCP had no clock | **`Simulator._nowStep()`** is now the one conversion (asked instant in seconds, 0 at rest) and `computeDhcp()` expires server bindings on every refresh. Three more things had to change for that to mean anything: (1) leases were stamped with `this.step`, the *frame-hop* counter, so `at 120000` measured nothing and a fresh lab reported 62 s on a 60-second pool — measured, then fixed by the clock above; (2) every refresh re-ran a full DISCOVER for a client that already held a lease, so the same host was handed two different addresses (`10.0.1.100`, then `10.0.1.101`) — a client now renews the address it holds (REQUEST, no DISCOVER) and only past T1; (3) `reset()` rebuilt `_dhcpStates` on every ping, so a binding vanished the moment a packet was sent and every run restarted the timer — bindings are server state and now persist and age, which is safe precisely because of (2). Console: `Expires in` is a countdown at the asked instant; the client view gained a deadline beside its length; and the empty table now distinguishes `no client has completed an exchange` from `N leases expired at this instant`, with the count recorded against the step it happened at so re-asking the same instant does not lose the explanation — `engine/simulator.js` (`_nowStep`, `computeDhcp`, `_runDhcpExchange`, `reset`), `engine/dhcp.js` (`leaseTable`), `engine/cli.js` (`_showDhcp`, `_dhcpExpiredAtThisInstant`), `test/dhcp-lease-clock.test.js` (56 assertions) |
| IPv6 addressing per port — the question with no command | `show ip interface brief` is IPv4, and `show ipv6 nd` reports policy rather than state, so "which global address is on this port, and did a human type it or did a router advertise it?" had no console answer at all — it could only be inferred from `show running-config` plus the knowledge that a *missing* `ipv6 address` line means SLAAC. The provenance is what an operator needs: an autoconfigured address disappears when the advertisements stop, which is not the same situation as a configured one | **`show ipv6 interface [<port>] [at <ms>\|converged]`**, one row per address with the link-local first and a `Source` column carrying `configured` or `autoconfig from <router link-local>`. A shut port is `administratively down` in the same words the IPv4 shows use, and claims no address. An unknown port is refused by name rather than printing every port — the silent-widening defect this console has fixed twice. A host that has not yet heard an advertisement says `no global address` **and names the escape hatch**: the router-advertisement pass runs at run start and whenever a question is asked at an instant, so a plain show is the state as last computed, and without that second line the host looks broken. Three findings while building it: (1) a router advertises a prefix only on an interface that has an address, so a lab with `ipv6 nd prefix` but no address on the port proves nothing about SLAAC — the test lab had exactly that bug; (2) my first draft read a field called `lastRunInstantMs` that does not exist; the run's instant is `runAtMs`, `Infinity` meaning none; (3) an injection aimed at the port filter hit the **transceiver** filter instead, because both methods contain the same line and `String.replace` takes the first — the injection passed until it was re-aimed with surrounding context, which is the argument for injecting into the specific method rather than the file — `engine/cli.js` (`_showIpv6Interface`), `test/ipv6-interface.test.js` (61 assertions) |
| IPv6 route table — and the two selection bugs it exposed | `ipv6 route` and `ipv6 gateway` were configurable, `show running-config` printed them, and `route6To()` routed with them — but there was no way to see the table. Adding one exposed that `route6To()` was a second, hand-written copy of the rule IPv4 keeps in `routeTo()`: (1) the connected loop had no comparison at all, so two matching prefixes routed by whichever interface came last in iteration order rather than the longest prefix — traffic for a directly attached /64 left the wrong port; (2) a static route beat a connected subnet of the same length, because the static loop compared prefix lengths and never the distance — traffic that should have been delivered locally went to a next hop that might not exist | **`show ipv6 route [<dest>] [at <ms>\|converged]`**, plus **one comparison rule for both families**: `Device.betterRoute(candidate, best)` (longest prefix, then lower distance) is now used by `routeTo()` and `route6To()`, and `routes6Table()` marks the installed row with the same rule, so the table cannot contradict the data path. The command answers the whole table (with a `*` installed mark and an explaining line only when something is shadowed), a longest-match lookup with the losing candidates named, and one prefix's rows. It composes with the `at` instant (`IPv6 routes at t=0 ms`), because a SLAAC host has a route only after it has heard a router advertisement. Two defects found while building it, both caught by running rather than reading: `parsePrefix6` reports `prefixLen`, and reading `prefixLength` printed `/undefined` and matched nothing — which looked like an empty table; and the new prefix-length guard fired on plain addresses too, refusing `show ipv6 route 2001:db8:1::5` with an internal error. The `help-forms` sweep then caught a third: its placeholder filler switched to IPv6 samples for a row containing `ipv6` but only for `addr`/`prefix`/`next-hop`, so `<dest>` was filled with `10.0.0.0` and the command refused it for the right reason — the guard reported a broken form that was never broken. The filler now has IPv6 samples for `dest`/`dst`/`net`/`subnet` too — `engine/device.js` (`betterRoute`, `route6To`, `routes6Table`), `engine/cli.js` (`_showIpv6Route`, `_routeLookup6`), `test/ipv6-route.test.js` (45 assertions) |
| Route table — per-protocol filter and summary | The table was all-or-nothing: finding the OSPF rows meant reading every row, and nothing said how much of the table was shadowed — the distinction the administrative-distance work introduced was visible per row but not in aggregate | **`show ip route <protocol>` and `show ip route summary`.** Selectors (`connected`, `static`, `ospf`, `bgp`) filter `routesTable()` and report `N routes, M installed`; `summary` counts rows and installed routes per protocol, totals them and calls out the shadowed count. Both compose with the `at` instant, both render through `renderTable()`, and an absent protocol is a refusal naming the codes that are present rather than an empty table. The selector map lives beside the class as data (`LOAD_ROUTE_FILTERS`), so a filter cannot drift from the legend codes — `engine/cli.js` (`_routeFiltered`, `_routeSummary`, `LOAD_ROUTE_FILTERS`), `test/route-filter.test.js` (44 assertions) |
| Test-suite counts quoted in prose, and suites quoted that do not exist | Eight documents name a specific suite with an assertion count (`test/show-at.test.js` (37 assertions) and so on), and nothing had compared a single one against a run; nobody had asked whether the files named exist at all | **Ran all eight and compared.** Six were exact (`mode-exit` 15, `port-addressing` 24, `route-filter` 44, `route-installed` 45, `route-lookup` 50, `target-resolution` 37) and two had drifted: `route-at` is **37**, not 28, and one citation still called `show-at` **37** while another in the same table already said 42 — so the page contradicted itself rather than merely being old. Both corrected. Then swept every `test/*.test.js` named anywhere in the prose against `test/`: five citations point at files that do not exist, and all five are **proposals** (`test/bgp-policy.test.js` and `test/bgp-policy-wire.test.js` in `BGP-POLICY.md`, `test/upstream-baseline.test.js` and `test/upstream-service.test.js` in `UPSTREAM-DESIGN.md`), each written as "New file:" or "Add …" — legitimate, but indistinguishable from rot by grep alone, which is why `md-check.js` in `CI.md` now prints them instead of guessing. |
| `npm test` — described in three documents as three different things | README called it "engine + CLI tests", `CONNECTORS.md` called it "Everything, in the order CI runs it", and `CI.md` called it "every suite, the same entry point CI uses" — three descriptions of one script, and the script is none of them exactly | **Read `package.json` and the workflow, then ran both audits.** `"test"` is `node scripts/audit-port-types.js && node scripts/check-control-bytes.js && node scripts/run-tests.js` — two audits chained ahead of the suites, so **a red audit aborts before any suite prints** and the failure has the shape of a hang rather than a red test. That is the one operational fact none of the three pages carried, and it is now in `CI.md` with the advice to run `npm run audit:ports` alone when no `suite(s) in …` line appears. README now says "the whole suite (see docs/CI.md — it is three commands)" and CONNECTORS names the three. Also checked the ordering claim against `.forgejo/workflows/ci.yml`: CI runs syntax → control bytes → node surface → canonical URLs → `npm test` → examples → missing-devices → smoke → dockerfile, so `npm test` is step five of nine, not "the order CI runs it" — and `check-control-bytes.js` therefore executes **twice per push**, harmlessly. Both audits pass on this tree (exit 0, exit 0), so the abort path is a syntax guarantee rather than something I could observe here. |
| `docs/CI.md` — the workflow table, and what "`.github/workflows/` was deleted" leaves behind | The page opened with "`.github/workflows/` was deleted in #1059", which reads as the GitHub directory being gone; it is not, and a reader who greps `.github` and finds eight files has found a contradiction rather than a bug | **Compared the page row by row with `.forgejo/workflows/` and re-ran the two red suites.** Everything else held: `ci.yml` triggers on pull request, push to `main` and dispatch; five checks (the 18/20/22 matrix, smoke, docker) and the test job's seven steps in the documented order; `auto-rebase.yml` fires on push with the schedule as the documented safety net; the smoke job is Node 20 with a 10-minute bound. The known-gaps section is also still true — re-measured today: `spec-vs-description` fails 1 assertion on the same four Ubiquiti boxes, `canonical-urls` fails 3 (`no file points at the old repository` plus the two cleanup assertions), so "4 failing assertions in two files" is exact. What the page did not say is that **`.github/` survives** with `ISSUE_TEMPLATE/` (including the `device_request.yml` the wiki links to), `labels.yml`, `dependabot.yml` and `PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md` — only `workflows/` went. Now stated, together with the consequence that matters for the queue: a stale PR head that predates #1059 still carries a `workflows/` directory this runner cannot satisfy. |
| `show ipv6 route` — a documented absence that the engine had already filled | `CLI.md` said plainly: "`show ipv6 route` and `show ipv6 interface` do not exist; `show ipv6 nd` is the whole IPv6 surface", and printed the refusal `% Invalid show command: ipv6 route` as evidence | **The docs-syntax suite caught it before I did** — `no exec command is undocumented - show ipv6 route` went red on a run that was green the iteration before, because the command landed under the page while the page still denied it. Built a two-router v6 lab and captured the real thing: the table prints `Codes: C - connected, S - static`, an `*` installed marker, `Prefix / AD / Gateway / Interface`, and **a static v6 route shows `-` under `Interface` even with a directly connected next hop** — the hop is resolved by `route6To()` at forwarding time rather than stored, so the blank cell is the truth, not a gap. `<dest>` answers a four-line block (`Destination / Matched … (static, AD 1) / Next hop / Interface`); an unreachable destination is two refusals and the second names the missing default (`% No IPv6 default gateway is configured on this device`); a malformed one is a parse error (`% Invalid IPv6 destination "zzz": illegal character in IPv6 address: zzz`); `at 0` prefixes `IPv6 routes at t=0 ms (control planes rebuilt for this answer)`. Wrote the section, then **my first table of forms broke the suite in a new way** — I had invented `<prefix>/<len>` and repeated `at <ms>\|converged`, and the test checks documented placeholders against the help row, so it went red with `show ipv6 route <prefix>/<len> \| show ipv6 route at <ms>\|converged`. Rewritten to the three forms help actually declares (`[<dest>] [at <ms>\|converged]`), and the stale sentence now points at `show ipv6 interface` alone being absent. |
| `docs/CONVERGENCE.md` — the readiness numbers had no arithmetic behind them | The page listed the defaults (STP 15 s, OSPF hello 10, exchange 1, SPF 5, VRRP advertise 1, DHCP 1) and then showed output like `vrrp R1 3609.375 ms  3 x advertise 1 s + skew` without ever saying how 3609.375 was arrived at — a page about convergence where the central number is unexplained | **Ran `converge` on four labs and wrote the formulas down.** STP `2 × forward delay` = `30000 ms`; OSPF `hello + exchange + SPF` = `16000 ms`; DHCP the exchange alone = `1000 ms`; VRRP `3 × advertise + (256 − priority)/256` = **`3609.375 ms`** at priority 100, confirmed at two more priorities — `3218.75 ms` at 200 and a clean `3000 ms` at 255, the owner case the RFC zeroes. **The gap that fell out of writing it**: `ospfDeadSec` (40 s) is in `DEFAULTS`, is read into `ospfTimers()`, and is validated (`deadSec` must be ≥ `helloSec`) — but it never reaches the schedule, whose only terms are hello, exchange and SPF. So no adjacency expires on its own, and the defaults table was missing the row entirely while the "can you change it?" table already listed it. Added both the row and a formulas section, so the number in the output can be checked rather than admired. |
| `docs/HYBRID-PATCH.md` §6.1 — the double count, described but never shown | The section explains that `ponBudget()` charges `connectorPairs × CONNECTOR_LOSS_DB` and then adds `patchCordLossDb` on top, which double-counts the cord's two mated pairs — and then stops, leaving a reader to trust the arithmetic | **Ran `ponBudget()` twice on the same path** (1 km, one 1:4 splitter, class B+): `lossDb 8.42, headroomDb 19.58, breakdown.connectors {pairs: 2, lossDb: 0.6}, breakdown.patchCord {lossDb: 0}`; with a 0.3 dB cord declared, `lossDb 8.72, headroomDb 19.28` — exactly `+0.3`, with `pairs` **still 2**. So the doc's claim is right, and the return value contains the evidence while none of it is a warning: the arithmetic is the warning. Pasted both objects into §6.1 with the consequence spelled out. Also re-checked the citation while there — `engine/optical.js:105-134` still lands on `connectorPairs` (105) and `patchCordDb` (132), which is the rare case of a line citation surviving; and confirmed field names by measurement rather than by reading, after `totalDb`/`marginDb` (my guesses) came back `undefined` and the real keys turned out to be `lossDb`/`headroomDb`/`breakdown`. |
| `docs/ROADMAP.md` — two ticked boxes that understated and overstated | The NAT box read "static and PAT" while `NAT_TYPES` carries three; the CI box read "Forgejo Actions on **every branch** and pull request" while the workflow triggers on pull requests to `main`, pushes to `main` and dispatch only | **Measured both.** `require('engine/nat.js').NAT_TYPES` is `{STATIC, DYNAMIC, PAT}`, and a dynamic rule was already shown translating a pool address in the earlier pass — so "static and PAT" was missing a working type. The trigger list came straight out of `.forgejo/workflows/ci.yml` (`push: branches: [main]`, `pull_request: branches: [main]`, `workflow_dispatch`) — a feature branch runs nothing until it becomes a pull request, and the dispatch entry exists precisely because an API merge never arrives as a push event. Rewriting the NAT line also caught a claim I had just invented: I wrote that the console "cannot show what the table holds beyond `show ip nat translations`", then found `show ip nat statistics` in the help table and measured it (`rules 1 (pat 1)`, `translations 0`, `dynamic entries 0`, `hits 0`). Both shows are now named. |
| `README.md` — NAT types again, and a palette count with no explanation | The Protocols row still said "NAT (static + PAT)" after the same error was fixed in `ROADMAP.md`, and the Vendors row said "25 `category` values rendered as 24 palette buckets" without saying which category falls outside or what happens to it | **Measured the catalogue and the UI constants.** Categories by count: 25, with `ap` at exactly **one** member (`huawei/WiFi Extender AX3000`). `CAT_ORDER` and `CAT_LABELS` in `web/app.js` carry 24 entries, `BADGE_LABELS` carries 23 — `tap` and `ap` are missing from the badge map, and `ap` is missing from `CAT_ORDER` entirely. Consequence, now stated: that one device **cannot be reached through the type filter** (the `<select>` is built by iterating `CAT_ORDER`) and its card shows the raw string `ap` where every other device shows a label; it is still findable by search and still draws with the wireless icon. **And writing it exposed a hole in my own sweeper**: my replacement cell wrapped across seven lines, which is not a table row at all — the shape check passed because the counts agreed, while GitHub would have rendered the wrap as prose and dropped the row. Added a fourth check (`every table must be followed by a blank line`), watched it fail on an injected sample, then confirmed all 20 documents are clean. It is published in `CI.md` with the rest. |
| My own sweeper had never run its first check | The `md-check.js` table-shape check was reported as "clean" after every documentation pass for many iterations, and was quoted in `CI.md` as the thing keeping table columns honest | **The separator test was `/^\|[\s:-\|]+\|$/`, and `[:-\|]` is a range from `:` to `\|` — `-` (0x2D) is not in it, so no separator line ever matched and the loop `continue`d on every table in the repository.** Check 1 had inspected zero rows. Moving the hyphen to the front (`[-:\s\|]`) found **seven real violations immediately**: `ARCHITECTURE.md` `POST\|PATCH` in a 4-column row, two `CLI.md` rows (`at <ms>\|converged`, `active\|passive\|on`), three iteration-log rows of my own that carried a fourth cell in a three-column table, and a fourth that only needed the escape. Repaired all seven — four by escaping the pipe inside the code span, three by folding the extra cell in — then proved the check fails on a deliberately broken table before believing the green line. The corrected script is published in `CI.md` with a warning about the range bug, because a checker that cannot fail is worse than no checker: it converts "I have not looked" into "this is clean". |
| The other three checks in my sweeper, which I had never made fail | After the separator-regex bug, the honest follow-up question was whether checks 2, 3 and 4 had the same defect — the anchor resolver, the suite-citation resolver and the new wrapped-row test had all only ever printed zero | **Probed all four with injected failures in one throwaway file**: a row with an extra cell, a link to a heading that does not exist, a citation of a suite that does not exist, and a table followed by a prose line. All four fired (`1 shape problem(s), 1 broken anchor(s), 1 cited-but-absent suite(s), 1 broken table(s)`) — so the green lines after the regex fix mean something. Then closed the gap they shared: **same-file `#anchor` links were checked, `OTHER.md#anchor` links were not**, and the cross-file kind is exactly what a heading rename breaks. New check 5 resolves each link relative to the linking file and requires the heading there. It finds six such links in the tree and all six resolve; proved it bites by pointing a probe at a heading that does not exist. |
| Refusal-code coverage, and the assertion that caught the current red build | `CLI.md` opened the appendix with "the engine defines 145 distinct `E_*` codes" and said nothing about how many of them its own tables describe; nothing documented why the hardware line-rate assertion kept missing 10G-on-2.5G-cage faults | **Measured both.** The 145 holds exactly — distinct quoted `E_` strings across `engine/` — but only **47** appear as table rows and **69** are named anywhere in `CLI.md`; the appendix now says so, with the one-line recount published. Then read the assertion behind the current red build: it compares each port's fastest `speedCaps` against the cage ceiling, and it used to be a **no-op for any spec that wrote its caps as text**, because `Math.max.apply(null, ["10G"])` is `NaN` and every comparison against `NaN` is false — how an EdgePoint S16 carried 10G on a 1G cage. New `CONNECTORS.md` §8.4 documents the trap, the console-port exemption history (163 ports on a 115.2 kbps cage), and the seven-device case behind the latest fix: the fault was the connector, `sfp` where `sfp+` was needed, since `CAGE_SPEEDS.sfp` is `[1000, 2500]`. Same lesson as my own broken regex: **a check that has never failed is a check you have not tested**. |
| `docs/CLI.md` "Which shows need a run first", and the same claim in four other places | The page told the reader that `show ip route`, `show ip bgp`, `show vrrp`, `show lldp` and `show spanning-tree` answer with a **placeholder** until something else — a `ping`, a `traceroute`, an `at` question — happens to run the control plane, and printed `(no neighbours have been evaluated yet - run a simulation)` and `0 of 0 neighbour(s) established` as the honest reply to a configured lab. The engine had since been changed so a reader derives the planes it displays, which made every one of those statements false: measured on one lab (two routers with OSPF and BGP, no ping), `show ip route` prints `O` and `B` rows with installed markers, `show ip bgp` prints `Established`, `show vrrp` prints `Master` and its virtual MAC, and `show lldp neighbors` lists the switch. A plain `show ip route` is now byte-identical to `show ip route at converged`, and `at 0` is the only form that shows `C` and `S` alone | **Rewrote the section as two kinds of fact**, measured rather than assumed. *Control-plane products are derived before printing* — `show ip route`, `show ipv6 route`, `show ip ospf [neighbor]`, `show ip bgp`, `show vrrp`, `show ip dhcp binding\|pool`, `show spanning-tree`, `show lldp neighbors`, `show ipv6 interface` — with a transcript of the route table, the BGP neighbour table and the VRRP group taken on a lab that had never been pinged. *Data-path products need frames* — `show arp`, `show interfaces counters`, `show ip nat translations`, `show flows`, `show mac address-table` — kept as a table of before/after with the measured detail that LLDP announcements alone are enough to populate a MAC table. Corrected the `show ip route — as computed, or at an instant` section (now *now, or at an instant*), added the plain form to its transcript so the "same table" claim is visible instead of asserted, fixed the `show ipv6 route` row, and softened the `show ipv6 interface` note to the one case where "ask again with `at converged`" is the truth and not a workaround (an address that has to arrive in a frame). Same correction in `CONVERGENCE.md`, whose BGP section still said a run is required. Files: `docs/CLI.md` (five passages), `docs/CONVERGENCE.md`, `docs/SIMULATION.md` |
| The BGP help row, which advertised a spelling the parser refused | `help` under `router bgp` offers `bgp neighbor <ip> remote-as <as>`; the parser accepted only `neighbor <ip> remote-as <as>`, so the documented form was answered `% Usage: router bgp <as> \| bgp router-id <ip> \| neighbor <ip> remote-as <as>`. The row packs three complete commands, and the first (`bgp router-id`) worked, so `test/help-forms.test.js` passed the row — its rule was "some reading of this row is typable". The same sweep also could not see it for a second reason: with no BGP enabled the refusal is `% BGP is not enabled`, which is a refusal on *state* and therefore indistinguishable from success. `show running-config` emits the `neighbor …` form, so the dump was pasteable; only the help was wrong | **Both spellings accepted, and the row pinned where BGP is on.** `_bgp()` now strips an optional leading `bgp` or `neighbor`, and `no bgp neighbor <ip>` removes the peer as `no neighbor <ip>` does; the usage line names both forms. `test/running-config.test.js` gained a section that enables BGP, types the documented spelling, asserts the peer appears, that `show running-config` prints the prefix-less form, that the prefix-less form is also accepted, and that `no bgp neighbor` removes it — 5 assertions, and proved non-vacuous: deleting the `neighbor` strip fails 4 of them, deleting the `no bgp` arm fails 2. `test/help-forms.test.js` learned `% Usage:` as a shape complaint (it was the one wording that meant "your form is wrong" and was not in the list) and now offers both readings of an arm inside a group, because `[proto tcp\|udp]` means `proto udp` and a literal reading offered a bare `udp`, which the parser rightly refused — that false finding was masking the real one. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/running-config.test.js`, `test/help-forms.test.js`, `docs/CLI.md` (BGP section) |
| Prerequisite refusals — six call sites, four spellings, and two hints missing entirely | `% BGP is not enabled` was the whole answer from `show ip bgp`, `show ip ospf` and `show vrrp`, while `bgp neighbor …` answered `% BGP is not enabled (use "router bgp <as>")` and `bgp router-id` said the same thing with `(use "router bgp <as>" first)`. So the *show* — the command you type when you do not know why something is missing — was the one door with no way out, and the same fact had four spellings. Nothing checked any of it: a hint that names a command the parser refuses is a promise made in passing, and no suite read the promise | **One string per feature, and the hint is typed back at the console.** `NEEDS` in `engine/cli.js` holds one wording each for BGP, OSPF, VRRP, SCION and privilege, and every site reads it, so a show and the verb it guards cannot disagree (asserted: the two answers are byte-identical). Each message now names the command in the form the console accepts — `% BGP is not enabled - "router bgp <as>" in config mode turns BGP on`, `% VRRP is not configured - "vrrp <id> ip <addr>" inside an interface block adds a group`, `% This device is not a SCION AS - "scion <isd-as>" in config mode makes this device an AS`, `% clear counters needs enable mode - type "enable" first` — and `% No VRRP group 10 on Gi0/0` gained the command that creates one instead of "give it an address first". New `test/refusal-pointers.test.js` (73 assertions) covers 12 refusals: it pulls the quoted command out of each message, fills the placeholders, types it *in the mode the hint claims* (config, interface block, or `enable`), requires no refusal, then re-enters the original mode and requires the original command to answer. Non-vacuous both ways: writing `"bgp <as>"` into the BGP hint fails 4 assertions, deleting the OSPF hint fails 3. Four stale quotes in `docs/BGP-POLICY.md` and three in `docs/CLI.md` were corrected to the measured wording. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/refusal-pointers.test.js`, `test/logging.test.js`, `docs/CLI.md`, `docs/BGP-POLICY.md` |
| The destination slot of `ping`, `traceroute` and `flow open` | All three take a destination first and all three have keywords of their own after it, but nothing kept the two apart. `ping` with no argument answered `% Invalid ping target: (none)` — a placeholder printed where a value belongs — `traceroute` the same, `flow open` quoted a synopsis containing `<dst-ip>`, a placeholder that appears in no help row. Worst of all, a line beginning with a keyword was handed *to the resolver as a hostname*: `ping at 5000` and `flow open port 22` answered `% Unknown host at: no DNS server in this lab hosts a zone for it`, so a forgotten address came back as a claim about the lab's DNS | **One helper, one message, and the resolver only sees a real destination.** `_requireDest(tokens, usage)` refuses when the first token is missing or is one of `DEST_KEYWORDS` (`at`, `count`, `port`, `bytes`, `proto`, `sport`, kept as data so a new keyword is one edit), and every message quotes the synopsis `help` prints — `% Incomplete command: ping <dst> [count <n>] [at <ms>\|converged]`, `… traceroute <dst> …`, `… flow open <dst> [port <n>] [bytes <n>] [proto tcp\|udp]`. `nslookup` with no name joined them (it said `% Usage:` for the same fact). `<dst-ip>` is gone from all three places it appeared, including the traceroute synopsis in `CLI.md`, which still said `<ip>`. The cost is stated in the page rather than hidden: a host genuinely named `at` or `port` cannot be pinged by name — resolve it first. New `test/dest-arguments.test.js` (38 assertions): every keyword in the destination slot must give the incomplete message and must never mention a host or DNS, an upper-case keyword is the same keyword, a name beginning with `at` is not, and real addresses, real names (asserting the `domain server (R1 (authoritative for lab.test))` line) and `traceroute … at <ms>` still work. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/dest-arguments.test.js`, `test/flow-cli.test.js`, `docs/CLI.md` |
| Arguments the command does not use | Five commands accepted a word they had no slot for and answered anyway: `show vlan 99` printed **every** VLAN (and `show vlan abc` printed it too, so a typo and a real id were the same input), `show flows counters 99` printed the counters, `show ip route connected extra` printed the filter, `converge 100 extra` converged, and `show logging last 0x10` / `last 1e3` were read as 16 and 1000 because the guard was `Number.isFinite` + integer rather than the spelling of a count. `flow close abc` answered `% no such flow: abc` — lower case, unlike every other refusal, and the answer to a question nobody asked | **One rule, one wording.** `_noExtra(tokens, from, usage)` refuses a tail the command does not use and quotes the synopsis; applied to `show flows counters`, `show flows <id>`, `converge`, `show ip route <protocol\|summary>` and `show vlan`. `show vlan [<id>]` now narrows (refusing an unknown id by name, separating *carried* VLANs from *configured but empty* ones, and printing `(no ports)` for a declared empty VLAN) while keeping `brief` and `id <n>` working — adding an argument must not withdraw a documented synonym. `_flowId()` gives one spelling for both doors (`flow-3` ≡ `3`), and `last <n>` validates digits. First attempt replaced `Number.isFinite` with `/^\d+$/` on the *flow id* too, which broke every real id (`flow-1`); the existing suite caught it and the shape became `flow-<n>\| <n>`. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/extra-arguments.test.js` (59 assertions, including a regression block over the nine port-taking shows), `docs/CLI.md` |
| The VLAN life cycle — create, name, remove, and who a removal would strand | One VLAN object, four commands that disagreed about whether it existed. `vlan abc` answered **`VLAN NaN created (name: VLAN0NaN)`** and wrote `config.vlans[NaN]`; `vlan 9999` refused with the data-model field name (`% accessVlan 9999 is outside 1-4094`) rather than naming a VLAN; re-typing `vlan 30` said "created" again though nothing was created; `no vlan 99` said "VLAN 99 removed" for a VLAN that never existed and `no vlan` said "VLAN NaN removed"; worst, `no vlan 30` said "removed" while two access ports still carried it, so `show vlan` went on printing the VLAN the command claimed to have deleted. And `show vlan` on a router printed VLAN 1 with every router port in it — a bridge domain invented whole, two lines after `interface vlan` refuses because "this device does not switch" | **Four commands, one model of existence.** `vlan <id> [name]` validates the id as a number, refuses out-of-range by name, reports "already configured (name: …)" instead of "created", and renames on a second word. `no vlan <id>` refuses a VLAN that is not there and refuses one a port is still in, naming the ports — and it distinguishes the two dependencies a port can have: an access port or a trunk's **native** VLAN is a hard dependency (refused, port marked `(native)`), while a trunk that merely lists the VLAN has it pruned from the allowed list, and the command says so. A router may still declare a VLAN (its running-config round-trips the line) but has no membership to report, so the Ports column holds `-`. `show vlan <id>` and `no vlan <id>` read one walk (`_vlanMap()`), so the ports a refusal names are exactly the ports the table lists. Two mistakes of my own, both caught by running: refusing `vlan <id>` on a router broke `running-config`/`startup-config` (a router may declare a VLAN — the tests encoded it), and my first data-path assertion `/0 percent/` was vacuous because `Success rate is 100 percent` contains it. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/vlan-lifecycle.test.js` (43 assertions), `docs/CLI.md` |
| The `switchport` verbs — a VLAN id read as `parseInt`, and a `no` form that answered with a reset | The same defect as the VLAN verbs, one level down, because the id was read the same way: `switchport access vlan abc` answered **`Access VLAN NaN on Fa0/1`** and stored it; `switchport access vlan` with no value answered the same thing — a success message for a missing argument; `switchport access vlan 9999` and `switchport trunk native vlan 9999` leaked the data-model field name (`% accessVlan 9999 is outside 1-4094`); `switchport mode trunk access` was answered "Switchport mode trunk on Fa0/1" with the extra word dropped; bare `switchport` answered `% Unrecognized switchport command: ` (an empty command), and `switchport trunk allowed` was reported as a wrong *word* while the next word down was reported as an incomplete command. Worst, every `no switchport …` became a full port reset, so `no switchport trunk allowed vlan 10` wiped the mode, the native VLAN and the access VLAN instead of removing one VLAN from a list | **One id rule, and a `no` as narrow as the command it undoes.** `_switchport()` validates every id with one helper (`% VLAN id must be a number, not "abc"`, `% VLAN 9999 is outside 1-4094`, `% Incomplete command: …`) and refuses a trailing word through `_noExtra()`; the `no` forms are parsed separately — remove a VLAN and the list shrinks (`Trunk on Fa0/1: removed 10, still allows 20,30`), `no switchport mode` returns to access without touching the access VLAN, `no switchport access vlan` reports what it replaced, and only a bare `no switchport` resets. Removing from a trunk that allows *all* is refused rather than guessed, because the model cannot say "all but 10". `switchport` is refused on a device that does not bridge and on a logical interface (SVI, subinterface) — both used to accept and store a membership nothing would ever read. `test/switchport-args.test.js` (42 assertions) proves the narrow removal changes the **data path**: on two switches joined by a trunk, removing VLAN 10 from the allowed list stops the VLAN 10 ping and leaves the VLAN 20 ping working — the old reset would have killed both, which is what makes the assertion non-vacuous. Files: `engine/cli.js`, `test/switchport-args.test.js`, `docs/CLI.md` |
| `CliSession.run()` silently ignoring a second line | `run()` took one line. Eighteen call sites in the suite pass several — `run('configure terminal', 'hostname R1', 'vlan 99 users', …)` — and all of them turned out to be test-local wrappers that loop, so nothing was vacuous; but writing a new suite against the engine class produced a block that configured nothing and then asserted against the output of its own mistake (`% Unrecognized config command: configure terminal` as `output[0]`), and a missing setup reads as a parsing bug. Nothing in the engine said which of the two it was | **The second line now runs.** `run()` accepts extra arguments, executes them in order and concatenates their output, which is what every caller already assumed; the single-line path is untouched. Files: `engine/cli.js` |
| An ARP entry learned two hops away had no interface, and its port filter could not see it | The port was derived only for an address whose owner was directly peered, so a learner behind a switch answered `-` — and `show arp <port>` said the port had no cached addresses while the plain table listed one in that port's subnet | The subnet decides when no peer does (as it already did for hand-written entries); an off-subnet address still keeps `-`. `STATIC` moved out of the Interface cell into a `Type` column, and the single-address view renders through the same table function. The Age dash is explained when a table is shown, since this model does not age its cache. **Evidence:** `test/arp-attribution.test.js` (26 assertions) |
| A doc row claimed a ping fills `show flows` | A flow is one conversation; a ping is a probe, so the table stayed empty — the doc said otherwise | The row now says a ping fills nothing and names `flow open <dst>`. **Evidence:** same suite, and `docs-syntax` |
| A static or default route named no interface, though it forwarded | `show ip route` and `show ip route <prefix>` answered `Interface -` for a gatewayed route whose next hop was in the ARP cache on Gi0/0 — the data path had resolved the port all along | The table and the lookup read `egressFor(nextHop)`, the helper the data path uses, so decision, table and cache cannot disagree; the default gateway got the same fix, and a gateway on no subnet here still keeps `-`. **Evidence:** `test/route-egress.test.js` (18 assertions) |
| Two routers on one segment through a switch formed no OSPF adjacency | Adjacency was built only from a direct link, so the ordinary `R1 -- SW1 -- R2` lab converged on nothing while `show ip ospf neighbor` blamed the passive, subnet and area checks that all passed | Adjacency also comes from the `segmentOf()` walk (VLANs, STP port state and down links respected), an area mismatch across a segment is named with both areas, and the segment route carries its egress port. **Evidence:** `test/ospf-segment.test.js` (26 assertions) |
| OSPF refused to start without a configured `router-id` while the view promised a derived one | `computeOspf()` skipped a process with no `router-id`, so a lab that never typed one had no speakers and no explanation; `show ip ospf` said "the model uses the highest interface address" | The id is derived from the highest interface address as promised, `show ip ospf` prints the id in use and says it was derived, and a configured id still wins. **Evidence:** same suite |
| `network <net> <wildcard> area <id>` refused the subnet of a subinterface | The matcher skipped `i.parent` (and `i.svi`), so router-on-a-stick could not put its own `Gi0/0.20` in an area: "No interface of R1 matches 10.0.20.0 0.0.0.255" for an interface holding 10.0.20.1 | The address is the test: subinterfaces and SVIs are matched like any other routed interface, and the engine already used them once listed. **Evidence:** `test/ospf-subinterface.test.js` (14 assertions) |
| A subinterface had no segment, so router-on-a-stick formed no adjacency | `segmentOf()` starts at `iface.peer`, which a subinterface does not have; a tagged frame was never walked, so two speakers on one trunk were never neighbours | `segmentOf(..., { vid })` walks a tagged frame, requiring every switch port it touches to carry the tag; trunk-not-allowing-the-VLAN, two different tags, an untagged peer and a shut parent all stop it. **Evidence:** same suite |
| A shut parent left its subinterface enabled, and a shut port still produced an adjacency from the far end | Participation checked only the interface's own `enabled` flag, and the walk started from a disabled port | A subinterface with a down parent does not participate, and a walk from a disabled port returns nothing. **Evidence:** same suite |
| `show ip bgp` printed the neighbour summary | The console had one table for two questions, so "what did my peers give me, and which row forwards" had no command at all | `show ip bgp` is the BGP table (prefix, next hop, AS path, `*>` = the row that forwards, decided by `routesTable()`), `show ip bgp <prefix>` is one entry with the reason it is or is not installed, and IOS's Metric/LocPrf/Weight/Origin columns are withheld rather than filled with invented defaults. **Evidence:** `test/bgp-table.test.js` (25 assertions) |
| The BGP summary counted received prefixes as "installed" | `2 prefix(es) installed from BGP` above a table where every row lost to a connected route, and a routing table with no BGP row installed at all — the count was `bgpRoutes.length` | The count comes from `routesTable()`, and both numbers are reported ("2 received, 0 installed - the rest lose on distance"); the neighbour detail counts the same fact. **Evidence:** `test/bgp-counts.test.js` (16 assertions) |
| `show ip bgp` did not list what the router announces | The table held only received routes, so a box with no peers said nothing had been received while it was announcing its subnets — and the refusal for `network` pointed at this view for "what it would send" | Locally originated prefixes appear with next hop `0.0.0.0` and a legend that says what that means; the announcement rule (connected subnets only, no `network`/`redistribute`) is stated in the answer. **Evidence:** same suite |
| The `network` refusal promised static routes are announced | Only connected subnets are originated; a static route on one router never reached its peer, so the sentence described behaviour the engine does not have | The refusal states the real rule, and the suite checks that the static route is indeed not announced. **Evidence:** same suite |
| `show ip bgp` printed `router identifier 0.0.0.0` for a box with an address | The identifier was elected only when two speakers were configured, because the election sat after the early return for a single speaker | Every speaker elects its identifier, peers or not. **Evidence:** same suite |

| The OSPF database listed a prefix that a static route held | A produced route was shown as if it forwarded; nothing marked it or named the winner | A mark per prefix taken from `routesTable()`, a key that always prints, and a line naming what holds the prefix and at what distance. **Evidence:** same suite |

| `reload` answered `% Unrecognized command` | Nothing in this model boots, so there is no reload — but a typo message sends the operator looking for a spelling instead of at the two things that do exist | The absent-family answer: no boot, no restart to schedule, no image to load; with a saved copy it names `copy startup-config running-config` and `show startup-config`, without one it names `write memory`, and it says what a reboot would have done (build the device again). | `no ipv6 nd ra lifetime 10` set the lifetime to 10 again | The removal took the positive branch: it answered `IPv6 RA router lifetime 10s` and re-applied the value, while the bare `no ipv6 nd ra lifetime` answered `% Incomplete command`. A removal that does the opposite of removing is worse than a missing one, because it answers in the affirmative | The removal resets to the protocol default (1800s, read from `engine/ra.js`, not typed into the message), says what it was, accepts the old value as an optional word, and leaves no line in the running-config so a paste round-trips. | `no spanning-tree vlan 10 root primary` refused its own words | The positive form is `spanning-tree vlan <id> root primary\|secondary`; the removal insisted on `no spanning-tree vlan <id>` and answered `% Unexpected argument "root primary"` | The removal accepts the words the positive form accepts. | `no ip domain-name lab.local` demanded a shorter spelling | The positive takes the name, the removal refused it (`% Unexpected argument "lab.local"`, usage `no ip domain-name`) — so the operator had to retype what was on their screen | The removal accepts the name, matches case-insensitively (the positive lowercases it), and when it does not match names the configured value instead of clearing a different one. **Evidence:** same suite |
| `switchport port-security violation rest` refused while `switchport mode acc` worked | The abbreviation tree is built from the **help rows**, so a row written `violation <mode>` made the three modes opaque values: `rest`, `prot`, `shut` were refused with *"is not a violation mode"* while every other keyword on the page abbreviated. The row now reads `violation protect\|restrict\|shutdown` | It is the same lesson the page already carries: **the row is the command surface**. A placeholder is right for a value with no vocabulary (`<name>`, `<ip>`) and wrong for a keyword set the parser accepts, because it hides those words from abbreviation and completion alike. `b` and `zz` still identify nothing and are refused with the mode list. **Evidence:** `test/keyword-abbrev.test.js` (27 assertions, including a sweep that types every `a\|b` alternative the page prints) |
