Probing found more: `bgp router-id <ip>` was `% Unrecognized` in global config although the console's own usage text named it there; `neighbor … remote-as` typed **inside an OSPF process** was accepted and configured BGP through the paste fall-through; `ospf router-id 1.1.1.1` answered **"OSPF enabled"** on a PC and a switch; and a repeated `neighbor` line was silent, looking like a second peer | **The page follows the process** (`routerKind`): BGP shows `neighbor`/`no neighbor`/`bgp router-id`/`exit`/`end`, OSPF keeps `network`/`router-id`. `bgp router-id` and `bgp neighbor … remote-as` work from **global config** too; the other process's verbs are **refused inside a process** (*"That is a BGP verb - "exit" first, then "router bgp <as>""*) while `router bgp` still switches processes; the OSPF gate moved onto the **family** so no spelling reaches a host or a switch; repeated peers and identifiers answer *"already configured"*, and a changed AS reports *"was 65002 … the session restarts"*. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/router-mode-help.test.js` (45 assertions) which asserts the **property** — every row a mode shows is typed back into that mode and must be accepted — rather than the row list, plus the cross-mode refusals and the dump |
| Interface-mode verbs that were accepted on devices with no use for them | Probing `?` **inside an interface block** (the previous two passes covered config mode and the `show` views): `channel-group 1 mode active` was **accepted** on a PC (*"Interface eth0 added to channel group 1 (LACP active)"*) and on a router (a bundle of routed interfaces), while `show etherchannel` had just been taught to refuse a non-switch — the command and the view disagreed about the same hardware. `ip ospf cost 10` on a switch answered *"% OSPF is not enabled"*, describing a missing **configuration** where the truth is a missing **capability**. The page offered `no power inline` to devices with no PoE ports, because it shared one row with `no mtu`, `no speed` and `no duplex`. And `no switchport port-security` was shown to a PC while its positive form was filtered — the removal of a setting the device could never have had | **`channel-group` refused by capability** (*"R1 does not switch, so it has no switch ports to bundle … Routed interfaces are a routing problem"*), no LAG left behind; **interface OSPF verbs name the device** on a non-router while a router without OSPF keeps the configuration answer; the four removals **split into separate rows** so each is gated with its positive form; every row naming a switchport is a switch row, `no` forms included. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/iface-capability.test.js` (32 assertions: refusals on PC/router/server, a switch keeping its LAG, a purpose-built PSE accepting `power inline`, and an orphan-removal sweep over four device kinds). The suite also caught **a defect in itself** — a doubled backslash made `rows()` always empty, so every "is not offered" assertion passed vacuously; it now asserts each page renders more than twenty rows first |
| The `show` views for tables a device cannot have, which answered instead of refusing | Found by asking every bridge view on a **PC**: `show vlan` printed **VLAN 1 with `-` for ports** (a fabricated broadcast domain), `show spanning-tree` printed **"Spanning tree enabled protocol ieee"** on a host, `show mac address-table` printed a header for a switching table that cannot exist, and `show etherchannel` said **"No link aggregation groups configured"** — true of a device that cannot have any. `show port-security` already answered correctly, so the four neighbours disagreed with it. The *page* offered all four to the same PC | **Four views brought in line with the fifth**: each names what the device is and where the equivalent is — *"PC1 is a host: it has no VLANs … a VLAN is a broadcast domain on something that switches it"*, *"does not bridge, so it runs no spanning tree"* (with a router's wording when it routes and a host's when it does not), *"does not switch, so it has no MAC address table … the equivalent here is the address cache, `show arp`"*, *"does not switch, so it has no link aggregation"*. A router keeps `show vlan` — it may declare VLAN names for its subinterfaces — but the table no longer invents **VLAN 1** it never declared, and an empty table became a sentence. The help page follows the handler, so a PC is not offered any of the five. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/show-capability.test.js` (36 assertions: the refusals on a host and a server, the router's declared-VLAN case, a switch keeping every table, and page-vs-handler agreement in both directions), plus `test/vlan-lifecycle.test.js` whose "header plus VLAN 1 and VLAN 99" assertion was encoding the invention and changed with it |
| The help page was identical on every device, and two verbs it listed were accepted on boxes that cannot use them | Found by dumping `?` in config mode on **seven device kinds** and diffing: **62 identical rows** on a PC, a server, a switch and a router. A host with one Ethernet port was offered `vlan <id>`, `switchport <verb>`, `spanning-tree vlan <id> root primary`, `ip nat inside source …` and `router ospf <process-id>` — and typing two of them **worked**: `vlan 10` answered **`VLAN 10 created (name: VLAN0010)`** and `router ospf 1` answered **`OSPF process 1 enabled, entering router configuration mode`** on a host. `switchport` and `router bgp` already refused a device that could not use them; `vlan` and `router ospf` had no gate at all, so the page and the parser were wrong in the same direction | **One capability predicate per verb family** (`_helpApplies`), applied to every help page: switching on `l2-forward`, routing and its protocols on `l3-forward`, NAT, DHCP server, ACLs, VRRP, IPv6 and the three exec tables on their own tags. **`vlan` and `router ospf` got the gate their neighbours had**, each saying what the device *is* — *"PC1 is not a switch or a router, so it has no VLANs … a host reaches other networks with ip default-gateway"* — and `switchport` on a host now answers the capability before the mode hint (it used to say "enter an interface first", advice that cannot work). A router keeps `vlan <id>` (it routes between them and the dump round-trips the line) and loses `switchport`/`spanning-tree`; an L3 switch keeps the switchport verbs and loses NAT. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/help-truthful.test.js` (32 assertions: every shown row typed back at its device must not answer `% Unrecognized`, six generic kinds plus two real catalogue entries; every hidden row proved refused by the device itself), and `help-coverage`/`docs-syntax` now read the unfiltered page and assert the filtered page is a **subset** of it, so the filter can hide a row but never invent one |
| `router bgp <as>` did not enter a mode, so the line after the block was lost; and four more gaps in families the first round trip did not reach | Found by **pointing last iteration's dump-and-paste harness at the protocol families** (IPv6, DHCP pool, VRRP, BGP, named ACL, STP root) and at a **real PSE from the catalogue**. `router bgp 65001` enabled the process and **stayed in global config**, so the `exit` that ends every BGP block dropped the operator to exec and every following command was read as exec and lost — the prompt was already claiming `(config-router)#`. Then: `ipv6 route` was not idempotent (a second paste doubled it) while `ip route` was; `vrrp <vrid> preempt` was stored and **never dumped**, so a paste silently reverted it; `spanning-tree vlan 10 root primary` dumped as `spanning-tree vlan 1 priority 24576`, inventing a VLAN; `show ip bgp summary` read **`0 of 0 neighbour(s)`** with a peer configured one command earlier; and `mikrotik/RB960PGS` — capabilities `poe-pse, 802.3at, 802.3af, passive-poe` — answered **`% POE1 neither sources nor draws PoE`** and refused `power inline never` on every port | **BGP mode**: `router bgp <as>` enters `(config-router)#` sharing the mode machinery with OSPF via `routerKind`, `exit`→config, `end`→exec, wrong-process verbs refused in that process's vocabulary, non-BGP lines leave the mode and re-dispatch as config (so a dump without `exit` pastes), and a layer 2 switch is refused BGP by name. **`ipv6 route` identity** matches the IPv4 rule. **`preempt` and `no preempt` are dumped.** **The VLAN the operator named is kept** for the dump and both STP forms say one spanning tree covers every VLAN here. **`show ip bgp summary` lists configured peers** with `Idle` / `not yet evaluated`. **A capability list with no port behind it is reported as a specification gap, not a device state** — the first fix derived a per-port wattage from the declared standard and three catalogue-wide guards refused it, correctly (a port's wattage is a datasheet fact); the console now prints *"declares poe-pse, 802.3at … but no port record on it carries power-out … that is a gap in the device specification, not a state of the device"*, from both `show power inline` and the `power inline` verbs, and **configured-withheld ports are listed** (`Admin never`, `off (administratively)`) instead of vanishing from the table. **Evidence:** `engine/device.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/bgp-router-mode.test.js` (28), `test/roundtrip-protocols.test.js` (22), `test/poe-admin.test.js` (34) |
| `ip routing` did not exist on an L3 switch; and three list-shaped settings were not idempotent | Found by turning the dump-and-paste **probe into a test** over five device kinds. `ip routing` answered **`% Unrecognized config command`** on `generic-l3-switch`, a device whose whole purpose is routing between its VLANs — the model forwarded whenever the device *could*, with no way to stop it. The round trip then failed the other way: **pasting the same dump twice was not the same as pasting it once**, because static routes, ACL entries (both spellings) appended blindly — an identical ACL line doubled its match counter and lengthened the dump, and an identical route appeared twice with the same AD (NAT rules already had the guard) | **`ip routing` / `no ip routing`** with a real data-path effect: `_forwardL3()` checks the flag first and drops with the reason `IP routing is disabled on L31 ("no ip routing")`, so the ping fails *as written*; the setting is dumped; `show ip route` leads with **`IP routing is disabled - these routes are configured but will not forward`** because a table full of live-looking rows is the most misleading output in the model; a host is refused by name and pointed at `ip default-gateway`. **Identity for the three lists**: a static route is its dest/mask/gateway/interface, an ACL entry is `acl.entryKey`, and each re-add now answers *"already configured … the table is unchanged"* with the command that removes it. `switchport <verb>` typed at global scope answers with the mode the verb lives in, like `vrrp`. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/config-roundtrip.test.js` (30 assertions: the reference config accepted on all five devices, identical dumps after one paste, identical dumps after two, the state actually landed, `ip routing` on/off/dumped/refused-on-a-host) |
| `router ospf <pid>` and `network … area …` did not exist, on a model that runs OSPF | Found by writing a **dump-and-paste round trip** over a broad reference configuration: `router ospf 1` → **`% Unrecognized config command: router ospf 1`**, `network 10.10.0.0 0.0.0.255 area 0` likewise — while **`router bgp <as>` was implemented**, so the asymmetry was nobody's decision, and the OSPF half is the one every lab guide types. The area lives on the *interface* here (`ip ospf area`), which is why `network` had no home | **`router ospf <pid>`** (1-65535, validated where typed) starts the process and enters `(config-router)#`, the mode's verbs being **`network <net> <wildcard> area <id>`**, `router-id <ip>`, `exit`, `end`. `network` writes the per-interface area for every interface the wildcard selects and **names them** (`Gi0/0 (10.0.0.1): area 0`); a statement that matches nothing is **refused, not accepted in silence** — IOS's silence is exactly the case where the adjacency never forms and nothing in the config looks wrong. A line that is not a router-mode verb **leaves the mode and re-dispatches as global config**, because a pasted config never says `exit` before `interface Gi0/1` and losing that line leaves a subtly wrong configuration. The pid survives the dump as `router ospf <pid>` plus one host-wildcard statement per area'd interface, and the paste round-trips byte-identically. **Two defects in code that was already passing:** `show ip ospf` printed the constant **`Routing Process "ospf 1"`** (right only for the id nobody could configure) and now prints the real one; and the abbreviation pass rewrote `router ospf 1` typed *inside* router mode into **`router-id ospf 1`** — `router` is a prefix of `router-id` in that mode — then refused it in the wrong vocabulary, so a word that is already a complete command of the enclosing configuration is no longer abbreviated into something else. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/abbrev.js` (unchanged, by design), `test/ospf-router.test.js` (56 assertions) |
| Commands from a lab guide, answered `% Invalid show command` — and three words that were read as port names | `show cdp neighbors`, `show inventory`, `show processes cpu`, `show users`, `show line`, `show history`, `show ntp status` all answered **`% Invalid show command: …`**, which reads like a typo and sends the operator looking for a fault that is not there; `show interfaces <port> switchport` **silently dropped the word** and printed the plain counters view (an operator checking a trunk was told nothing about it); `show interfaces counters errors` was read as a port called **`errors`**, and `show interfaces vlan 20` as a port called **`vlan`** | **One table (`_absentShow`)** answers each family with the equivalent here, and every pointer in it is a command the test runs: CDP → the LLDP views, `inventory` → `show version` + `show interfaces transceiver`, `environment`/`power supply` → `show power inline`, `users`/`sessions`/`line` → one console session, `processes`/`cpu` → the per-port counters, `ntp` → the fact that time here is an **instant you ask about**, `show switchport` → the three tables that split a port's L2 state. **`show history`** is real: the console keeps the lines typed into it (bounded 200, prints IOS's last 10) and *asking for the history is not recorded* — a question that changes its own answer is the same defect as a `show` that moves a counter. **`show interfaces <port> switchport`** is a real view (mode, access VLAN with its name or native VLAN + allowed list, port security, description, channel-group); two bugs came out of running it — the shut marker read `iface.shutdown` where the model stores **`enabled`**, so no port ever showed as shut, and the access-VLAN line ended in a trailing space. **`show interfaces vlan <id>`** resolves the SVI; `show interfaces counters errors` gets the honest "no error counters, drops are named events" sentence. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/absent-commands.test.js` (79 assertions) |
| Three LLDP questions with no view, and two of them answered with a different view | `show lldp neighbors` was the only LLDP view, so `show lldp neighbors detail` answered **`% No interface named detail`** (a keyword read as a port name), while `show lldp traffic` and `show lldp interface` **silently printed the neighbour table** — a wrong answer, which nothing in the output marks as wrong. `show vlan name users` answered **`% Invalid VLAN id "name"`** even though VLANs have names | **`show lldp`** (status, interval, hold time, which ports announce, neighbours learned), **`show lldp neighbors detail`** (one block per neighbour from the DU that arrived: chassis, port id, description, TTL, medium/speed), **`show lldp interface [<port>]`** (the per-port Tx/Rx decision beside its neighbour), **`show lldp traffic [<port>]`** (counters). Counters are **port state** (`lldpTxFrames`/`lldpRxframes`/`lldpDiscarded`), written where a DU is sent or heard, so `clear logging` cannot empty them; a DU heard while the port is told not to listen counts as **discarded, not unheard**. `show lldp interface detail` is answered **by name** and pointed at the neighbour table. Also **`show vlan name <name>`** — case-insensitive, narrowing, listing the names that exist on a miss. Two bugs caught by running: a declared VLAN is a record, so `% Names here:` printed **[object Object]**; and the trailing-word refusal quoted `show vlan [<id>|brief]` for an operator who had typed `show vlan name users`. **Evidence:** `engine/lldp.js` (`DEFAULT_INTERVAL_S`), `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/lldp-views.test.js` (61 assertions)
| Three aging settings printed by `show port-security interface`, with no command to set them | `show port-security interface` printed **`Aging Time : 0 mins`, `Aging Type : Absolute`, `SecureStatic Address Aging : Disabled`** — three lines describing a device that was not there, since `switchport port-security aging time 1` answered `% Unrecognized port-security command: aging`. The same defect as recommending a command that does not exist, one layer quieter; and aging is what stops a secure port being pinned to a laptop that has left the building. One real bug in the fix: a **configured** address had no timestamp at all, so `aging static` was accepted and could never remove one | **`switchport port-security aging time <1-1440>\|type absolute\|inactivity\|static`** (plus the three `no` forms), with "now" being the instant the question is asked about — the same `at <ms>` clock the rest of the console reads — so the two types are genuinely different: `absolute` counts from the first sighting and ignores traffic, `inactivity` resets at every sighting. Aging runs **before** the ingress decision, so an address that has aged out makes the arriving frame a **new occupant instead of a violation** (the test asserts both sides), and it is reported on the timeline as `port-security-age`. Configured addresses record the instant they were held from, which is what makes `aging static` real. The empty table now distinguishes "nothing was ever learned" from "aged out at this instant". Every word validated where typed (range, `0` refused with a pointer at `no … aging time`, unknown type, missing/extra argument), idempotent both ways, dumped inside the interface block and pasteable. **Evidence:** `engine/portsecurity.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/ps-aging.test.js` (53 assertions) |
| The recovery message that recommended a command the console did not have | Last iteration's `no shutdown` on an err-disabled port printed **`Security violation count kept at 1 - "clear port-security counters" resets it`**, and **`clear port-security counters` was `% Unrecognized command`** — the console pointing at a command it did not have, which is worse than a missing feature. Two smaller lies in the same sweep: `show port-security address` printed a **constant VLAN of 1** for every row (a station secured on VLAN 20 was reported in VLAN 1), and `vlan 20 name users` created a VLAN called **"name users"** because the IOS keyword was swallowed as part of the name. And `vlan 20` followed by `show vlan` printed **VLAN 1 and nothing else**, because the table was built from membership only — while `show vlan 20` answered for the VLAN the table had just denied | **Three clear verbs that clear three different things.** `clear port-security counters` resets the violation count and nothing else (the port stays err-disabled: that is a separate decision), `dynamic` forgets what was learned and keeps what was written or pinned, `sticky` unpins the pinned addresses and says the next station may be a different one. All take an optional `interface <port>`, report what they cleared and what they left, and say "nothing to clear" the second time; from exec they ask for enable mode with the same sentence as `clear arp`, and in config mode both are `% Unrecognized config command` (verified identical). The VLAN column now reads the port's own VLAN; `vlan <id> name <text>` consumes `name` as a keyword; `show vlan` unions membership with declared VLANs so a VLAN with no ports is a `(no ports)` row. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/ps-clear.test.js` (45 assertions) |
| Port security — a bridge with no opinion about who may speak | **`switchport port-security` was `% Unrecognized interface command` and `show port-security` was `% Invalid show command`**, on a model that already learns a source MAC per port and VLAN and forwards to what it learned. The bridge accepted every source and had no way to refuse one, so the one L2 feature an operator configures *before* an incident could not be configured at all — and the three violation modes, which differ in what they cost, had nowhere to live | **`engine/portsecurity.js` owns the decision, `_processFrame` asks it before learning**, so a refused source is never in the CAM and the two tables cannot contradict each other. `switchport port-security` (IOS defaults: one address, `shutdown`), `maximum <1-3072>`, `mac-address <mac>\|sticky`, `violation protect\|restrict\|shutdown`, and the four narrow `no` forms. The three modes behave as they cost: **protect** drops silently and counts nothing, **restrict** drops and counts while the port stays `Secure-up`, **shutdown** drops, counts one violation and err-disables the port (further frames are dropped but not recounted — one incident is one violation). A violation feeds the line state, so `show interfaces`, `show interfaces status` (`err-disabled`, with a reason, not `notconnect`) and `show port-security` describe one port; recovery is `shutdown` then `no shutdown`, which keeps the violation count as evidence. Three views: summary (port, limit, held, violations, mode, plus the err-disabled roll-call), detail (`Secure-up`/`Secure-shutdown`, counts, last source), and `show port-security address` (`SecureConfigured`/`SecureSticky`/`SecureDynamic`). Validation where typed: limit range, mode words, malformed and multicast MACs, the same station on two ports, trunk ports, logical interfaces, and a router by what it lacks. Dumped inside the interface block and pasteable. **Evidence:** `engine/portsecurity.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/port-security.test.js` (99 assertions) |
| ARP cache that could only be learned into — no `arp <ip> <mac>` | **`arp 10.0.0.50 0200.0000.0100 arpa` was `% Unrecognized config command`**, on a model where every routed packet reads the cache first and `clear arp` had just been added to empty it. A static entry is the one row an operator writes by hand and the one row the lab must not be able to take back, so its absence removed both a shortcut and the only ARP defence a device has | **A second table (`arpStatic`) with one lookup door (`dev.arpLookup`), and the rules that make static mean static.** The data path uses the entry and never asks: the timeline shows `via: arp-static` and contains no `arp-request`. **A reply cannot overwrite it** — a conflicting reply is refused with an `arp-static-hold` event naming what was offered and what was kept, and the pending frame goes to the configured MAC (without the entry the same reply is believed; the test asserts both). `clear arp` keeps the hand-written rows and says how many it kept, because a cache clear must not edit the running-config. `show arp` marks the row `STATIC` and prints one line saying what that means; `show ip arp <addr>` answers a static entry with a `Type` column. Both MAC spellings accepted and normalised; `arpa` optional and not a slot for anything else; all-zero and multicast MACs refused by name; entries dumped as configuration and pasteable; a learned entry for the same address is dropped when the static entry replaces it, and the command says so. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/device.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/static-arp.test.js` (58 assertions). Also fixed in passing: `plural(n,'address')` printed `0 addresss` |
| Name resolution that was always on and always asked as typed — no `ip domain-lookup`, no `ip domain-name` | **`ip domain-name lab.test` and `no ip domain-lookup` were both `% Unrecognized config command`**, on a model that *does* resolve `ping <name>` through its own zones and the lab's resolvers. So a lab about DNS could not show the two facts a DNS lab is about — that resolution can be switched off, and that an unqualified name gets a suffix — and a name was only ever looked up exactly as typed, with no way to make `ping www` mean anything | **Give the console the two switches, and make the answers explain themselves.** `ip domain-name <name>` sets the suffix (validated as a DNS name where it is typed: empty label, leading/trailing hyphen, illegal character, label over 63 characters), `no ip domain-lookup` refuses names while an address still works, and both are idempotent, dumped and pasteable. `_resolveHost()` applies the suffix before asking and **reports the name actually asked** (`as www.lab.test - "ip domain-name" appended the suffix`) so the answer is never from a name the operator did not type; `ping`, `traceroute` and `flow open` print it identically. A disabled lookup is refused by naming the switch and the way back, never as `% Unknown host` (which would send the operator to look at DNS). `show dns` prints both settings beside the zones they govern. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/dns-policy.test.js` (42 assertions: a qualified name resolving, the bare name failing without a suffix, the suffix applied and shown by both ping and traceroute, a qualified name never double-suffixed, clearing and re-clearing, names refused with the switch named while an address still answers, `show dns` reporting the policy, four malformed suffixes each refused with its reason, an empty and a trailing-word command, and the dump round-trip for both settings) |
| A neighbour table that could only ever fill — no `lldp run`, no `lldp transmit`, no `lldp receive` | **`no lldp run`, `no lldp transmit` and `no lldp receive` were all `% Unrecognized`**, on a model whose LLDP neighbour table is filled from LLDPDUs that really crossed the link — the reason the table is trustworthy, and the reason it could only grow. The only way to empty it was to unplug the cable, so a lab in which discovery must not work (the usual reason to care about LLDP) could not be built, and `show lldp neighbors` on a device that had stopped speaking would have said `(none - no LLDP has been heard)`, which reads like a cabling fault | **Three decisions at three scopes, each enforced in the data path and each honest about what the model cannot do.** `no lldp run` stops the device announcing and listening and forgets its table (reporting how many neighbours were forgotten); per port, `no lldp receive` drops the LLDPDU at the door **and drops the entry that port had already learned** (this model has no ageing, so a leftover would be a neighbour the port refused to hear), while `no lldp transmit` says plainly that the peer keeps its entry because nothing ages here. `show lldp neighbors` answers a disabled device with the state and the way back, and a non-listening port with its reason; transmit and receive are independent, so "announces but does not listen" is expressible. All three are dumped (`no lldp run` globally, the two port lines inside the port block), paste back, are idempotent in both directions, and have `no …` help rows the `?` answers. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/lldp-admin.test.js` (40 assertions: the populated table, both neighbours forgotten and the peer's retention, the state-shaped refusal, re-enable and re-learn, per-port receive dropping exactly one entry and leaving the other port alone, transmit/receive independence, both directions' idempotence, trailing-word refusals, `?` in both modes, and both dumps pasting back) |
| A PSE that could refuse a port for budget, and an operator who could not refuse one | **`power inline never` / `auto` were `% Unrecognized interface command`** while the PoE plane was the most completely modelled thing in the simulator: a 67 W budget, a per-port allocation, a powered device marked `poeUnpowered`, its frames refused with `poe-deny`, and `show power inline` reporting every watt. The one decision an operator makes about PoE — whether this port feeds what is plugged into it — could not be made: **power was only ever withheld by a budget running out**, a number you could watch but not spend | **Give the console the decision, and check it before the budget.** `power inline auto\|never` per port (`no power inline` → auto), validated where typed: a non-PSE device and a non-PoE-out port are refused by different sentences (the second naming the ports that *can* source), `static` is refused by describing what this PSE does instead (no pre-allocation), a bare command is incomplete, trailing words are refused. `computePoe()` checks `poeAdmin === 'never'` **before** the budget and records `poe-admin-off` as a decision rather than a shortage, so the withheld watts stay available: `Used 14 W` → `7 W`, `Available 53 W` → `60 W`, AP1 marked unpowered, AP2 untouched. `show power inline` gains an **Admin** column and distinguishes `off (administratively)` from `denied (budget exhausted)` on both the PSE and the PD side, and the running-config carries the line in its interface block so a paste cannot re-power a port. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/poe-admin.test.js` (34 assertions: Admin column and both statuses, the freed-watts arithmetic, the decision event, `poeUnpowered` per device, restore both ways, idempotence both ways, `static`/bogus/bare/trailing refusals, non-PSE and non-PoE-port refusals, the dump line inside its block, an untouched port contributing none, and a paste landing the decision) |
| The switch status table, the description list, and a state column that said `up/up` for ports with no cable | **`show interfaces status` and `show interfaces description` did not exist** (`% "show interfaces status" is not a command here`), `show ip interface` (no `brief`) answered `% Invalid show command: ip interface`, and — the real defect — **`lineUp` was maintained only for SVIs and subinterfaces**, so a physical port with nothing plugged in answered `up/up` in `show ip interface brief` and `show interfaces`: the two commands an operator types first were missing, and the state column they trust was wrong for every untouched port | **Derive the port's line once, and print it in three tables.** `Simulator.computePortState()` decides `lineUp` from the three facts the data path already requires (not shut, cable attached, link up) and runs at the front of `computeSviState()`, so `show ip interface brief`, `show interfaces <port>`, the new `show interfaces status` and the new `show interfaces description` all read one answer; a **physical** port with no cable is now `down/down` (and `notconnect` in the status table) while a **logical** interface keeps IOS's `up/down` with the reason in `show ip interface` (`Line state: no port is cabled`). `show interfaces status` renders Port/Name/Status/Vlan/Duplex/Speed/Type from the description, shutdown, VLAN membership, and configured-vs-negotiated (`a-full`/`a-1G`) speed, totals its own rows (`2 connected, 1 disabled, 5 notconnect`), refuses a router by naming why it has no per-port VLAN state, and a trunk answers `trunk` in the Vlan column. `show interfaces description` lists every label with its state (the label could previously be read only one port at a time) and says how to set one when none exist. `show ip interface` prints address/prefix, broadcast, MTU, NAT role and ACL bindings — only the fields somebody set. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/iface-status.test.js` (39 assertions: the seven columns, connected/notconnect/disabled/trunk, the totals line counted against the rows, router and trailing-word refusals, the label list and one-port filter, `down/down` for an unplugged port in three tables, administratively-down, an SVI `up/down` that comes up when a member port is cabled, the detail view's address/broadcast/MTU, NAT role and inbound ACL without the unbound direction, and `brief` still routing to its own table) |
| A keyword read as a port name — five `show` commands answering "No interface named summary" | **`show spanning-tree summary`, `show spanning-tree root`, `show mac address-table count`, `show mac address-table dynamic` and `show ip arp <address>` all answered `% No interface named <word>`** — each a real IOS sub-word, each read as a port, so the console sent the operator to look for a port that cannot exist; the summary/root questions are exactly what you ask of a tree that is not converging. No new data was needed: the election already stores root bridge ID, priority and the root path cost, the MAC table already holds every entry, and the ARP cache is keyed by address | **Teach each command its own keywords, and word the refusal from them.** `_portArg(args, dev, what, keywords)` now refuses an unknown word as `takes a port, or summary / root - not "bogus"`; `show spanning-tree summary` prints the root, the root path cost, ports counted by role and by state, and explains a blocking port as the tree working; `show spanning-tree root` names the root by name, the cost to reach it and the root port — both rendered from the election's own result so the counts cannot drift from the rows `show spanning-tree` prints; `show mac address-table count` totals per VLAN, `dynamic` lists the table this model learns, `static` says plainly that all entries are dynamic, `vlan <id>` filters (with the range refused by name); `show ip arp <address>` answers for one address and, when the cache has nothing, says the cache holds N addresses and that a ping would learn it. A router asking for a tree is refused with why. Three help rows updated to advertise the new shapes. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/show-keywords.test.js` (40 assertions: summary counts by role and state, agreement with the table's own rows, root vs non-root views, root port matching the table's role, MAC totals matching the row count, dynamic/static/vlan/count spellings and their refusals, ARP by address with the same MAC the table prints, missing-address refusal, two-address refusal, keyword-shaped refusals, router refusal, trailing-word refusals) |
| `mtu`, `speed` and `duplex` — three settings the engine used and the console could not reach | **`mtu 1400`, `speed 100` and `duplex full` were all `% Unrecognized interface command`**, on a router port and on a switch port — while the model already carried `iface.mtu`, `iface.speed` and `iface.duplex`, already derived a **path MTU** from the MTU (minimum over the path) and already derived a **spanning-tree path cost** from the speed. So an operator could read `MTU 1500` out of a flow's MSS and had no way to change it, and could watch a 1G port cost 20000 while having no way to say the port is really 100M | **Wire the console to the numbers the engine reads.** `mtu <68-9216>` sets and enforces the port's frame size — an oversized egress frame is dropped naming both sizes (`1 frame of 134 bytes exceeds the 100-byte MTU on Gi0/0`), and `pathMtu()` feeds the same number into a flow's MSS; `speed <mbps>\|auto` is validated against the port's own `speedCaps` (refusing with the caps listed) and becomes the spanning-tree path cost (`speed 100` on a 1G port: 20000 → 200000); `duplex full\|auto` is stored, shown and dumped, while **`half` is refused by naming what the model lacks** (no collision domain, no backoff, no shared medium — a "half" port would behave exactly like a full one while claiming otherwise). `show interfaces <port>` separates configured from inherited (`(configured)` / `(default)` / `(negotiated)`), the dump carries only the lines actually typed, `no` forms report what they restored, and the three verbs (and their `no` forms) are rows the `?` answers. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/port-physical.test.js` (44 assertions: MTU set, range and word refusals, the ping failing with a named accounted drop, restore, speed changing the tree cost and back, hardware caps refusal, half-duplex refusal, defaults vs configured in `show interfaces`, dump round-trip, and `?` at every depth) |
| The `no` half of the command set was invisible to `?` — 30 removal branches, 9 rows | **`no vlan ?`, `no ip nat ?`, `no spanning-tree ?`, `no bgp neighbor ?` and `no ip ospf ?` all answered `% No completions for …`** while every one of those commands worked. The engine had 30 `no` branches and 9 help rows, so removal was the one part of the command set an operator had to already know — the console could set a thing and not show the way back. Writing the rows found two further defects: **`no encapsulation dot1q` did not exist at all** (a subinterface's tag could be set and never taken back), and **a refusal that knew the reason also claimed not to recognise the words** — `ipv6 nd suppress-ra` on a switch answered `% Unrecognized interface command: …` followed by the true reason, burying the answer under a false claim | **Every removal is a row in the mode that accepts it**, so `?` answers at the depth a reader reaches (`no vlan ?` → `Next: <id>`, `no span?` → `Then: vlan`). `no encapsulation dot1q` now reverts the tag to the VLAN the subinterface number infers and names the tag it took off; the interface fall-through prints `Unrecognized interface command` **only** when the global handler had nothing to say (a single generic refusal), so a reason is never prefixed with a denial. The translation table joins the tables that can be emptied: `clear ip nat translation [*\|<inside-local>]` clears entries, keeps the rules, and reports "was already empty" the second time. **Evidence:** a source-driven guard in `test/no-forms.test.js` reads the mode dispatchers and requires a `no …` help row for every `no` branch (so the next command cannot repeat the omission), plus 44 assertions covering the `?` depths, tag removal both ways, the two refusal wordings, and the new clear verb |
| NAT was fully modelled and completely unconfigurable — and ICMP crossed it untranslated | **`ip nat inside source static` / `… list <acl> interface <if> overload` / `ip nat inside` / `ip nat outside` were all `% Unrecognized`**, and `show ip nat translations` told the operator NAT was *"configured from the topology"* — a model whose purpose is being driven from a console pointing at the editor instead. Worse, a lab built the only way it could be built **could never show a translation for the commonest test there is**: `_natOutbound` required `frame.l4`, and an ICMP echo carries `frame.icmp`, so a ping crossed the NAT box untouched and the table stayed empty | **Give the console the commands the library already answers.** `ip nat inside\|outside` marks a port's side (movable, removable, refused on a device that does not route); `ip nat inside source static <local> <global>` and `ip nat inside source list <acl> interface <if> overload` write real rules, validated where typed (list must exist and is named with the ones that do, interface must exist and is named with the ports that do, that port must have an address, overloading onto the *inside* port is refused with why, missing/extra words quote the synopsis); removal takes the words that created the rule and prints it back; the dump carries roles and rules and pastes back. **The data path was fixed too:** ICMP gets an L4 view (`frame.icmp.id` as the flow key, written back to the header, not a fabricated transport header) so a ping is really translated, the cached translation table is dropped when a rule is written so a rule added after traffic takes effect, and a declared role is **enforced** (translation only when leaving by an `outside` port, resolved through the next hop exactly as `_forwardL3` resolves it) while a topology-built lab with no roles behaves as before. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/nat-cli.test.js` (68 assertions: the rule written and stored, an ICMP translation appearing in the table with real hit counts, a late rule taking effect, static both directions, nine distinct refusals, role moves/removals and the switch refusal, roles enforced on *and* off and restored, removal to empty, and the dump pasting back with its ACL and interface intact) |
| The link-state database printed the interface table, and a BGP peer had no detail view | **`show ip ospf database` and `show ip ospf interface` printed byte-for-byte the same output** - the process, its interfaces and a neighbour count - so the command whose whole purpose is the link-state database answered with a different table, and an unknown sub-word (`show ip ospf routes`) was answered with it too. `show ip bgp neighbors` printed **the summary table again**, so per-peer detail - which peer, what did it give me, why is it down - had no command at all. And `show ip bgp summary` on a freshly configured peer answered `0 of 0 neighbour(s) established` plus **"run a simulation"** with a simulator attached, because nothing had asked the simulator to evaluate the peers yet | **Ask the model what it actually has.** `show ip ospf database` renders the link-state view from the graph the SPF ran on - per area, each advertised link with its cost, the adjacency it produced (`FULL`, `stub`, `passive`), the peer's own link state, and the prefixes the database installed (read from `ospfRoutes`, the same list `show ip route ospf` uses) - behind a header that states plainly that this model keeps no aged LSAs, rather than printing invented ages and sequence numbers. A peer in another area is named as excluded. `show ip bgp neighbors [<address>]` gives per-peer detail (remote AS, eBGP/iBGP, state, hold time from the library, installed-prefix count) and prints **`Reason`** when a session is not established; an address that is not a peer is refused with the peers that are. Both shows refresh from the simulator first, so the first question after configuring a peer answers with the state now instead of promising a simulation that is already running, and unknown sub-words are refused by naming the ones that exist. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/ospf-bgp-detail.test.js` (37 assertions: the two shows no longer matching, every database row against real adjacency state, no fabricated LS age/sequence number, per-area filtering and the mismatch line, no-OSPF answer, the interface alias and unknown sub-word, per-peer detail agreeing with the summary count, a down peer with its reason, and the first-question refresh) |
| An access list could be built by number only, never named, never applied by name, and never counted | `ip access-list extended BLOCK` was `% Unrecognized config command`, so a list could only be built one numbered line at a time; a list that *had* a name could not be applied, because `ip access-group BLOCK in` ran `parseInt("BLOCK")` and answered **`% Invalid access list number: BLOCK`**; and no rule ever carried a hit count - `show access-lists` printed a list of intentions, which is the one thing it cannot be when the question is *which line ate my packet*. Numbering was 1-based position, so `no <sequence>` had no meaning and a line could not be named at all | **The named block, and the counter.** `ip access-list standard\|extended <name>` opens a real sub-mode (`config-std-nacl` / `config-ext-nacl`) with `permit\|deny` lines, `no <sequence>`, `no <entry>`, `show` from inside, and `no ip access-list <name>` that reports the bindings it takes with it. Sequence numbers are IOS's step of 10 and they **persist**, so a number is a stable name for a line. The data path counts every match, including the **implicit deny** (the line that actually ate the packet), `show access-lists` prints `(N matches)`, and `clear access-list counters [<id\|name>]` zeroes them and reports how many - evidence you cannot zero is evidence you cannot date. Bindings are keyed by the list's name so a named list can be applied; `access-group` refuses a third word, a list that does not exist, and names the lists that do. Entry identity moved to one function (`acl.entryKey`) after a live counter made `no access-list 130 deny ip any any` refuse a line that was plainly there. The dump emits ACL blocks **before** the interface blocks that bind them - pasting in order otherwise binds a list that does not exist yet and silently drops the binding - and a pasted block ends at the next unindented command, while a refused command never moves the session. **Evidence:** `engine/acl.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/named-acl-counters.test.js` (58 assertions: the sub-mode and prompt, standard vs extended, applying by name, per-line and implicit-deny counters, clearing one list and both spellings with the privileged check, removal by number and by meaning including a used line, dump round-trip with the binding, and every refusal wording), plus updated `test/acl-remove.test.js` and `test/acl-data.test.js` |
| A spanning tree you could watch but not choose, and a root the table never named | `spanning-tree vlan 1 root primary`, `spanning-tree vlan 1 priority 4096` and interface `spanning-tree cost 19` were all `% Unrecognized`, so the tree was decided by **device creation order and cable speed alone** - the root could not be influenced at all. `show spanning-tree` printed roles and states with **no root anywhere**, while its own help row promised "root bridge, port roles and states". Two defects sat underneath, visible only once cost could be configured: **priority `0`** (legal, wins every election) was thrown away by a truthiness check, and the path cost charged the port a BPDU *left* by while the root-port choice charged the port it *arrived* on, so the two steps disagreed and `spanning-tree cost` set on the port you care about moved nothing | **Choose the root, and name it.** `spanning-tree vlan <id> priority <n>` (0-61440 step 4096, refused with the rule) and `... root primary\|secondary` (answers with the priority it chose: 24576 / 28672, lowered when the current root is already lower) with a `no` form that reports what it replaced; per-port `spanning-tree cost <n>`, honoured by the election on the **receiving** port, which is the port configured. `show spanning-tree` gains `Root bridge:` and `This bridge:` lines (priority, bridge ID, the root's name, `root path cost`) plus a `Cost` column, and a blocked port now explains itself (`Fa0/1 is alternate: SW2 reaches the root for the same 20000, and the tie goes to the lower bridge ID`). `port-priority`, `portfast` and `bpduguard` are refused by naming what the model lacks; a router is refused with "does not bridge". The dump carries both lines and pastes back to the same tree. **Evidence:** `engine/stp.js` (path cost charged on the receiving port, `pathCost` exposed), `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/stp-config.test.js` (52 assertions: root naming and bridge IDs, priority and root primary/secondary moving the election, priority 0, cost changing path cost and roles, every refusal by name, router refusal, dump round-trip, and the library rule stated at unit level) |
| A box could not ping its own address, and one VRRP group had two masters | `ping 10.0.0.1` typed on the router that owns 10.0.0.1 answered **0 percent** with **no drop event anywhere**: the echo was routed out of the port it was addressed to, ARP'd for itself and died - the first check an operator runs after configuring an address. The same defect left a VRRP **master unable to ping its own virtual address**, the one it advertises to the LAN. Separately, after the master's cable was pulled both routers reported `State: Master` for the same group, because the election's `up` flag asked only whether the port was enabled and had an address, never whether the cable was still in it. `show vrrp` made it worse twice: the Master column printed the *asking* device's own name beside `State: Backup` (a row contradicting itself), and a backup said "no group holds its address yet - run a simulation" with a simulator attached and its peer holding the group | **Answer locally, and ask the cable.** `_sendIcmp` short-circuits when the destination is an address the box holds - configured, SVI, or a claim from the VRRP election - emitting the reply without a frame reaching a wire (so `show interfaces counters` stays at "no port has carried a frame", which is what makes it a loopback rather than a lucky round trip). The election's `up` now includes `lineUp` and the link's state, so a failed master gives the group up, and **preemption** returns it. `show vrrp` names the group's real holder and, when the group moved, says why (`R1: Gi0/0 has no line, so group 1 moved to R2`; a shut port says `is administratively down`), while the nothing-claimed note distinguishes "not converged" from "no simulator". **Evidence:** `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/ping-self-and-vrrp.test.js` (28 assertions: self-ping on a port, on another port and on an SVI, counters untouched, an address nobody holds still failing, the master pinging its VIP, the backup holding no claim, a master column that names the holder, cable-pull and port-shutdown failover with exactly one master, the reason line, and preemption putting the group back) |
| A DHCP lease could be watched, never renewed, never given back | `ip address dhcp` and `show ip dhcp binding` existed, so the client half of the exchange was readable and untouchable: **`renew dhcp` and `release dhcp` both answered `% Unrecognized command`** - the two things an operator does with a lease had no command, and "the address is stuck" had no console answer. The lease was also answered two ways depending on what had been typed first: a fresh client's `show ip interface brief` said **`unassigned`** while `show ip dhcp binding` printed the lease, and only after a ping did the brief agree - because the address arrives when the ACK is delivered, and delivering it is part of deriving the control planes, which the address tables never asked for | **The verbs, with both tables written by one call.** `renew dhcp [<port>]` and `release dhcp [<port>]`, each refusing a non-client port and a port that does not exist by name. A release writes the client's address and the server's binding together in `releaseDhcpLease()`, and marks the port **released** so the lease pass does not hand the address straight back on the next refresh (renew, or re-issuing `ip address dhcp`, is the way back). A renewal by hand forces the exchange instead of answering "still valid", because it is an instruction. Any `show` that prints an address now derives the planes, and the DHCP plane tracks whether its queue has been run (`_planesDhcp`, as LLDP and RA already did) so the first question answers with the address. **Evidence:** `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/dhcp-client.test.js` (32 assertions: the first question, renew reporting address and length, a renewal that really reaches the server, release clearing both tables and the pool count, staying released, re-acquiring, the three refusal wordings, and a 60s lease expiring at a later instant with the cable pulled). One of my own errors is in the test as a comment: I first asserted `lease 2` was two seconds, when IOS spells it days/hours/minutes. |
| IPv6 was configurable and untestable, and its words were dropped | `ping 2001:db8:1::2` answered **`% Invalid ping target`** and `ping ipv6 …` answered **`% Unrecognised argument "2001:db8:1::2"`**, so every IPv6 feature in the model - addressing, static routes, router advertisements, SLAAC - could be configured and never tested from the console, although `sim.ping6()` existed all along. `ipv6 unicast-routing` did not exist while the model forwarded IPv6 regardless. In the same corner: `ipv6 address … extra` dropped the word; the same address twice answered success twice; `ipv6 address ::1/128` and **`ff02::1/64`** were accepted, and the multicast one then produced a *connected route for `ff02::/64`* in `show ipv6 route` - a group address worn as a subnet; `ipv6 address` quoted an empty string back (`% Invalid IPv6 address ""`); `ipv6 route … 250` and three `ipv6 nd` verbs dropped trailing words; `show ipv6 interface brief` looked up `brief` as a **port name**; and `ipv6 nd suppress-ra extra` inside an interface block was refused by the *global* handler whose wording the fall-through did not recognise, so a **refusal left the interface block** and the operator's next commands went to the wrong prompt - the note then invented a per-port form (`ip ipv6 ...`) that does not exist | **The door, then the rules.** `ping` accepts an IPv6 destination with or without the `ipv6` keyword, reports in the same shape as the IPv4 run, and on failure prints the drops the run recorded (`% IPv6 unicast routing is disabled on M`), so a timeout names its cause. `traceroute` refuses an IPv6 destination by name and points at `ping` rather than calling the address malformed. `ipv6 unicast-routing` exists and is a **real switch** on forwarding, with `show ipv6 route` warning that its rows are configured but not forwarding; it starts on, which is stated rather than implied. `ipv6 address` validates every word where it is typed (incomplete, no trailing words, loopback/multicast/link-local/unspecified refused by name, duplicate and duplicate-subnet refused naming the other port, replacement named as a limitation); `ipv6 route` and the `ipv6 nd` verbs refuse words they do not use. Any `%` line from the global fall-through now counts as a refusal, so a refusal no longer changes mode, and the note names a per-port form only where one exists. `show ipv6 interface brief` is a real table. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/simulator.js`, `test/ipv6-console.test.js` (48 assertions: both ping spellings, link-local target, failure that names the reason, forwarding switched off and on across a **transit** router with the table still listed, a host refusing the command, every argument rule, mode retained on refusal, the brief table including a shut port, and the traceroute refusal) |
| A logical interface's state, and its counters, disagreed with the data path | A subinterface whose parent was shut reported **`up`/`up`** in `show ip interface brief` while a ping to its own address answered **0 percent** - the panel was wrong, not the packet. `show interfaces <sub>` said `status: up` with no reason, and every frame was attributed to the physical port, so the same command reported **`input : 0 packets`** after pings that answered 100 percent. Three state sites (`show ip interface brief`, `show interfaces`, `show ipv6 interface`) each answered from `i.enabled` alone | **One state, borrowed from what carries the interface.** `computeSviState()` — the pass that already derives an SVI's line from VLAN membership — now also derives a subinterface's from its parent (administratively down, no cable, or parent gone, each recorded as `lineStateReason`), and a single `_ifaceState()` helper feeds all three columns. Frames counted on a subinterface as well as its parent, on the way out (the interface the route chose) and on the way in (the tag the frame carries). **Evidence:** `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/logical-iface-state.test.js` (29 assertions: healthy, parent shut, restored, cable pulled vs port shut as different facts, subinterface shut alone, counters with a quiet sibling as the discriminator, and an SVI regression guard) |
| A help placeholder rewrote the command line | `switchport trunk allowed vlan all` - the spelling the help row documents - answered **`% cannot parse VLAN token "all>"`**. The help-to-forms lexer used `WORD = /[^\s\[\]\|]+/`, which does not exclude angle brackets, so `<list\|all>` became the two keywords `<list` and `all>`; abbreviation expansion rewrites a typed word to the documented one, so `all` was sent on as `all>`. Two neighbours in the same corner: a router's `show running-config` emitted three `switchport` lines for the port carrying its subinterfaces, which the same device refuses (`does not switch`) - a dump that cannot restore the device; and `interface Gi0/0.20` silently took VLAN 20 from the number while the dump printed `encapsulation dot1q 20` as though it had been typed | **Lex a bracketed placeholder as one token** (so it is a value slot, never a keyword), plus a sweep asserting no token of any help form in any mode contains a bracket. A dump emits `switchport` only for a device that bridges; the tagging requirement is stated once by the subinterface's own line. The subinterface answer names the inference it made. **Evidence:** `engine/abbrev.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/help-placeholders.test.js` (20 assertions: the documented spelling, the bracket sweep, a router dump pasted into a fresh router and compared line for line, the creation message, and a trunk that admits VLAN 10 but not VLAN 20 - 100% then 0%, then 100% once admitted, which is what makes "the tag is real" mean something) |
| `ip address` accepted addresses no interface can hold | Five lies in one command, all answered "IP address set": `0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0` became an interface address of **0.0.0.0/0** (a default route worn as a port); `10.0.0.255 255.255.255.0` took the subnet's **broadcast** address; `10.0.0.1 255.0.255.0` printed **/8** because `prefixOf()` counted the leading ones of a mask that is not a mask; the **same address** could sit on two interfaces; and a **second interface in one subnet** was accepted. Then `ip address <ip> <mask> secondary` stored the address and dropped the word, so the answer claimed the whole line was understood. Two commands elsewhere had the same hole: `_bgp` defined its own `isIp` regex (`/^\d{1,3}(\.\d{1,3}){3}$/`), so `neighbor 300.0.0.1 remote-as 65002` was accepted and written to running-config; `ip name-server` had the same regex; and `vrrp 1 preempt extra` answered "preempt enabled" | **The rules a router address actually has, checked where it is typed.** `net.isContiguousMask()` (new, in the library) refuses a mask that is not a run of ones; the network and broadcast addresses of the interface's own subnet are refused by name with the nearest host offered (skipped for /31 and /32, which have neither); `0.0.0.0` and `255.255.255.255` are refused outright; a duplicate address names the interface that holds it; an overlapping subnet names the other interface and the reason ("two interfaces in one subnet is ambiguous" - it is the question every ping asks). The overlap comparison is **unsigned**: a signed one made 192.168.0.0/24 "overlap" 10.0.0.0/24, which my own first draft did and the test now guards both ways. `secondary` is answered as a limitation with the subinterface that does the job, not as an unexpected word. `_bgp` and `ip name-server` use `net.isIp()`, so a 300 octet is not an address anywhere. **Evidence:** `engine/net.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `test/address-rules.test.js` (39 assertions). One existing test (`port-args.test.js`) had been configuring a second interface in an occupied subnet and was narrowed to its actual subject. |
| Two learnt tables that could be read and never emptied | The ARP cache and the MAC table fill up as traffic crosses a lab, and there was no way to empty either: `clear arp`, `clear ip arp` and `clear mac address-table` all answered **`% Unrecognized command`**, while `clear counters`, `clear interface counters` and `clear logging` existed. So the family was half-built, and the two members an operator reaches for to watch *learning* happen a second time were the two missing - the only way to see a fresh ARP request was to rebuild the topology. `clear ip route *` and `clear ip bgp *` are a different case: this model derives those tables, so there is nothing to flush - but `% Unrecognized command` tells an IOS operator the tool is missing a feature and sends them looking | **Clear what the model actually holds, and say what it does not.** `clear arp [<port>]` / `clear ip arp` and `clear mac address-table [interface <port>]` empty the real tables and report the count, or say `was already empty - nothing to clear` rather than claiming a clear; the next packet re-learns the entries, which is what makes them a reset of learning rather than a broken cache. A **port-scoped** clear is exactly as honest as the table it reads: the ARP cache holds address -> MAC only, so the port is derived the same way `show arp` derives it, and an entry behind a switch (shown `-`) is not attributable and therefore survives - asserted, because the tempting implementation clears everything and looks successful. A router refuses the MAC clear by naming the reason (`does not bridge`) and pointing at the table it does have. `clear ip route *` / `clear ip bgp *` now answer with why there is nothing to flush and which question can be answered instead. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/clear-tables.test.js` (27 assertions: fill, clear, refill-by-learning, empty-cache wording, port attribution both ways, the non-bridge refusal, user-exec privilege, and the two reasoned refusals), `docs/CLI.md` |
| `traceroute` accepted options it never read | `traceroute 10.0.0.5 source abc` and `traceroute 10.0.0.5 ttl abc` both printed a **full hop table** and said nothing about the word they dropped. The parser loop looked for `at` and `continue`d past everything else - the shape of "the answer is fine, the answer to what you asked is not". `ping` refused the same words, so the two commands disagreed about whose mistake a word they cannot use is. `ping … at` with no value answered `% Invalid instant: (none)` - a placeholder quoted at the operator. Separately, `show ip arp` - the spelling IOS prints in its own help - answered `% Invalid show command: ip arp` while the shorthand `show arp` worked | **One rule for both commands, and `numeric` actually implemented.** Every word after the destination must be `at <instant>` or `numeric`; anything else is `% Unrecognised argument "<word>" (expected at <ms>\|converged or numeric)` and no hop table is printed. `numeric` suppresses the Device column and the names in the path line - accepting the word while printing names anyway would have been the same silence. `at` with no value is `% Incomplete command: …` for both commands. `show ip arp` is now the same command as `show arp` (same table, same port filter, same instant label). **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `test/exec-options.test.js` (26 assertions, incl. the two-hop path where `numeric` and the named trace are compared line by line) |
| `% [object Object]` — an ACL parse error printed as the shape of the answer | `access-list 110 permit tcp any any eq abc` and `access-list 110 permit icmp any any echo` both answered **`% [object Object]`**. `engine/acl.js` returns `{ error: { code, message } }` and the console did `'% ' + entry.error`, so the operator got the object's `toString()`. The sentence was right there in `message`. While fixing it: a missing port value was quoted as an empty string (`bad port value ""`), and a bare `permit` answered `extended entry needs a protocol …, got ""` — an empty value quoted at the operator, and “needs” wording that read as a complaint about the help row rather than a hint | **Print the message, not the object.** `% access-list <n>: <message>` uses the library's sentence; a missing port is `missing port after "eq"`; a bare `permit` is `a permit or deny with no protocol - e.g. "permit tcp any any eq 443"`; an unknown protocol is quoted. `test/error-text.test.js` sweeps seventeen bad commands and asserts **no** answer contains `[object Object]`, `undefined`, `NaN`, `null` or `(none)` — the class, not just the symptom. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js`, `engine/acl.js`, `test/error-text.test.js` (16 assertions) |
| OSPF areas — an id truncated at its first dot, and a rule the model never applied | `ospf area 255.255.255.255` answered `OSPF area 255` (parseInt stops at the dot), and anything above `2147483647` was refused although the model allows `0-4294967295` — so half the range and the whole RFC 2328 spelling were unreachable. Worse, the area was **never compared**: two routers in areas 1 and 2 formed a FULL adjacency and exchanged routes, while `engine/ospf.js`'s own header states “a link is usable only when both endpoints sit in the same area” — configurable, displayed, inert. `ospf router-id` used a shape regex, so `1.1.1.999` was a router id | **The library's parser, and the rule it was written for.** `_ospfConfig`/`_ipOspf` call `parseAreaId()` (exported for the purpose), `net.isIp()` decides the router id, and a trailing word is refused. The adjacency builder now requires `ai.area === bi.area` and records the excluded link per device, so `show ip ospf neighbor` answers the question an empty table raises: `% R2 on Gi0/0 is in area 2 and this interface is in area 1 - no adjacency without a matching area`. A per-interface override still wins. **Evidence:** `engine/simulator.js`, `engine/cli.js`, `engine/ospf.js`, `test/ospf-area.test.js` (28 assertions: both spellings, the range, the mismatch, the dotted-form equivalence where `parseInt` put one end in area 0, the override, and a 2/2 ping when the areas match) |
| A router did not answer for its own addresses | A ping to a router's address on a port other than the one it arrived by timed out with **no drop event at all**. The event log looked correct at each step: R2 *routed* the packet addressed to itself (`route d2 10.9.9.2 via 10.9.9.2 iface Gi0/1`), then ARP'd for itself on the far port, then nothing. Two defects in one decision: `ownsIpOn()` compared the address's port with the ingress port and refused when they differed (so only the address on the arriving port was ever local), and having accepted the echo, the reply was sent out of the port that held the address — an empty socket. Every router address except one was unreachable from the router that held it, and `ping` reported a silent timeout | **A router owns every address configured on it, and the reply leaves by a port that can carry it.** The L3 check sits after the WAN/LAN role rule (which still refuses a LAN address on a WAN port — asserted), so role separation and NAT are unaffected; the echo reply is now routed back to the sender, falling back to the ingress port. `test/local-delivery.test.js` (12 assertions) proves it from both ends: router-to-router and host-to-router pings to the far address answer 4/4 (they were 0/4), the `icmp-echo` event names `Gi0/0` rather than the address's own port, and `show interfaces counters Gi0/1` on R2 reports **no frames** — the empty socket is observable, which is what makes the assertion non-vacuous. **Evidence:** `engine/device.js` (`ownsIpOn`), `engine/simulator.js` (`_handleLocalIp`), `test/local-delivery.test.js` |
| A DNS record, a DHCP pool value and a hostname — three things accepted before anything judged them | **A DNS record** was stored without checking it against its own type: `dns add www.lab.example A abc` was answered **`Added www.lab.example A abc`** while `validateZone()` in `engine/dns.js` — the library that answers the query — called the same record *not a dotted-quad IPv4 address*. Worse, the resolver then handed `abc` to the data path, so `ping www.lab.example` printed `Sending 1, 100-byte ICMP Echos to abc` and reported timeouts against a value no packet can be addressed to. The value was also **one token**, so `dns add note TXT hello world` stored `hello` and dropped `world` without a word, an `MX` record was stored with no preference (which the library rejects), and `show running-config` emitted `dns add mail MX mail.lab.test` — a line the console would itself refuse, so the dump could not be pasted back. **A DHCP pool** was silent per command by design ("nothing is validated per command" — correct for an incomplete pool), but that silence also covered values that were already wrong: `network abc`, `default-router abc`, `dns-server abc` and `range 10.6.0.50 10.6.0.10` were stored and reported at `exit` as `% E_DHCP_RANGE_ORDER: DHCP pool BAD: rangeStart 10.6.0.50 is above rangeEnd 10.6.0.10.` — two commands later, in validator codes and field names. **A bare `hostname`** printed nothing and cleared `config.hostname`, while `device.name` kept its old value: the two fields a comment in that very handler promises "cannot disagree" disagreed | **Judged by the library that owns the answer, at the point of typing.** `dns add` takes the rest of the line as the value, asks for an MX preference explicitly, then appends the record and re-validates the zone: a record that introduces an error — a bad address, a duplicate, a `CNAME` sharing its owner name — is removed and the library's own sentence is printed (`% www.lab.example A: "abc" is not a dotted-quad IPv4 address.`). Errors the zone already had are compared away, so a zone containing a hand-written bad record can still be repaired. `MX` preference is stored as a number and printed by both `show dns` and `show running-config`, and a fresh-device paste of the dump reproduces the zone byte for byte. `_resolveHost()` refuses to pass a non-address to the data path even when a hand-written zone contains one. In the pool sub-mode, *incomplete* still waits for `exit` but a value that is not an address — and a reversed range — is refused on the spot in console words, and the `exit` messages lost their `E_DHCP_*` prefixes and field names (`no address range was given - "range <start> <end>" is required.`). A bare `hostname` is `% Incomplete command: hostname <name>`. Found while doing it: `dns.js` messages ended in a doubled period (`… at dup.example.test..`) where an FQDN met a full stop. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js` (`_dns` add path, `_dhcpPoolMode`, `_resolveHost`, running-config writer, `show dns`), `engine/dhcp.js` (message wording), `engine/dns.js` (`add()`), `test/refused-where-typed.test.js` (56 assertions, including a running-config round-trip into a fresh device), `docs/CLI.md` |
| `ip route` — four words, none of them checked, and one of them bricked the device | The command stored its arguments verbatim. `ip route abc 255.0.0.0 1.1.1.1` was answered `Route added: abc/8 via 1.1.1.1`, and from then on **every** lookup on that router threw, because `routeTo()` parses the stored destination: `ping 10.0.0.5` answered `% Error: Invalid IPv4 address: abc` — a message about a typo from three commands earlier, surfacing in an unrelated command, with no way to see which route caused it. A non-address next hop was installed the same way (`via abc`), `ip route … 1.1.1.1 250` stored `250` as an *interface* (the fourth word is an interface, not an IOS distance), a route with no next hop was installed as `via on-link`, and `ip route <net> <mask> <iface>` was installed and listed — both of the last two forward **nothing** (0/4 replies, against 4/4 for the same lab with a next hop). `no ip route` answered `Route removed` for routes that were never there, `ip default-gateway abc` stored the word, and `net.isIp()` called `10.0.0.999` an address so the parse behind it threw at the console | **Each word is checked where it is typed.** `_ipRoute()` validates the network and mask as addresses (`isIp` + `prefixOf`), the next hop as an address, and the optional fourth word as an interface that exists — with a numeric fourth word explaining that this model has no per-route distance rather than looking like a typo. A route with no next hop and an interface-only route are both refused, the second with its reason (`this model forwards to a next-hop address`, plus the measured 0/4-vs-4/4 evidence in the docs). `_noIpRoute()` matches on what was stored, names the route it removed and refuses one that is not configured, listing what is. `ip default-gateway` is validated the same way. `net.isIp()` now parses (regex first pass, `ipToInt` decides) so `10.0.0.999` is not an address anywhere in the codebase. Found while doing it: running-config printed an interface-only route with a doubled space. **Evidence:** `engine/cli.js` (`_ipRoute`, `_noIpRoute`, `_defaultGateway`, `_ifaceNamed`, `_ifaceList`), `engine/net.js` (`isIp`), `test/static-route.test.js` (53 assertions, ending in a 4/4-ping proof that the refused routes forward nothing and the accepted one forwards), `docs/CLI.md` |
| The LAG mode seam, `channel-group` and `description` — three verbs that answered with something other than the truth | `channel-group 1 mode active` stored the console word verbatim while `engine/lag.js` knows only `lacp-active`/`lacp-passive`/`static`, so **every use** of the command printed `% Warning: LAG lag1: unknown mode "active" (expected one of lacp-active, lacp-passive, static)` and the validator then raised `E_LAG_BAD_MODE` — a warning about the console's own vocabulary, and `mode on` (the one mode with no LACP in it) could not be configured at all. `channel-group abc` was accepted as a group id. `no channel-group 9` answered "Removed Fa0/2 from any channel group" for a group the port had never joined. `description` with no text answered **`Description set`** — a confirmation that wrote an empty string, i.e. cleared the description it claimed to set — and `no description` printed nothing, so a clear and a typo looked the same. `show etherchannel` printed the stored identifier as the protocol | **One translation table, read by the command and the table.** `LAG_CLI_MODES` maps `active→lacp-active`, `passive→lacp-passive`, `on→static` (and accepts the library spellings), `lagModeWord()` renders `LACP active` / `LACP passive` / `static` for `show etherchannel`, and the group id is validated (`% Channel group id must be a number, not "abc"`, `1-4096`) with a trailing word refused. `no channel-group <id>` removes the port from *that* group and reports what is left (`(1 port left)` / `- the group is gone`), refusing a group it is not in; | The wiki's own "getting started" page, and the publish step behind it | The wiki is the first thing a new user reads and had never been checked against a running tree; `CONTRIBUTING.md` warned that `wiki:publish` crashes when there is nothing to publish, but that was the only failure mode documented | **Worked the page top to bottom against the code.** `engines: {"node": ">=18"}` with an empty `dependencies` block, so "no runtime dependencies" holds; `npm run smoke` exists and passes (18 `ok` lines, ending `HTTP smoke passed`); the 1G-in-a-10G-cage claim was already measured in an earlier pass; and the "impossible cable" example is real — mating SC/UPC to SC/APC throws with the part number in the message: *A hybrid patch cord (`SMSCSX`, sc-upc to sc-apc, 0.3 dB insertion loss) joins these two polishes: declare it on the link (`patchCord: "SMSCSX"`) and the run becomes buildable.* No claim on the page was wrong. **Two things the page could not tell you, both now written**: `npm run smoke` picks a free port with `listen(0)` and points `PF_DATA` at a throwaway directory, so it is safe with your own lab open (added to the wiki template, `CI.md` and regenerated); and `wiki:publish` can **hang** as well as crash — it printed six `updated` lines and then nothing for five minutes, because `core.autocrlf` had already made the copy identical, so the commit had nothing to do. The manual clone-copy-commit-push path is now in `CONTRIBUTING.md`; it is how this iteration's wiki change landed (commit `3e1fba3`), and drift is 0. **And the checkers earned their keep on the way out**: once the publish run was done, the sweep reported two defects in my own prose from earlier passes — an anchor written `#51-dhcp` where the heading slugs to `#51-dhcp--in-the-data-path-since-1051`, and a DHCP-lease row carrying four cells in a three-column table. Both fixed; both had survived earlier "clean" runs, which is the reason the tooling work of the last three passes was worth doing. |
| The iteration log itself, rendering as garbage | Seventeen rows of this table had stopped being rows: six were written without their closing pipe, ten carried unescaped `\|` inside a code span, and one had been split across two lines mid-predicate (`… `canTerminate` was `l3` / `\| isHost()`, so a switch bridged the packet …`). A table row that is not a row does not fail any test — it renders as a paragraph under the table and the reader loses a whole entry | **Repaired all seventeen mechanically, then verified the render.** The split row was rejoined at the pipe that caused the break; the missing closing pipes were added; the in-code pipes were escaped (`\|`), which is the form GitHub renders back as a pipe inside a code span. Also normalised the two rows that still differed after escaping by keeping the first two separators and escaping the rest. `md-check.js` now reports 0 shape problems for this file; before, 17 rows plus one wrapped row in `docs/CLI.md`. **The citation sweep still flags five suites that do not exist, deliberately**: four of them are named *by* the row above about phantom citations (that row is about nonexistent suites, so of course they do not exist), and `poe-derived` belongs to work in flight by another agent in this tree, which is also why `engine/cli.js` and `test/help-*.test.js` are modified and `docs/CLI.md` carries two broken anchors I have not touched. The tree moved under this pass — HEAD is `1140bc2`, 195 suites, `docs/CLI.md` now 3,700 lines — so the sweep matters more than usual: a shared log written by several hands needs a checker, not a proofreader. |
| The engine module map — two modules missing and a row that was not a row | The map claimed "Thirty files, one job each" while `engine/` holds **32**; `abbrev.js` and `portsecurity.js` had no row at all, and one row began `\| \`device.js\` — \`addSvi()\` …` — a paragraph of `device.js` detail that had lost its cells and was rendering as a stray line under the table | **Counted files against rows instead of reading.** 32 files, 30 rows, two absent (`abbrev.js`, `portsecurity.js`) — so the row count and the prose count were both wrong in the same direction, which is why nobody noticed.
