| Two anchors in the command index that went nowhere, and a checker that cried wolf | `CLI.md`'s command table linked `show vlan name` to `#vlan-lifecycle-creating-refusing-and-removing` and `show lldp neighbors` to `#l2-admin-state-the-switch-was-told-not-to-speak`; neither heading has ever existed in that file — the slugs were lifted from iteration-log titles in this document. Meanwhile the wrapped-row check kept flagging a *paragraph*, which sent me looking for a broken table that was not there | **Both links repointed at headings that exist** (`#vlans`, `#one-lldp-view-and-three-questions-it-could-not-answer`), and the check taught to tell a table from prose. The false positive was real: a sentence that wraps immediately after a pipe inside a code span begins a line with `\|`, which is exactly what a table row looks like — but with no separator line above it, it renders as text. The check now only considers lines **inside** a real table (a `\|` line followed by a `\|‑‑‑\|` separator), and it still fails on a deliberately wrapped cell, which I re-proved on a scratch file containing both cases: prose-with-pipe ignored, wrapped row caught. Two lessons in one pass: **a dead anchor is invisible to every renderer until somebody clicks it**, and **a checker that reports a real-looking defect in the wrong place costs more than one that reports nothing** — you go and edit prose that was fine. |
| A coverage count I "corrected" into being wrong | `CLI.md` said 47 table rows and 69 `E_` codes named anywhere; I measured 71, decided the page was stale by two, and rewrote it — then ran the shell recount and got 69 again | **My measurement was the wrong instrument.** I had asked, for each of the 145 engine codes, whether the string appeared in the page — and `E_PORT` appears inside `E_PORT_PROTOCOL`, so substring membership counted codes the page never names. Tokenising (`grep -o \| sort -u`) gives 69, unchanged, and 47 rows: the page had been right and I nearly made it wrong. The paragraph now states the numbers **with the commands that produce them** and says why they must be counted as tokens, because the next person to check will reach for `in` exactly as I did. Re-verified the other direction too: no row in the table names a code the engine does not define (47 rows, all real). A doc page that quotes a count is only as good as the sentence next to it explaining how to re-derive it. |
| The README's feature table, four subsystems behind the engine | It described L2 as "MAC table, learning, flooding" with no port security, and the CLI row listed verbs that stopped at `router bgp` — while the console has since grown port security (limits, sticky, three violation modes, aging, `clear`), LLDP in both directions, static ARP, `ip routing`, `mtu`/`speed`/`duplex`, `power inline`, `lldp run`, `ip domain-lookup` and IOS-style abbreviation. Each one was evidenced by its own suite in `test/` | **Added them, then checked the one claim I had invented.** `port-security.test.js`, `lldp-admin.test.js`, `static-arp.test.js`, `abbrev.test.js`, `svi*.test.js` and `clear-*.test.js` all exist, and every verb is in the help under `grep helpLine` — so the additions are real. But I also wrote that an SVI "reports `up` only when a port carries it", which the probe contradicted: create `interface vlan 10` on an L3 switch and `show ip interface brief` prints **`Vlan10 10.0.10.1/24 YES manual up down`** — Status `up` because the interface is administratively enabled, Protocol `down` because nothing carries the VLAN. Cable a port into VLAN 10 and it becomes `up up`; shut that port and it returns to `up down` with the port reading `administratively down`. So the *Protocol* column is the honest one, not the Status. `CLI.md` already said this correctly ("shows `up/down` in the brief and installs no connected route"), which is how I caught myself: the page was right and my summary of it was wrong. README now repeats the page's wording. |
| `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` — the PoE section had drifted from three directions at once | Its `show power inline` transcript had no **Admin** column (added when `power inline auto\|never` landed), its budget-refusal example named a hostname the engine never prints, its "`Used` is the rounded total" detail said a 12.95 W port prints `Used : 13 W`, and the PSE-without-budget section still claimed the console answers "neither sources nor draws PoE" | **Re-measured all four and rewrote what the measurements contradicted.** The table now carries `Admin` with the three outcomes spelled out (`on`, `off (administratively)`, `denied (budget exhausted)`). The refusal names the **device specification** and that spec's own port spelling — *"UniFi Switch Lite 16 PoE PoE budget exceeded: 2 powered ports draw 60 W of a 45 W budget (port1, port2)"* — not the hostname you configured. `Used` is **truncated** to one decimal, not rounded: 12.95 W prints `12.9 W`, and 7 + 12.95 prints `19.9 W` where rounding would say `20.0`, with `Available` derived from the printed figure (240 − 19.9 = 220.1). And a PSE with no budget no longer pretends to be a non-PSE: it prints *"R1 declares poe-pse, 802.3at, 802.3af, passive-poe in its catalogue entry, but no port record on it carries power-out … That is a gap in the device specification, not a state of the device"*. The numbers moved too — **85 of the 102** `poe-pse` devices carry no budget (57 MikroTik, 28 Ubiquiti), not 60 of 102 with 3 Ubiquiti. Also recorded the powered-device view that names the PSE's port (`12.95 W from SW1 port 1 - powered`), since the port spelling belongs to the PSE's spec. |
| The device schema documented thirteen fewer keys than the catalogue actually uses | `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` lists every field it gates on, and a survey of all 1,584 files found **thirteen more keys in the wild** — `insertionLossDb`, `formFactor`, `splitRatio`, `dualRole`, `comboPorts`, `source`, and a cluster of passive-optical figures (`returnLossDb`, `channelCount`, `channelPlan`, `monitorTapDb`, `attenuationRangeDb`…). A contributor copying an existing spec meets them with no statement of whether they do anything | **Measured which are read and which are decoration, because that is the only distinction that matters.** `grep -rn "\.<key>" engine/ web/ server/` puts `insertionLossDb` (optical loss), `formFactor` (cage fit), `splitRatio` (splitter fan-out) and `dualRole` in the real column; `comboPorts`, `source` and the whole passive-optical cluster are read nowhere — the pairing they describe is expressed by per-port `comboWith`/`comboRole`, and the optical figures annotate parts the model does not simulate. Those annotation keys sit almost entirely on `devices/fs/` CWDM muxes, VOAs and attenuators. **Then the `source` key turned out to be a real defect in a number**: 22 specs point at their datasheet with `source` instead of `sourceUrl`, and none carry both — measured by walking every file (`{both: 0, onlySource: 22, onlyUrl: 1234, neither: 328}`) — so the long-quoted "1,234 of 1,584 specs carry a datasheet URL" undercounts by 22 and the true figure is 1,256. Both spellings are now documented, with the rule that a key has to be read somewhere to matter. |
| Two documents still told the reader that `main` is red — and it is not | `docs/CI.md` opened its known-gaps section with "**Two suites are red on the current tree, so `Test (Node *)` would be red too** … 4 failing assertions, in two files", and `DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` said "`test/spec-vs-description.test.js` fails on exactly those four for that reason". A contributor reading either would go looking for a red build that does not exist | **Ran the two suites instead of trusting the paragraph.** `spec-vs-description` **3 passed, 0 failed**; `canonical-urls` **43 passed, 0 failed**; `scripts/check-canonical-urls.js` prints `OK — 2114 files, every project URL points at https://git.jayw.uk/jaylouisw/packetforge`. Both closed — and only one closed the way the page recommended, which is the part worth keeping: the four Ubiquiti specs still carry `"poeOut": true` on `eth0` and their **descriptions** were corrected to *"1x PoE++ passthrough"* (measured; the port key never moved), so prose and port now agree; `DO-NOT-USE.md` was **exempted** through `canonical.EXEMPT_FILES` rather than deleted, on the reasoning that a file whose purpose is to record a stale URL is not a violation. CI.md now states the measured reading first and keeps the history as *how they closed*; DEVICE_REGISTRY.md's paragraph says the same and draws the general rule — the port key is the model's truth, so when it disagrees with prose, one of the two is wrong and correcting the prose leaves the simulation untouched. |
| Two pages called the CI pipeline "five checks" while listing three jobs — and one contradicted itself in a single sentence | `docs/CI.md` headed its table "Five checks, all required before a merge", and `CONTRIBUTING.md` said "names the **five checks** a pull request has to pass — `Test (Node 18/20/22)`, `Server smoke test`, `Docker build`": five checks, three names, no explanation of the gap. A contributor comparing the page to the Actions list would find three jobs and wonder which two they were missing | **Counted `- name:` under each job in `ci.yml` instead of trusting either page**: `test` **7 steps**, `smoke` **1**, `docker` **3**, over **three jobs** — and the reason for "five" is that the `test` job's Node matrix (`18, 20, 22`) expands one job into three check runs named `Test (Node 18)`, `20`, `22`, which is exactly the three rows the table already had. Neither count was wrong; the page simply never said which noun it was counting. Both now read "**three jobs, five check runs**", the table is headed "Check run", and CONTRIBUTING names the three jobs and notes the matrix expansion, so the arithmetic is visible where the reader needs it. |
| `TRIAGE.md` claimed *every* open issue of the tracker is a labelled device request — and the sweep reads labels it never uses | The page opened with "Every open issue in this repository is a device request — **all 126 of them** — each carrying both the `device` and `device-request` labels", and the number appeared twice more. An absolute like that is exactly what stops a reader checking | **Paged the live API instead**: `x-total-count` says **127**, and walking all 127 issues finds **126 of 127** carrying both labels. The exception is **#1335** ("The console over HTTP has its own counters…"), which carries **no labels at all** and is not a device request — so the page now names it rather than repeating "every". The more useful finding underneath: `sweep-device-requests.js` keys a device request on the **title prefix** `[device]`/`[hardware]`, never on labels, so the sweep is safe over the unfiltered queue and the label claim was never load-bearing. Also recorded the pagination constraint measured while paging: the server caps a page at **50** whatever you ask for (`limit=51` returns 50), `LIMIT_PER_PAGE` is 50, and the loop stops on a short page — so the constant sits on the cap with zero headroom, and a lower cap would silently reduce the sweep to one page. |
| `ARCHITECTURE.md` gave the right total for the API surface with a breakdown that was half wrong | The paragraph said "eleven namespaces … carrying **eighteen call signatures** (counted from the dispatcher: `links` alone has four, `devices` and `flows` two each)". Eleven and eighteen both check out; the parenthetical does not — `flows` carries **four**, not two | **Enumerated the dispatcher with a script instead of reading it**: 15 `parts[3]` blocks, 11 namespaces, and per-block method sets — `links` 4 (`POST` a link, `DELETE` one, the length edit, `…/:linkId/state`), `flows` 4 (`GET` list, `POST` open, `GET` one, `DELETE` one), `devices` 2, and eight single-call namespaces. The 18-versus-15 gap is exactly **three** blocks that answer more than one method; counting methods naively gives 19, and merging the length edit's `PATCH` *or* `POST` — how the table lists it — gives 18. Spot-checked the two flows calls against a live server while there: `GET …/flows` → `{flows: [], counters: {}}` with `200`, `GET …/flows/<bad>` → `404 {error: "flow not found"}`. The page now shows the per-namespace arithmetic so the next reader can check the total instead of trusting it. |
| `CONVERGENCE.md` said the timers are "read from `dev.config`", which is three different shapes and one of them silently does nothing | The page named the keys correctly — `stpForwardDelaySec`, `ospf.helloSec`, `dhcpExchangeSec` — but never said that the OSPF ones are **nested**, and nothing recorded the VRRP group field `advertCs` at all. A reader who follows "read from `dev.config`" literally writes `dev.config.ospfHelloSec`, gets no error, and watches a lab that converges at exactly the default rate | **Set each key and read the timer object back** — eight assertions, all green: flat `{stpForwardDelaySec: 4, stpMaxAgeSec: 11}` → `listenEndMs 4000`, `learnEndMs 8000`, `maxAgeMs 11000`; flat `ospfHelloSec: 2` → **unchanged** `10/40/11000/16000` with no warning; nested `ospf: {helloSec: 2, exchangeSec: 3, spfDelaySec: 9}` → `adjacentAtMs 5000`, `routesAtMs 14000`; `dhcpExchangeSec: 7` → `leaseAtMs 7000`. Then `advertCs`, which the page never mentioned: it is **centiseconds**, validated (`E_ADVERT_RANGE` refuses `0`, `-5`, `2.5`; accepts `200`), `250` gives `advertSec 2.5` and `masterDownMs 8109.375` — and when both fields are set **the two modules disagree**, `vrrp.advertInterval()` preferring `advertCs` (`{advertSec: 3, advertCs: 200}` → `2`) while `convergence.vrrpTimers()` prefers `advertSec` (→ `3`, masterDown `9609.375`), so a lab can show a readiness number its advertisements do not use. Also wrote down that `masterDownInterval(group, skew)` takes the **skew**, not the priority — passing `100` returns `103` seconds. |
| `BGP-POLICY.md` opened with "design specification — not yet implemented" over a blockquote describing code that runs | The status line was written before the session layer landed, and the blockquote below it explains `establishSession()` walking RFC 4271's FSM and refusing with eight `E_*` codes. Read together they say "none of this exists" and "this part exists" in the same screenful | **Checked what actually runs, then split the status in two.** All eight codes are present in `engine/bgp.js`/`engine/simulator.js` (`E_PEER_NOT_ON_SUBNET`, `E_PEER_NOT_FOUND`, `E_PEER_NOT_CONFIGURED`, `E_REMOTE_AS_MISMATCH`, `E_IFACE_DOWN`, `E_NO_ROUTE_TO_PEER`, `E_NO_SOURCE_IP`, `E_POE`), and `comparePaths()` really is the **8 steps** the anchors table claims. Exercised each step to make the claim falsifiable: weight over local-pref, local-pref over AS-path, locally originated, AS-path length, origin, MED, eBGP-over-iBGP, router-id — every row `-1` (first wins), identical paths `0`. The MED row is the interesting one: `AS [65002] med 10` beats `AS [65003] med 20`, so MED **is** compared across different neighbouring ASes — the declared simplification, now measured and pointed at §4.5. Status now says "two layers, one implemented" and names exactly what is still a spec: route-maps, `match`/`set`, communities, MED non-transitivity, `always-compare-med`, `next-hop-self`, the elimination pipeline. |
| `HYBRID-PATCH.md` described `insertionLossBasis` as a field "on every entry" without saying which catalogue, and said the clean fix only "needs an engine change" | The sentence reads as if the device specs carry a loss basis. They do not: `grep -rl insertionLossBasis devices/` finds **zero** of 1,584 files, and the only parts that carry a loss number at all are 13 assemblies holding `insertionLossDb` with no basis field | **Located the field and counted it**: it lives in `catalogue/optics/hybrid-patch-cords.json` — 13 assemblies, all 13 carrying it — split 11 `per-connector-max` (FS.com 0.3 dB ×3, L-com 0.3 ×3, Siemon 0.4 ×2, Panduit 0.5, Corning 0.25, Eaton Tripp Lite 0.3), 1 `per-connector-typical` (3M 0.3) and 1 `iec-61755-1-grade-c-max` (CommScope 0.5), which is exactly the vendor mapping the prose already asserted. Then the sharper part: `engine/optical.js` mentions `insertionLossBasis` **only in a comment** about which fields older entries predate, so the basis-aware branch is not "an engine change away" — nothing reads the field today. Added a measured table of the three budgeting conventions (`8.42` no cord, `8.72` cord as-is, `8.52` delta, `8.12` cord with `connectorPairs: 0`) so the choice is visible as numbers. |
| `FIBRE-CHANNEL.md` stated the FC speed ladders correctly but gave a reader no way to check them, in a file where the obvious check returns `undefined` | `CAGE_SPEEDS` and `CAGE_MEDIA` are **functions**, not objects, so `JSON.stringify(m.CAGE_SPEEDS['fc-sfp'])` prints `undefined` — a reader doing the natural thing concludes the cage is unlisted, when the ladder is right there | **Called them and wrote the results down**: `fc-sfp` → `[2125, 4250, 8500, 10519, 14025]` (2/4/8/10/16G), `fc-sfp28` → `[8500, 14025, 28050]`, `fc-sfpdd` → `[14025, 28050, 57800]`, `fc-qsfp28` → `[28050, 112200]` — each matching the page's table, with the media lists measured alongside (`2gfc-sw`…`16gfc-*`, and the media are `2gfc-sw`, not `fc-2g`). Then found the asymmetry nobody had written down: **the `maxMbps` argument is ignored by the optical and FC ladders** — `sfp(1000)` → `[1000, 2500]`, `fc-sfp(1000)` → the whole ladder — while `rj45` filters to `[10, 100, 1000]`, `wwan(1000)` → `[1000]` and `combo(1000)` → `[10, 100, 1000]` (never offering 2500/5000 at all). So a port's declared ceiling narrows a copper ladder and does not narrow an FC one, which is a real difference between "what could this socket ever do" and what a run negotiates. |
| `MIRRORING.md` contradicted itself about the wiki, and its issue-handling plan named a workflow file that cannot live where it said | The table said "Wiki \| Follows the code \| … mirrors with the repo", while §1 twenty lines later listed "wiki" among the things a git push mirror does **not** copy. And §3 said the redirect workflow "must run **on GitHub**, because GitHub is where the issue is" — true of execution, false of the file, since a file committed on a push mirror is overwritten by the next sync | **Split the wiki row into the two things people mean**: the **source pages** (`docs/wiki/*.md`) are committed and regenerated by `npm run wiki:build`, so they do travel with every push; the **rendered wiki** on either forge lives in its own `<repo>.wiki.git` and a repo mirror never touches it — Forgejo's is published by `npm run wiki:publish` (both npm scripts verified present). For the issue workflow, the correction is where the *file* goes: it must be committed here under `/.github/workflows/` — a directory that does not exist today, deleted in #1059 — and mirrored out, while editing the mirror's issue *settings* is safe because settings are not files. |

And `cf-access-bypass.js` parses only `--apply`/`--check`, so `--help` is ignored and **runs the check**: a reader asking for help gets a network request and a verdict | **Probed the instance anonymously instead of describing it**: browse `200`, `git ls-remote` returns the SHA (so anonymous clone works — the thing the old path-scoped policy broke), `POST …/issues` `401`, and the write paths `/user/settings`, `/jaylouisw/packetforge/settings` and `/repo/create` all `303 → /user/login`. The page now carries that table and says the bare `/settings` 404 is routing, not the bypass. For the script, documented that "dry run" means *no writes to Cloudflare*, not *no I/O* — with the bypass in place it prints `HTTP 200 / PUBLIC — a stranger can read this repository / Nothing to do.` — which is the honest thing to expect from a command whose name looks like help. |

It also needs `FORGEJO_TOKEN` before anything else and exits **2** with *"FORGEJO_TOKEN is not set - the tracker is not readable without it (this is not the same as \"no open requests\")"*, now quoted. `missing-devices.js` turned out to have four flags plus help, to **also scan the tracker by default** (degrading to `not scanned: FORGEJO_TOKEN is not set … the topology scan above is unaffected` with exit 0 and a green gate), to be steerable with `--no-issues`, and to gate CI differently under `--strict`; an unknown flag prints `Unknown flag: --bogus` and exits **2** while `--help` exits **0**. All measured. |
| The WebSocket section was accurate but untested, and it never said how many frames greet a client that has sent nothing | Every claim in it was true when written; the gap was the one a UI bug would come from — a client that treats the first frame after connecting as the answer to its own request | **Drove the socket with a hand-rolled RFC 6455 client instead of trusting the prose.** Upgrade on `/`, `/ws`, `/deep/path` and `/api/topologies` all answer `101`; a handshake with **no `Sec-WebSocket-Key`** gets **zero bytes** and a closed socket rather than a `400`; **two frames arrive unprompted immediately after the handshake**; and three junk frames (`{"type":"bogus"}`, `not json`, `{}`) draw **0** frames in reply over 800 ms. The new sentence is the two-frame one: subscribe first, filter by `type`, and never assume the socket is quiet because you have not written anything yet. Also pinned the saved envelope by creating a board, adding a device and reading the file — `{format, version, savedAt, topology}` with the nested object carrying its own `{format, version, seq, devices, links, layout}`. |

For the SVI: `vlan 30 GUEST` → `VLAN 30 created (name: GUEST)`, `interface vlan 30` → `Vlan30 created - this device's own address in VLAN 30`, `show vlan brief` marks it `Vlan30 (SVI)`, and `no vlan 30` refuses with `% VLAN 30 is this device's own address: Vlan30` / `Remove the interface first - "no interface vlan 30" - and the VLAN stays empty`. | `UPSTREAM-DESIGN.md` graded the upstream's claims, and four of the grades had themselves gone stale — including one that contradicted a later row in the same table | The page carries a verdict column that is only useful while it is true, and nothing re-checks it: the medium counts (57 media, eight families), the `wwan` port count (122), the `inferCage` split (104 `wwan` / 33 `antenna` / 1 `rj45`), the `dsl` port count (432) and the `routeTo()` call count (8) had all moved, and the `inferCage` row contradicted row 603 of the same file, which correctly records that the name-first rule claims those ports for `wwan` | **Recounted every grade against the catalogue and the engine, then wrote the numbers as measured and said they move.** `MEDIA` at `media.js:54` now holds **89** entries across **nine** families (copper 7, fiber 31, fibre-channel 16, coax 6, dsl 3, powerline 4, dac 7, console 6, wireless 9) and the proposal accordingly asks for a *tenth*; the note that `docs/PHYSICAL_LAYER.md` §2 "still says 53" was deleted as obsolete — that page carries 89/9/26 now. | **17** of the 321 `file.js` + line number citations across these docs pointed at code unrelated to the sentence — the citations rot because nothing reads them | A "Where" column full of line numbers looks like the most rigorous part of a page and is the least verifiable: `E_NO_COMMON_SPEED` cited at `media.js:709` (the `qsfpdd:` speed table), `W_POE_DRAW` at `validation.js:668` (a `Math.min` line, 150 lines above the warning), `E_POLISH_MISMATCH` at `media.js:262` (a comment about Cat 4 modems, ~1,000 lines from the check), `W_OPTICAL_HEADROOM` and `W_DISTANCE` and `W_COPPER_LONGRUN` each pointing at a brace or a `];` | **Swept all 321 citations mechanically** — file resolves, line within the file, and the symbol the sentence names appears near the cited line — then opened every flag. Two cited `simulator.js:1054` for RA lifetimes, which is the DHCP section comment — that state lives in `engine/cli.js:2259-2289` and is displayed at `:9921`. One cited `flow.js:400-411` for `_showFlows`, which is in `cli.js` at **8679** (dispatch at 7411); the flow.js lines are `listFlows`/`describe`. Note added to the section: prefer the symbol, because a line number cannot be checked by reading and a grep for the symbol can. |
| `CONTRIBUTING.md` attributed flags to the wrong scripts, and nothing checked: `missing-devices.js` was credited with `--skeleton` and `--apply`, `import-devices.js` with `--dry-run`, and no page said that a flag these scripts do not know is **ignored** | A flag table reads like a contract. Following it, `import-devices.js --stubs` looks like "write skeletons" and is actually a normal import; a typo in a catalogue editor is not a usage error but the default action | **Swept all 28 documented script+flag pairs against each script's own parser** (`grep` for the `indexOf`/`known` set it actually reads). Two attributions were wrong: `--skeleton` and `--apply` belong to `request-queue.js` and `missing-devices.js` respectively, never to `missing-devices.js`'s own set (`--stubs --json --no-issues --strict --help`), and `import-devices.js` reads only `--all --apply --write --upgrade --json` — its dry run is the default, not a flag. Then measured what an unknown flag does: `import-devices.js --stubs` → `Scanned 49 file(s), 670 spec(s)` / `Would add: 0`, exit **0**; `audit-devices.js --bogus`, `audit-port-types.js --bogus`, `check-node-surface.js --bogus` all run their normal job and exit **0**. `grep -rln "Unknown flag" scripts/` returns exactly one file, `missing-devices.js` (which prints `Unknown flag: --bogus` plus usage and exits **2**). Fixed both table rows and added the paragraph, including the reason the editors are run flagless first. |
| `show vrrp <group>` and `show ip ospf <pid>` were documented only in their no-argument form, and the command reference never said which number is an id and which is a time | The engine had just been changed (`1fde7dd`) so that a group id is an id and not a millisecond timestamp — the developer updated `SIMULATION.md` and `ARCHITECTURE.md` but not `CLI.md`, which is where an operator looks the syntax up. The old form was the one that contradicted itself: `show vrrp 1` answered "VRRP at t=1 ms" and then reported no master for a group `show vrrp` listed as Master | **Measured all nine forms, then wrote the rows and the transcripts.** `show vrrp 1` narrows to the group, `show vrrp 9` answers `% No VRRP group 9 on R1` / `The groups here are: 1 on eth1`, `show vrrp abc` refuses with `% show vrrp takes a group id, not "abc"` plus the three accepted forms, `show ip ospf 9` answers `% No OSPF process 9 on R1` / `The process here is "ospf 1"`, and `at <ms>` still rebuilds the control planes (`VRRP at t=5000 ms`). Also found the asymmetry the change left behind: `show vrrp 1 at 5000` works, but `show vrrp 1 2` is **not** refused — the second number is consumed as the instant, so it answers "group 1 at t=2 ms" with the group reported `Backup` and *"has not converged"*, while `show vrrp at 5000 1` is the order that does refuse (`% Unexpected argument after the instant: 1`). Both command rows updated and a subsection added with the four transcripts; my first draft of that subsection claimed a two-id refusal, which the console disproved. The rows themselves were first written as `show vrrp [<group> \| at <ms>]`, inventing a placeholder the help does not declare, and `test/docs-syntax.test.js` failed on it (`names <ms>, help has show vrrp`) before I aligned them to `show vrrp [<group>]` — the `at <ms>` form now lives in the prose, where help does not have to agree with it. |
| `README.md` described `lab4` as "VLANs 10 and 20 over a trunk with a deliberate loop, so STP has to block one port before anything passes" — implying that once STP settles, everything passes | `PC3` sits on `SW2 Fa0/2` in **VLAN 20** with an address in the VLAN 10 subnet, and the lab has no router and no SVI. Import the file, ping `PC1 → PC3`, and you get `0/4` — so the documented outcome ("anything passes") is the one thing the lab cannot show, and a reader concludes STP broke it | **Imported the file through `Topology.fromJSON()` and measured every pair.** `PC1 → PC2` is `4/4` over `SW1 Fa0/7 → SW2 Fa0/7`; `PC1 → PC3` and `PC2 → PC3` are `0/4`. `SW2` blocks `Fa0/8` as alternate and explains itself (`Fa0/8 is alternate: SW1 reaches the root for 0, this bridge for 20000`), so STP is doing exactly what the lab asks; the unreachable host is a VLAN boundary with nothing to cross it. Wrote a note under the examples table with the three measured pairs, the reason, and the console fix — `interface Fa0/2` / `switchport mode access` / `switchport access vlan 10` — after which all three PCs answer `4/4` both ways and `Fa0/8` is still blocked. Also verified the rest of the table while there: `lab3` really is 8 devices / 7 cables with `EDGE` an `ISR4321` carrying `ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 198.51.100.1` and `10.0.0.1/24` behind it, and `lab4` has the promised **2** `SW1↔SW2` links. |
| `CHANGELOG.md` kept a "Known defects (filed, not fixed)" list of five issue numbers, and nothing had re-checked one of them since it was filed | A defect list is a promise about the present, not a record of the past: three of the five no longer reproduce, so the list was telling readers the software is worse than it is — and it is the first thing a visitor reads under "Known defects". Worse, a defect that is fixed quietly is indistinguishable from one that was never real | **Rebuilt each defect's scenario and ran it.** #41 still reproduces — three iBGP speakers in AS 65001 with a missing mesh link end on `B 10.1.0.0/30  200  10.2.0.1` on the third router, i.e. R2 re-advertised what it learned over iBGP and rewrote the next hop to itself. #42 does not: an OSPF speaker shows `C` and only `C` for its own subnets. #43 does not: with the LAN port genuinely passive (`show ip ospf` state `passive`) the neighbour still installs `O 172.16.1.0/24`, which is correct OSPF. #54 does not: `flowKeyOf()` now keys on `insideLocal` and `insideGlobal`, and two hosts behind one overload pool get `203.0.113.2:1024` and `203.0.113.2:1025`, both `4/4`. #44 is marked **not exercised** rather than guessed at. **Chasing #43 turned up a new trap**: `ip ospf passive` typed before `router ospf <id>` exists is accepted, echoed (`OSPF passive on eth1`), written nowhere and has no effect — `show running-config` omits it and the adjacency stays up; applied after the process exists, it tears the adjacency down on both ends. Documented in `CLI.md`, and the CHANGELOG list now carries the measurement beside each entry instead of just the ticket number. |
| `docs/BGP-POLICY.md`'s "Anchor / Today / This design changes it to" table was split in two: a header with **zero body rows** at one point in the file, and its eleven body rows forty lines later under a different section — the first of them carrying five cells against a three-cell header | The shape sweeper reports `0 shape problem(s)` on this file and is right by its own rule (it compares rows to the header *within a contiguous block*), so a table can be broken in half and still pass: the reader sees a bare header followed later by a wall of pipes, and the one row that survived as prose-parsable prose lost its first cell | **Verified all nine documented comparison pairs by calling `comparePaths()` directly** — weight over LP, LP over AS length, locally-originated over any path, shorter AS path, lower origin, lower MED, MED across two different ASes, eBGP over iBGP, lower router id — every one returns `-1` as the table says, so the audit rows were right; the table around them was not. Moved the header down to its body, rebuilt the five-cell row as three (`constants in `bgp.js`` / the three values / what changes), and added a note that only `DEFAULT_LOCAL_PREF` is exported — `require()` of `DEFAULT_WEIGHT` or `DEFAULT_MED` is `undefined`, they are module-private at `bgp.js:57` and `:58`, so the table is only checkable by reading the file or probing (`comparePaths({ weight: 1 }, {})` → `-1`). Recorded here: the shape sweeper cannot see an orphaned table, and that is a gap in the tool, not in this page. |
| Two newly-written sections of `docs/CLI.md` illustrated their rule with a transcript of the **behaviour that had just been fixed**, in an unmarked code block that reads exactly like current output: `switchport port-security violation rest` → `% "rest" is not a violation mode`, and `show ip bgp neighbors` → `(no neighbours configured)` while `show ip bgp summary` listed the peer | A doc that teaches by before/after has to say which half you are looking at, or it becomes a page telling you a command is broken when it works — the failure mode is worse than no transcript, because the reader concludes the simulator is worse than it is and stops trying the abbreviation the page recommends | **Ran both transcripts against the current tree.** `violation rest` now answers `Violation mode on Fa0/1 set to restrict (was shutdown)`, and `prot`/`shut` expand the same way, while `b` and `zz` are refused with `% "b" is not a violation mode` / `% Use protect, restrict or shutdown` — the message quotes what was typed. The three BGP views now agree: `show ip bgp summary` says `Idle / not yet evaluated`, `show ip bgp neighbors` lists the peer with `Remote AS 65002 (eBGP)`, and naming it narrows the same block. Labelled both blocks `BEFORE`, added the measured current output under each, and spot-checked the rest of the section while there: `show ip ospf 1` → `% No OSPF process 1 on R1` / `% The process here is "ospf 7"` while `show ip ospf 7` prints the process, `show ip protocols` prints both protocols with the real `ADMIN_DISTANCE` values, and `show standby` points at VRRP. |

Those are history rows, so per this section's own convention they stay as written; the phantom citation is not history and was replaced with `test/poe-admin.test.js` (34), the suite that actually asserts the `Admin never` / `off (administratively)` table. Also amended the previous audit's conclusion in place — "all five are proposals" was four-fifths right and the wrong fifth was the only one that mattered. |
| `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` said the HTTP console "shares the board's counters", so `show interfaces` prints what a `/flows` call moved and `runtime[id].counters` counts the console's pings — read as: one accounting, one set of numbers | A sentence that promises agreement is worse than silence when the two counters disagree, because the reader takes the `0` byte count for truth: a console ping moves 664 bytes across the port and leaves `bytesOut: 0`, and the two views disagree on packets too | **Drove a live server on a spare port and counted.** `POST /api/topologies` → `load` `lab2` → three `ping 10.0.2.20` runs through `/config`, each answering `4/4`: `show interfaces` on **d1** goes `6 packets, 664 bytes` → `11 / 1264` → `16 / 1864` (**6 on the first run, 5 per run after**), while `runtime.d1.counters` goes `4/4` → `8/8` → `12/12` — four packets per run and `bytesIn`/`bytesOut` pinned at `0`, because only `/flows` feeds the byte fields. Also confirmed the `atMs`/`runAtMs`/`convergence` half of that section from the response keys, and that a *refused* flow still moves wire bytes (`connection refused` yet the port went `0 → 3 packets, 192 bytes`). Corrected the row to say what each view is for, added a second row with the numbers, and said plainly not to read `0` bytes there as "nothing was sent". |
| `docs/CLI.md` said "every verb the page prints has a way back" (a claim the newest commit made true for three commands), and the claim was never swept: 46 config rows, 25 positives, 12 `no` types on a router — nobody had checked whether the remaining rows undo | A completeness claim is only worth what someone typed to check it, and the rows most likely to be broken are the ones whose positive form is buried in another mode (port settings, VLAN priority, router-id), which is exactly where a sweep by hand stops | **Swept the help page by column position, not by eye.** Parsed `?` in config, `config-if` (router and switch port), `config-vlan` and `config-router` into 46/46/37/6/5 rows, separated command from description by the help column layout (continuation lines are indented past it), then compared each positive verb to the `no` types the same page lists. Every remaining positive has a negation except the catch-alls (`vrrp <vrid> ...`, `switchport <verb>`, `<any config-mode command>`) and `show`/`do show`, which have nothing to undo. **Typed each one to be sure**: `no speed` → `Speed on Fa0/1 back to negotiation (was 100M)`, `no lldp transmit`, `no lldp receive`, `no router-id` → `Router-id cleared (was 1.1.1.1)`, `no vrrp 1` → `VRRP group 1 removed from Gi0/0`, and the three `no spanning-tree` forms behave differently from one another — documented as a table rather than described in prose. The page now states the wrinkle a reader will hit: `no spanning-tree vlan 10 priority` is `% Incomplete command`, naming `priority` obliges you to name the value, while the same `root primary` words mean "already the default" or "wrong tool" depending on what is configured. |
| `docs/CLI.md` stated the counter arithmetic for a three-ping run (`LLDP 64 + ARP 64 + 3 × 134 = 530`) and explained the ARP term as "the cache is per run, so the first echo resolves the next hop" — a claim about where the cache lives that nothing had checked | Arithmetic a reader cannot reproduce is a round number with a citation, and a wrong statement about *where* state lives sends the next person looking in the wrong object when their totals do not add up | **Reproduced the run and then stepped it.** `ping 10.0.1.1 count 3` leaves `eth0` at `5 packets, 530 bytes` both directions — the documented sum exactly. A second command, `count 4`, takes the totals to `10 packets, 1130 bytes`, a step of **64 + 4 × 134 = 600**: LLDP paid again, ARP not. Then the part the doc had wrong: the ARP entry **survives** a second command **and** a brand-new `Simulator` over the same topology, while the counters start at zero in that new run (`(no port has carried a frame since this lab started)`). So the cache is device state and the counters are run state — the opposite pairing to what "the cache is per run" implies. Added the measured steps and corrected the sentence. **And learned a doc-convention trap the hard way**: the first version put the commands in a table, and `test/docs-syntax.test.js` read the row — pipes and all — as a documented command (`ping 10.0.1.1 count 3 \| 5 packets, 530 bytes \| …`) and failed the suite. A command with arguments in a table cell is a command line as far as that checker is concerned; transcripts belong in a fenced block. |
| `docs/CLI.md` listed eight event types and which ones charge a port's drop counter, but the table was prose-level: it never said which *condition* produces `vlan-drop` versus a plain `drop`, and the example a reader builds first ("a port in each of two VLANs") produces `drop` with the port named | A table of event names is only usable if you can tell which name your scenario will emit — otherwise you grep the log for `vlan-drop`, find nothing, and conclude the drop was not counted when the counter plainly moved | **Built three labs to hit three rows.** A trunk allowing VLAN 10 only: the switch's own LLDP in VLAN 1 is dropped as `vlan-drop` — `VLAN 1 not allowed on egress, on Fa0/1, vlan 1` — and the trunk shows `1 in, 0 out, 1 dropped` by event **#1**, before any user traffic. Two access ports in different VLANs: the ping fails `0/4`, the log says `drop … no egress ports in VLAN 10, on Fa0/1`, and the counters charge **4 drops on the ingress port** and 0 on the other. A ping to an unreachable network: `drop … no route to 192.0.2.9`, **0 drops everywhere**. Also confirmed the mechanics rather than trusting the wording: the charging rule is `FRAME_DROP_TYPES` in `engine/simulator.js`, a set of exactly those six names, with `poe-budget` absent — so the table is checkable by reading one line. Documented the measured labs, the `drop`-vs-`vlan-drop` distinction, and the warning that a mistyped `switchport trunk allowed vlan` shows a drop on the trunk before anything else happens. |
| `docs/CLI.md` introduced the event log with a prose list ("25 separate drop sites, forwards, ARP and LLDP, STP blocks …") and never enumerated the vocabulary — yet `show logging <type>` filters on that vocabulary and nothing else | A filter whose argument space is undocumented is a guessing game: 80 names exist, the page named about a dozen in passing, and the count it did give ("25 separate drop sites") was stale — `type: 'drop'` alone now appears at 28 sites | **Enumerated the vocabulary mechanically and grouped it.** `grep` over `engine/*.js` gives **80 event types across 148 emit sites**; thirteen semantic groups hold 78 of the names, with `acl-deny` and `lldp` the two left over, and the table in `CLI.md` now lists every literal string to pass the filter. Recounts are one-liners next to the table (80 / 148 / 28), so the next reader can check it in a second rather than trusting it. **Then tested the claim the table makes** — that these are filter arguments — by passing 36 of them through `show logging <type> last 2`, at least one per group plus the awkward ones (`tap-inject-refused`, `poe-admin-off`, `arp-static-hold`, `scion-delivered`, `flow-refused`): every one accepted, and a hit answers in a form worth quoting (`Event log (1 icmp-echo events, from 16 in the lab)`), so an empty result still says how much it skipped. |
| `docs/CLI.md` promised that a wrong `show logging` type "answers with the types that do exist rather than an empty table" | The filter cannot tell a typo from a real event type that has not fired yet, and its refusal lists only the types that **have** events — so a reader can conclude `icmp-reply` is not a type when it is one of the eighty, and stop looking for a name that will work as soon as the lab produces it | **Tripped it deliberately.** `show logging not-a-type` and `show logging icmp-reply` return the *same* refusal word for word — `% No event of type … has happened here` / `% Types with events so far: arp-request, frame, lldp, ping` — and its third line names the ambiguity itself. Rewrote the sentence to say what the refusal means, quoted both transcripts, and pointed the reader at the event-type inventory for "what can happen" versus this list for "what has happened". Also measured `show logging summary` and `… last 3`: the window is honoured, counts reconcile, and the device half admits its own blind spot (`(plus 6 link-level events with no single device)`). |

Nothing in the repo notices: `md-check`, `docs-syntax` and the wiki build all pass on the reverted page, because a stale line citation is not a broken link | **Re-applied the corrections and this time committed them**, so they exist in history rather than only in a working tree. | `docs/DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` listed every per-port key and omitted the one nested object a port can carry: `hardware: { … }`. So the block is authoritative for four keys (`poeOut`, `poeIn`, `speedCaps`, `maxRangeM`) and ignored for three (`cage`, `media`, `maxSpeedMbps`), which now has its own table with the evidence beside each row, plus the reason the cage wins (a spec contradicting its own `connector` is not believed) and the flat-versus-nested note — 51 of the 52 files that state a PoE rate use the flat key, and my first draft of that sentence said 1,585, so the counter now states what it counts. |
| `docs/REQUESTING-HARDWARE.md` insisted, in a paragraph written *to correct an earlier error*, that `--skeleton` is "the **only** flag this script parses" and that "there is no `--json` switch" | A correction is the most confident prose on a page and the least likely to be re-checked, so when the fact changes it becomes the most wrong sentence in the document. `--json` had been added to the script since; the page sent readers away from a flag that works | **Read the argv handling instead of trusting either version.** `scripts/request-queue.js` parses two flags — `argv.indexOf('--skeleton')` and `argv.includes('--json')` — and the stub branch `return`s before the JSON branch, so `--json --skeleton 12` prints the stub. Replaced the paragraph with a two-row table naming the lines, and kept the part that was still true and worth keeping: no `FORGEJO_TOKEN` exits **2** (re-measured) with the message this page quotes verbatim. Also found the categories list had stopped at 21 of the 25 values in the catalogue — `splitter` (16), `tap` (9), `tablet` (10) and `ap` (1) were missing — and added the counting one-liner, verified to print 25 lines. |
| `docs/MIRRORING.md` described the GitHub→Forgejo bridge with four details it had invented: the branch was `gh-pr/<n>` (the script writes `gh/<n>`), idempotency was "keyed on the head SHA" (it is keyed on the `<!-- gh-pr: N -->` marker), the merge comment was `Landed as <url>` (it is a two-sentence paragraph naming the commit), and the bot "closes" the GitHub PR (it closes nothing) | A page about plumbing is read as the specification of that plumbing, and every one of these is the kind of detail a script-writer acts on. The page even contradicted itself — the status box below the list said `gh/<n>` | **Read `scripts/gh-bridge.js` and ran its refusals instead of trusting the prose.** The branch is `'gh/' + pr.number`; the fetch is `refs/pull/N/head:refs/heads/gh/<n>` with `--force`; `indexByMarker(existing, /<!-- gh-pr: (\d+) -->/)` is what makes it idempotent, with the head SHA appearing only inside the PR body; the report pass asks GitHub whether any comment already contains `/pulls/<n>` before posting (so `--report` does not stack); and the only `close` in the file is a query for closed PRs. Re-measured the `forgejo-api.js` refusal the page quotes — it says `the tracker is not readable without it (this is not the same as \"no open requests\")` and exits 2, not the \"cannot look\" the page claimed. Corrected all of it, quoting the code and the real comment text. |
| `docs/TRIAGE.md` told readers the sweep was unusable off a LAN and that its token came out of a git remote — `API` "hard-coded near the top of the script to `http://10.0.10.20:3000/api/v1`", token "scraped out of the `github` or `origin` git remote" | That paragraph was a **warning**, and a wrong warning is worse than none: it tells a contributor not to try, and the tool it describes had been rewritten to be reached from anywhere. Both halves were false | **Read the module the sweep imports and then used the page's own command from here.** `scripts/forgejo-api.js` builds `API = (process.env.PF_API \|\| canonical.WEB) + '/api/v1'` — the public host, overridable — and reads the token from `FORGEJO_TOKEN`/`PF_TOKEN` in the environment, nothing else. Ran the documented `curl`: `HTTP/1.1 200` anonymous with `x-total-count: 121`, so the read path works off-network exactly as the table now says, and the quoted backlog dropped from 127 to 121 within the same day — which is why the page now tells you to re-measure that number instead of quoting it. |
| `docs/CLI.md` documented `show ip ospf <process-id>` as a working filter — "read the number as what it names, filter to it, and refuse one that is not there" — with a transcript only for the *missing* process | The form refuses the process that **does** exist too, so the one example the page showed made a dead command look alive: `show ip ospf` works, `show ip ospf 1` answers `% No OSPF process 1 on R1` on a router whose process is `ospf 1` | **Ran the positive case, which the page had never shown.** `command -` style probing found `config.ospf.pid` is `undefined` — no command ever sets a process id — so `Number(what) !== Number(cfg.pid)` is `1 !== NaN` for every argument, and the refusal's second line prints `(cfg.pid \|\| 1)`, inventing the id it just refused. Documented the limitation with both transcripts, said which forms still work (`show ip ospf` bare, plus `neighbor`/`database`/`interface`, all three re-measured), and annotated the command-table row so a reader is not sent to a dead form. Also recorded two smaller artefacts of the same path: `show ip ospf process 1` prints a stray `OSPF at t=1 ms` line before refusing, and the `database` header repeats the invented process id. The engine fix is a code change, so it is written down rather than made. |
| `docs/CLI.md` documented `vlan <id> [name]` but never said there is **no VLAN sub-mode** — the one thing an IOS operator's fingers will assume. `vlan 20` creates and returns to config mode, so the block form fails twice: `name GUEST` is `% Unrecognized config command`, and `show vlan name GUEST` then answers `% No VLAN named "GUEST"` while listing `VLAN0020, VLAN0030` | The second failure is the trap. Nothing is broken — the VLAN exists under its auto name — but the refusal lists names that match neither what was typed nor the id, so it reads as though the VLAN vanished. A reader following the page's own one-line form was fine; a reader doing what IOS taught them got two errors and no explanation | **Swept every optional-id form in the command table, then chased the one that behaved oddly.** Ran both the bare and argumented form of `show vlan <id>`, `show spanning-tree <port>`, `show interfaces counters <port>`, `show ipv6 interface <port>`, `show ipv6 route <dest>`, `show arp <port>`, `show ip interface brief <port>`, `show mac address-table interface <port>`, `show lldp neighbors`, `show power inline` and `show vrrp <group>` in a three-device lab: every one accepts an existing thing and refuses a missing one, with the refusal naming what does exist — the OSPF process-id form from the last iteration is the only dead one. The odd result was VLAN naming, so the page now carries the block-form transcripts and says the sub-mode does not exist. |
| `docs/LINK-STATE.md` said "`fullState()` attaches `carries` and `stateReason` to every link in the payload" | It reads as `topology.fullState()` and there is no such method on `Topology` or `Simulator`, so the sentence sends a reader (me, this iteration) hunting for a method that does not exist. The real thing is `fullState(topo)` in `server/index.js`, and it adds **three** keys, not two | **Tried the call the page named, got `t.fullState is not a function`, then read the server.** `fullState(topo)` is a module-level function called by every handler that returns a topology and by the WebSocket broadcast; per link it sets `verdict` from `Topology.linkVerdict()`, `carries` from `linkState().up` and `stateReason` from `linkState().reason`. Replaced the sentence with that table and listed `linkState()`'s five `cause` values (`cable`, `admin`, `line`, `gone`, `null`). Everything else on the page reproduced exactly in a rebuilt lab — the four duplicate `% Still converging` hints per lost echo, `E_STP` with its two-line `yet:` hint, the `converge` table with `(pending)` markers, the 41 s success, and the router-only over-promise (`converges again at 40000 ms` against `Fully converged at 10000 ms`). |

| `docs/CONVERGENCE.md`'s timer table was correct but gave no way to reproduce it — and my first recipe for reproducing the OSPF row asserted two things that turned out to be false: that a lone OSPF speaker has nothing to converge and says so, and that two speakers with different `hello` values separate the rows | Both were invented from how OSPF behaves on real hardware. Here a lone configured speaker still promotes `ospf R1 16000 ms` (the schedule reads configuration, not adjacency), and `ospf hello <n>` is not a command at all — `% Usage: ospf router-id <ip> \| ospf area <id>` | **Wrote the recipe, ran it, and corrected the recipe.** Two routers on one link give two rows at 16000 ms ✓, but the lone-router case prints a full promise for a speaker with no peer, and the only way to change `hello` is `ospf.helloSec` in the topology JSON — `convergence.ospfTimers()` reads `num(ospf.helloSec, C.ospfHelloSec)`. Also measured the command that does answer "is anybody there": `show ip ospf neighbor` prints a header plus `(no adjacencies - no neighbour passed the passive, subnet and area checks)`, which I had assumed was an empty table. The page now says all three, and the invented version is gone rather than left in as plausible prose. |
| §12 of this page listed "whether `Link.state` was meant to gate forwarding" as a question the code cannot answer, claiming "nothing reads it" | It is answered, and answered the other way: `Link.isUp()` is `state === 'up'` and the forwarder checks it. The `cannot tell you` section is the one place a reader goes to find out what is unknown, so a false entry there sends them looking for ambiguity that is not there — or worse, trusting a cable-down test they think is meaningless | **Read `Link` and then proved it end to end.** `simulator.js:528` skips forwarding when `!link.isUp()` and `segment.js:61` refuses to build a segment the same way; two PCs behind a switch answered `4/4`, answered `0/4` after `link down l5`, and `!!!!` again after `link up l5`. Struck the bullet, wrote the measurement in its place, and kept the part that is genuinely open — the comment advertises a `'blocked'` state that no code ever writes, so STP blocking cannot be told apart from a pulled cable by any caller of `isUp()`. Also sharpened the `voiceVlan` bullet after measuring `show vlan` with `switchport voice vlan 10`: `Fa0/2(v)` under VLAN 10, and the same port still listed under VLAN 1, so a voice port appears twice with nothing marking one list advisory. |
| `docs/CLI.md` documented the `ip route` refusals for a bad network and a bad next hop, but not the one that everybody types first: the **prefix-length** form. `ip route 0.0.0.0/0 10.0.0.254` is refused as `"0.0.0.0/0" is not an IPv4 address` | The refusal names the whole slash-form as a bad *address*, so it reads as a typo rather than a notation mismatch — and the success line one keystroke later prints `Route added: 0.0.0.0/0 via …`, in exactly the spelling just rejected. It also bit this pass in an unrelated lab: two PCs whose default route was written as `0.0.0.0/0` had no route at all, which made a firewall test look like an ACL | **Hit it while building a lab, then documented it.** Measured on a router and on a host: router refuses `0.0.0.0/0` and `192.168.2.0/24`, accepts `0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.0.254`; a `generic-pc` accepts the same mask-form command and `show ip route` then prints `S 0.0.0.0/0  1  10.1.1.1  -` with an empty `Interface` column. Added the transcripts and the notation-vs-address point. The same lab then let me check §12's firewall bullet properly — a router carrying a `firewall` capability and no bound ACL pings through exactly like one without the capability, so that entry stands as written. |
| `docs/CLI.md`'s command table summed up both route commands in a few words — `add static route`, and `ip default-gateway <gw>` = `set default gateway (hosts)` | Two undocumented behaviours sat behind the shorthand: the `Interface` column of `show ip route` is filled only by the optional fourth word (so a static route can print a next hop and no port), and `ip default-gateway` is **not** host-only — a router accepts it and installs `0.0.0.0/0` at AD 254 | **Wrote the transcripts for both.** `ip route … 10.0.0.2` gives `S 10.9.0.0/24 1 10.0.0.2 -`, the same command with `Gi0/0` appended gives `S 10.8.0.0/24 1 10.0.0.2 Gi0/0`, and the echo mentions only the hop either way. Also measured the interface-only refusal, which is unusually good: `% Gi0/0 is an interface, not a next hop - this model forwards to a next-hop address` plus what IOS would do on a point-to-point link. And on a router, `ip default-gateway 10.0.0.254` answers `Default gateway: 10.0.0.254` and adds `S 0.0.0.0/0  254  10.0.0.254  -`; the table row now says so, and a new section explains that AD 254 is the only distance the console can reach because the fourth word of `ip route` is an interface, not a metric. |
| The two static-route commands are documented as near-twins — `ip route <dest> <mask> <gw> [iface]` and `ipv6 route <prefix>/<len> <next-hop>` — with nothing saying that the optional interface exists on one side only | A reader moving a lab from v4 to v6 keeps the extra word and is refused, and the `Interface` column of `show ipv6 route` can never show a static route's port, which looks like a rendering gap until you know the command takes no interface | **Compared the two by running them.** `ipv6 route 2001:db8:8::/64 2001:db8:1::2 Gi0/0` answers `% Unexpected argument "Gi0/0" - ipv6 route <prefix>/<len> <next-hop> takes no more`; without the extra word the route installs and prints `S 2001:db8:9::/64  1  2001:db8:1::2  -`. Also measured `::/0` as the default-route spelling and `% Invalid IPv6 next hop "not-an-address"` for a bad hop. Documented the asymmetry beside the v4 section it contrasts with, rather than in a v6 section a reader would reach after making the mistake. |
| `docs/CLI.md` never mentioned `no ipv6 route` at all, and its help advertises per-prefix removal (`no ipv6 route <prefix>/<len> [<next-hop>]`). So nothing warned that the handler ignores the tail: any `no ipv6 …` clears the IPv6 gateway **and every static IPv6 route**, including when the named prefix was never configured | The IPv4 sibling removes exactly one route and echoes `Route removed: 10.9.0.0/24`, so a reader reasonably assumes the same. Losing an entire IPv6 table to a scoped-looking command is the kind of difference a page has to carry, even when the underlying behaviour is the engine's to fix | **Ran the pair side by side.** With two static v6 routes, `no ipv6 route 2001:db8:9::/64` answered `IPv6 routes cleared` and left only the connected prefix; a never-configured prefix cleared the table identically. Documented the transcripts and told readers to treat any `no ipv6 …` as clear-all. Also verified the carve-out: `no ipv6 unicast-routing` keeps both routes and adds the `!` banner, matching what the page already said at line 3764. While in the same area, measured that `ip route` accepts a next hop on **no connected subnet** — and because equal prefix and equal AD tie to insertion order, the unreachable hop took the `*` and shadowed the correct route, which `show ip route <dest>` reports line by line. Both traps now sit next to the commands that cause them. |
| Last pass documented what `no ipv6 route` swallows but left the IPv4 contrast at one sentence — `no ip route 10.9.0.0 255.255.255.0` answers `Route removed` "and leaves every other static route alone" | That is only true *across prefixes*. Two routes to the same prefix both go, and the echo names the prefix once with no count, so there is no way to tell from the output whether one route or two disappeared. Naming the gateway removes exactly one hop and says which (`Route removed: 10.9.0.0/24 via 10.0.0.3`) | **Measured all three forms.** `<dest> <mask>` → one echo, both next hops gone; `<dest> <mask> <gw>` → `Route removed: 10.9.0.0/24 via 10.0.0.3` with the other hop still installed; an unconfigured gateway → `% No route to 10.9.0.0/24 via 10.0.0.99 is configured` plus a line saying what *is* configured. Also found the swallow reaches `no ipv6 gateway`, which reports `IPv6 routes cleared` and takes the table with it — a reader removing a gateway should not lose routes — and that repeating it on an empty configuration still reports a clear. |
| `docs/CLI.md` explained the two-line refusal for an unreachable destination (`% No route to 172.16.0.1` / `% No default route is configured`) as though that settled the lookup, and never showed what happens once a default *does* exist | The refusal is conditional, and the fallback answer is where the interesting labels are: `Matched 0.0.0.0/0 (default, AD 254)` for an `ip default-gateway`, and after adding a static default the same gateway appears as `Shadowed S 0.0.0.0/0 (static, AD 254)` — labelled `static` there but `default` on the IPv6 side | **Followed the refusal to the case it excludes.** Measured v4 and v6 side by side: v4 refuses with a second line naming the missing default, resolves to `(default, AD 254)` once `ip default-gateway` exists, then shows the gateway shadowed as `(static, AD 254)` when a static `0.0.0.0/0` is added; v6 resolves the same way through `ipv6 gateway` and labels its shadowed row `(default, AD 254)`. Documented both plus the labelling disagreement, which is the sort of small inconsistency a reader will otherwise assume is their own misreading. |
| `docs/CLI.md` described the flow defaults as "an ephemeral source port" and listed the refusal codes the data path produces, with nothing about the three argument refusals or about what a closed port does over UDP | "Ephemeral" reads like an allocator, but `srcPort` is the literal constant `EPHEMERAL_FIRST` (49152) unless `sport` names one, so two flows can share a five-tuple; and a TCP refusal on port 80 does not imply a UDP refusal there | **Opened the same port twice, both protocols.** Two TCP flows to the same server:port both printed `10.0.1.10:49152` and were recorded as `flow-1`/`flow-2` with identical five-tuples; a UDP flow to the same port 80 was accepted as `open` with MSS 1472 against TCP's 1460. Captured the argument refusals too — `% proto must be tcp or udp (got icmp)`, `% port out of range: 0`, `% port out of range: 65536`, `% port needs a number (got abc)` — none of which the page had. Wrote the MSS arithmetic rather than the hand-wave: 1500 − 20 − 20 and 1500 − 20 − 8. |
| "`flow close` runs … nothing at all for UDP" read as though closing a UDP flow were a no-op, and the `show flows <id>` example only ever showed `(still open)` | Closing UDP changes no counters but does change the record (`closed  (closed at step 16)`), and the annotation is driven by `closedStep` alone — so it answers "did this ever close", not "what is the state" | **Closed a UDP flow and watched the counters either side.** 1 packet out before, 1 after — invisible, as the page said — but the detail view changed to `closed  (closed at step 16)`. Closed a TCP flow for contrast: the closer's packet count rose by 2 and the peer's by 1, three frames for the four-way close. Then read the formatter: the suffix is `closedStep === null ? '(still open)' : '(closed at step N)'`, so a reset flow would still be labelled `(still open)` — documented as two forms and the field they follow, instead of the three I first guessed at. |
| The flows heading explained `recorded < opened` as the trace of a refusal and described `refused` as "the one a closed port turned away", implying the two counters are exclusive and that every refusal behaves alike | They overlap: a closed-port refusal counts in **both** (`none (1 opened, 1 refused, no record kept)`), while a no-route attempt counts only as refused (`none (0 opened, 1 refused, …)`) with the counters left at `0 0` because no frame left the device | **Split one refusal from the other.** Same lab, two runs: closed port → `1 opened, 1 refused`; unreachable destination → `0 opened, 1 refused`. My first patch of this pass said `opened` "counts every attempt made", which the second run disproved, so the paragraph now shows both transcripts and states that `opened` minus `refused` is not a count of conversations in progress. Also found that `show flows counters` omits devices with no traffic entirely — an isolated PC is absent rather than zero — and noted that a refusal is always attributed to the opener. |
| §5.1 listed four DHCP failure reasons — `no-server`, `no-pool`, `pool-exhausted`, `nak` — without saying where any of them can be seen, and the table's only "where" row described the success event (`phase: 'ack'`) | The reasons are real but reach no console: an exhausted pool leaves the client `unassigned` with `Method DHCP`, a timing-out ping, and `% PC2 is not a DHCP server (no pools) and holds no DHCP lease`. The words exist only in the timeline event | **Built a one-address pool with two clients.** PC1 leased `10.0.5.100`; PC2 timed out silently and its `show ip dhcp binding` accused it of not being a server. The reason turned up in `sim.events` as `{ type: 'dhcp', phase: 'failed', reason: 'pool-exhausted', serverName: 'R1', pool: 'ONE', detail: 'pool ONE has no address left to offer', step: 0 }`. Documented that mapping in both pages — the reason table now says where the reason lives, and the CLI section says plainly that no console command prints it, so diagnosis is `Method DHCP` plus `In use : 1 of 1` on the pool. |
| The relay paragraph said a helper address nobody owns leaves the client at `no-server`, which is true, and said nothing about several helpers on one port or about the command being accepted where no relay can happen | The sentence `(DHCP requests will be relayed to it)` is printed by a switch port and by a host interface too, and a helper on a switch relays nothing — the client fails with the same `no-server` a missing relay gives | **Put helpers where they cannot work, on purpose.** A helper on `SW1`'s port, with none on the router: client `no-server`, ping `....`, and the console had still promised relaying. Then two helpers on the router's port, the first pointing at nothing: the client leased anyway, so the list is searched rather than the first entry used, and `show ip dhcp binding` prints one relay line per helper. Documented both, since the honest reading is "what was stored" versus "what will happen". |
| `docs/CLI.md` printed a `show logging nonsense` refusal as `% No events of type "nonsense" in this lab` / `% Types seen: …` — two lines, and the wrong two | The command answers in four lines (`% No event of type "…" has happened here`, then `Types with events so far`, then two lines explaining that the vocabulary is the log itself), and it has two more undocumented behaviours: an unambiguous prefix is accepted (`fra` → `frame`, header reads `from 67 in the lab`) and a shared prefix is refused with candidates (`% Ambiguous event type "f": forward, frame`) | **Checked the vocabulary claim, then the filter.** `80 event types across 148 emit sites` verified mechanically: the 80 names in the doc table and the 80 emitted type strings in `engine/` match exactly, with nothing extra on either side. Then drove the filter: fresh lab + type → `% The log is empty: nothing has run in this lab`; unknown word → the four-line refusal; `fra` → frames with a filtered header; `f` → ambiguity refusal. Replaced the stale two-line transcript and documented prefix matching, the ambiguity message and `all`. |
| The event-log filter list named five forms — `device <name>`, `last <n>`, `summary` — without showing what any of them prints, and a typo'd device name was unmentioned | `show logging device PC1` prints `Event log (11 events on PC1, from 72 in the lab)` and an unknown name is refused as `% No device named "NOPE"`; `summary last 3` is `Event summary (last 3 of 72 events)`; and the summary's by-device column accounts for frames with no single device in a pluralised line | **Ran the three forms.** Device view, unknown device, `summary last 3`, `summary` and `last 3` all measured; the shapes matched the page where it existed and the missing parts are now written. Also fixed a direction error from my own previous pass — the new cross-reference said the vocabulary was "below" when the table is above — and removed a duplicated refusal transcript I had introduced in a section that already showed it. |
| `docs/CLI.md` claimed an empty event table "distinguishes" a cleared log from a lab where nothing has run, and showed no transcript for `clear logging`'s two refusals | There is no cleared flag — the command empties the array and both cases print `No events: the log is empty - nothing has run yet in this lab` word for word | **Cleared a log with 67 events and asked again.** Same message as a fresh lab; the only difference is that `show history` still lists the `clear logging` line, because history is not an event. Also measured the enable-mode refusal (`% clear logging needs enable mode - type "enable" first`), the argument refusal (`% Unexpected argument "now" (clear logging takes none)`) and `Event log cleared (67 events dropped)`, and removed the false claim rather than softening it. |
| `docs/CLI.md` said the reconciliation of the event summary was covered because "`test/logging.test.js` asserts both sums" | The suite sums the per-type column against the total (line 190) and, for devices, only matches `by device:` — the per-device plus link-level sum is unchecked. The page now says which half is guarded | **Read the test rather than trusting the page's summary of it.** Both sums reconcile in a 72-event run (types 72; devices 41 + 31 link-level), but only the type sum is asserted; a miscount in the device column would pass. Reworded to separate the asserted property from the measured one, and noted the suite's 53 checks and that it runs standalone. |
| The `show clock` section showed the pinned form as a single line, implying the explanatory paragraph was a one-off, and never showed a fractional instant or the refusal for a bad one | The paragraph prints on every invocation; `at 3218.75` reports `t=3218.75 ms  (3.21875 s)`; `at -5` and `at 5s` are both `% Invalid instant: …`, even though `traceroute` prints units in its delay column | **Pinned the clock four ways.** Bare, `at 5000`, `at 3218.75`, `at converged` back to `t=0 ms - the lab is at rest`, plus the two refusals. Corrected the transcript to show the paragraph that actually accompanies every answer, and added the fractional form and the "no units here" contrast, checking the traceroute side too so the contrast is `50.9 us` and not a guess. |
| The abbreviations section stated the prefix rule as universal and gave `sh ip int br` as its proof, without testing the near-miss or the config-mode case | `sh int br` resolves `int` to `interfaces` and reads `br` as a port (`% No interface named br on R1`, plus a hint naming `show ip interface brief`); and in interface config mode a bare `show` skips abbreviation entirely (`sh vers` → `% Unrecognized interface command`), while `do sh …` behaves like exec | **Probed twelve prefixes across three modes.** Exec and global-config agree on the rule; interface config is the exception, and there an ambiguous prefix is reported as `% Invalid show command` with no candidates — the wrong diagnosis. Documented the exception with the `do` workaround and added the `sh int br` near-miss, since that is the line most readers will type first. |
| The page asserted "a command is abbreviatable exactly when it is documented" without ever checking the documented side, and never mentioned that modes are sticky | 130 documented forms (57 exec, 46 config, 27 interface) each run in its own session: all executable, zero unrecognised. Kept the useful half: refusals name the current mode, so a refused line tells you where the last line left you. Measured all four wordings incl. interface mode's double line and ospf's `In this mode:` hint. |
| The refusal table added one iteration earlier said only `router ospf` names what it wants, and implied `vlan <id>` opens a sub-mode like IOS | `router bgp` gives the same `In this mode:` hint with a shorter list; `vlan 10` opens no mode at all — `name USERS` on the next line is `% Unrecognized config command`, only `vlan 10 name USERS` works | **Checked the claim I had just written.** Two of five sub-mode candidates give hints, and they are exactly the two router modes; `vlan` is a one-line creator, not a mode, so the classic two-step is refused. Extended the table with the bgp row and the measured vlan transcripts rather than leaving a table that was right about three modes and wrong about the fourth. |
| The catalogue grew to 1,628 models and five pages still quoted 1,599 / 16,471 ports / 1,249 sourced specs — `CONTRIBUTING.md` (three places, one of them the label above the command that prints them), `TRIAGE.md`, `UPSTREAM-DESIGN.md` (C5 row) and the hardware-request wiki page | Recounted with the documented one-liners: 1,628 models / 246 vendors / 16,746 ports, 104 models carrying `+`, 1,278 specs with `sourceUrl`, 229 suites. | `docs/CI.md` said the Server smoke test catches a server that "boots but cannot serve: every API route, the catalogue, WebSocket upgrade" | It is 18 checks over the UI shell, catalog, media, version and topology CRUD; `config`, `interfaces`, `ping`, `flows`, `layout`, `load`, `reset`, `link-options`, `links/:id/state` are outside it, and the WebSocket upgrade is driven by nothing anywhere (`grep -rl Sec-WebSocket` finds only `server/ws.js`) | **Ran the smoke script and counted.** 18 `ok` lines, listed route by route, then diffed against the route table in `server/index.js` to name the nine uncovered paths instead of implying "most". Separately: the catalogue count moved again mid-iteration (1,628 → 1,629), so `CONTRIBUTING.md` now says a prose count is a claim about one revision and carries the rule to copy the command rather than the number. |
| The per-mode help section stated "57 exec forms / 130 total" verified, and `clear arp` had a second and third spelling nobody had measured, while `show interfaces transceiver`'s two refusals were unquoted | Two new exec forms landed the same evening, so the page now says 59 / 46 / 27 = 132 and tells the reader to recount; `clear arp interface <port>` is a third spelling of one implementation and `clear arp <address>` a fourth; the copper-only answers are `% Every port on R1 is copper: there are no module bays` and `% No transceiver port named Gi0/0` | **Counted the exec list again rather than trusting a number one hour old, and it had moved.** Probed the near duplicates the new list revealed — two `clear arp` rows and three `show ip interface` forms — and found them aliases of one implementation, not competing commands, which is worth saying so a reader does not hunt for a difference that is not there. |
| §5.5 said an adjacency needs a **direct link between two OSPF speakers**, and that two routers on one VLAN through a switch "never become neighbours, however long you wait" — a claim this page's own §10 row and `docs/CLI.md` had already contradicted after the segment walk landed | Measured `R1 -- SW1 -- R2` with OSPF on both: `show ip ospf neighbor` answers `FULL 10.0.0.2 … Gi0/0`. `segmentOf(R1, Gi0/0)` returns `SW1:Fa0/1, R2:Gi0/0`, and the tagged walk `{ vid: 10 }` on an untagged port returns nothing. Row corrected from *Approximate* to *Modelled* | **A correction of mine was the stale one.** I had tightened that row earlier to "direct link", which was true before `8ac57e8` taught `segmentOf` to walk segments and 45 lines later to walk them tagged; two pages then disagreed and the newer work was in the other one. Fixing it meant re-measuring rather than picking the page I trusted. |
| §5.5 cited OSPF cost to `simulator.js:352`/`:380` (a traceroute helper and a closing brace) and called the link metric "a fixed 10" | Cost is per interface — `ip ospf cost` else a speed ladder against a **10 Gbps** reference (100M→100, 1G→10, 2.5G→4, 5G→2, 10G+→1) — and a link's cost is the **sum of both ends**, with `cost: 10` only a last-resort fallback. Proven by flipping a prefix between a direct link and a detour by slowing one end to 100M | **Nearly wrote the wrong correction here.** Reading `buildGraph()` alone says an interface cost counts only when *both* ends set one, so I was about to delete the page's "100 Mbps reference" claim as false; the path test disagreed, `_ospfCost()` at `simulator.js:1489` was the missing half, and its table turned out to be 10 Gbps-referenced while its own comment says 100 Mbps. Both numbers re-derived by hand (detour 20 + 20 = 40, not 30). |
| `docs/LINK-STATE.md` documented STP convergence in detail and never mentioned path cost — the one number that decides which port blocks — nor that `spanning-tree cost <n>` exists | Ladder read from `speedToCost()` and measured live: 100M→200000, 1G→20000, 10G→2000, 25G→800, 40G→400, 100G+→200, unknown→20000; override measured flipping a triangle's blocked port to `alternate` with the reason line printed | **Added the section, then tightened it after a second test.** I first wrote "the override is per end", which is true of the command but wrong about the effect: raising cost on SW1's side alone leaves SW2 at `root path cost 20000` and forwarding, because a bridge measures from its own port. Measured both cases rather than reasoning about the first result. |
| `docs/DEVICE_REGISTRY.md` documented `apply-research.js` as "the only supported path" to catalogue fields while four sibling scripts in `scripts/` also write `devices/*.json`, two of them one-off repairs that are unsafe to re-run | Added a table of all five with their dry-run behaviour measured from `argv` handling: three are dry by default, `fix-optics.js` and `fix-optics-claims.js` need `--apply`, and `merge-research.js` writes as it runs with no flags at all. Labelled the two spent tools as spent | **Counted doc mentions per script and found six at one or fewer.** Five were catalogue writers, one was the syntax checker. Read each one's argv handling rather than its header comment, which is how the two "--apply" tools and the flagless one were separated. Also refreshed `CI.md`'s two moving numbers mid-pass (2156→2200 files, 831→835 heads). |
| `README.md` explained the 25th category (`ap`) rendering as a raw string "where every other device shows a label" | `l3-switch` (136 models, the third-largest category) is in `CAT_ORDER` but missing from **both** `CAT_LABELS` and `BADGE_LABELS`, so its filter option and cards read `l3-switch`; `tap` (15) is missing from the card map too. 23 of 24 buckets are labelled in the filter, 22 on a card | **Diffed the two label maps against `CAT_ORDER`.** The page's claim was built on one example and generalised; counting keys found two more categories in the same state, both far more common than `ap`. Also re-confirmed the example labs regenerate byte-for-byte (hash-compare before and after `build-examples.js`, no diff) because that claim is load-bearing for the CI note. |

They draw in the default `#3c4a63` with no legend swatch | **Ran the canvas's own rule list over `Object.keys(MEDIA)`** rather than reading the regexes and guessing. Fibre Channel falling through is the notable one — it *is* fibre, drawn as unclassified because no rule names `fc`/`gfc` — and it is the kind of thing a reader would assume works. |
| Nothing documented what the UI keeps across a reload, and the obvious guess (device positions) is the wrong one | `localStorage` holds one key, `pf.cableLegend.collapsed`, and nothing else — zoom, pan, selection, filters and console scrollback all reset.
