While writing it up I attributed a `catch` comment to the layout POST that actually belongs to the faceplate render — checked the two line numbers and moved the quote. Also had to repair my own insertion, which had split a paragraph in half. |
| `show vlan name <name>` was documented as "narrowing" without saying how, and IOS habit reads that as a prefix match | Measured three forms: `GUEST` and `guest` both answer, `GUE` is refused with `% No VLAN named "GUE" on SW1` and `% Names here: GUEST`. The match is exact-and-case-insensitive, and the refusal lists configured names | **Tried the prefix case specifically** because the row that promised narrowing left the operator's next keystroke ambiguous. Also re-verified the neighbouring switch-command claims in the same run (`show etherchannel summary` → `% No link aggregation groups configured`, `show power inline` → `% SW1 neither sources nor draws PoE`, `show mac address-table interface` → per-port listing), all unchanged. |
| Every live catalogue figure had drifted again — 1,645 models in four pages, `1,629 / 246 vendors / 16,754 ports` in the re-measure block, `22 more carry source`, `dualRole 2` | Re-measured by running the re-measure block's own command: **1,650 models / 247 vendors / 16,975 ports**; 1,302 `sourceUrl` plus **20** `source`; `dualRole` on **3** files; `+`-bearing model ids still **104**, denominator now 1,650 | Every figure the page quotes now traces to one runnable command, and the four pages that cross-quote it were updated in the same pass |
| The undocumented-key survey said "thirty-two exist in total, eighteen in that table, fourteen undocumented" | Counted the keys directly across all 1,650 files: **33** distinct top-level keys. The schema table carries **16**, `poe` is documented by mention, so **17 documented / 16 not** — and one of the sixteen, `powerMaxW` (5 files, a radio's transmit power that nothing models), was missing from the survey entirely | An inventory table is only as good as its totals: 16 + 17 did not add up to the 33 keys measured, which is what exposed the missing row |
| `inferCage()`'s connector rule was documented with hyphenated names that the regex cannot match | `lc/upc1` and `lcupc1` resolve to `lc-upc`; `lc-upc1`, `sc-apc1`, `lc-apc1`, `sc-upc1` fall through to `rj45`. No catalogue port uses a connector name today, so the trap waits for hand-written specs; `connector` overrides every rule | Probe the rule with the spelling the table prints — a table of regex behaviour written from reading the regexes will be wrong somewhere |
| `CONNECTORS.md` described `canMate()`'s verdict order in five gates, starting at the antenna check, and credited two refusals to it that it does not produce | The function has **seven** gates: `E_NO_PORT`, then `E_WIRELESS_TO_WIRED`, then `E_PROTOCOL_MISMATCH` for a console/data pair (both before `cagesMate()`), then polish, connector, media, speed. `E_CONSOLE_TO_DATA` comes from `validateTopology()` and `E_COMBO_OCCUPIED` from `connect()`. Measured: console↔rj45 → `E_PROTOCOL_MISMATCH`, rj45↔missing → `E_NO_PORT`, antenna↔antenna → `ok: true` with two bands | A refusal table headed by a function name is a claim about that function's internals: read the returns in order and count them, because the three gates before the connector check are the ones an operator misreads |
| `CONNECTORS.md` said `preferredMedia` ranks "`fiber > dac > copper > console/wireless`, breaking ties on speed" | The rank map is `fiber 3, dac 2`, and **copper, console and wireless all at 1** — copper outranks nothing below dac. There is also a pre-filter to media that fit the port's max rate and a `linkOnly` demotion to rank −1 (`['1000base-bx','1000base-lx']` → `1000base-lx`, but `['1000base-bx']` alone keeps `1000base-bx`). A complete tie keeps input order: `['1000base-t','1000base-t-sfp']` answers `1000base-t`, the reversed list answers `1000base-t-sfp` | A total order written as `>` hides equalities. Reading the rank literal and then probing four tied pairs showed two media families sharing rank 1 and a direction-dependent answer |

---

## 12. Things this page cannot tell you

Places where the code does not answer the question, stated rather than guessed:

- ~~Whether `Link.state` was meant to gate forwarding.~~ **Answered, and this page had it wrong:**
  it does gate forwarding. `Link.isUp()` is `state === 'up'`, the forwarder checks it
  (`simulator.js:528`, `if (link && !link.isUp()) continue;`) and segment building checks it again
  (`segment.js:61`). Measured end to end: a 4/4 ping between two PCs, `link down l5`, the same ping
  answers `0/4`, `link up l5`, `!!!!` again. What is still open is narrower — the constructor comment
  advertises `'up' | 'down' | 'blocked' (STP)`, and **nothing in `engine/` ever writes `'blocked'`**,
  so an STP-blocked cable and an operator-pulled one are indistinguishable to anything that asks
  `isUp()`.
- Whether a LAG below `minLinks` was meant to shut the bundle or merely stop
  forwarding.
- What `voiceVlan` was for. The forwarder never consults it, but `show vlan` does: a port whose config
  carries `voiceVlan` is annotated `(v)` in the port list — `10  VOICE  active  Fa0/2(v)` — which is
  the only place the field has any visible effect. Note the port still appears in VLAN 1's list too, so a
  voice port is listed twice and `show vlan` gives no hint that one of the two lists is advisory.
- Whether RA valid/preferred lifetimes were meant to age during a run (they are
  stored and displayed; nothing decrements them).
- Whether the `firewall` capability was meant to imply default-deny. Today a
  device with `firewall` and no bound ACL permits everything.

If you are relying on one of these, the honest move is to check the code at the
commit you are running, not this page and not the README.

---

## Checking a claim yourself

The engine is dependency-free, so every claim above is one command away:

```bash
node -e "const m=require('./engine/media'); console.log(Object.keys(m.MEDIA).length, Object.keys(m.CAGES).length)"
node -e "const {Simulator,Topology}=require('./engine'); /* build a two-host lab, ping, inspect replies[0].delay */"
grep -n "computeDhcp()" engine/simulator.js   # DHCP is in the data path (#1051)
grep -n "computeVrrp()" engine/simulator.js   # so is VRRP (#1052)
grep -rn "setTimeout\|setInterval\|Date.now" engine/*.js   # comments only: there are no timers
npm test                                 # the suites that pin the behaviour above
```

## Convergence is timed, interleaving is not

Control-plane processes used to finish at t=0, which made "power it on and watch it come up"
impossible to demonstrate. `Simulator.setRunTime(ms)` now asks a question at an instant: processes
that have not finished leave their device in the state the standard puts it in — STP ports
Listening or Learning (and dropping traffic), OSPF without learned routes, no DHCP lease — and a
failed ping reports `converging` with the process and its ready time instead of pretending a packet
was lost.

What is not modelled: real-time interleaving (a question is answered *as of* an instant, computed
from the timers — nothing runs a clock during a run), hello loss and dead-interval expiry, and
re-convergence after a topology change. See [CONVERGENCE.md](CONVERGENCE.md).
